If anyone needs me I'll be flirting over espresso and waiting for this movie to get here.

#302. Google brings the Gemini app to Apple CarPlay

The chatbot was already available through the Google Maps app.

Continuing on its apparent quest to ensure the chatbot is inescapable on as many screens, speakers and experiences as possible, Google has brought the Gemini app to Apple CarPlay. It's available starting today for all supported Gemini Live languages.

Google previously made the AI assistant available on CarPlay through the Google Maps app. Now, you'll be able to access it just by tapping the Gemini icon on your display. Google said you can pick up where you left off with Gemini in your car or start a new conversation with the chatbot.

The company suggested drivers could use Gemini to prep for a meeting during their commute, catch up on emails, hear their Daily Brief or get recommendations for local places worth checking out in an area. Your conversations will sync with the Gemini iPhone app and remain in your chat history.

The Gemini app is now available directly on Apple CarPlay. iOS users can have free-flowing conversations with Gemini Live right from their car's display, hands-free.

Use Gemini in CarPlay to catch-up on what you have coming up in your schedule, talk through your inbox, or... pic.twitter.com/1s4luZ6q0v

To use the assistant on CarPlay, you'll need to make sure you have the latest version of the Gemini app on your iPhone and the microphone permissions are on. You'll need to have used Gemini Live at least once on your iPhone as well.

Google brought the Gemini app to CarPlay not long after it enabled Personal Assistance (which can access details from other Google apps for more personalized responses) in the chatbot on Android Auto. The company is bringing Personal Intelligence to Gemini on cars with Google built-in as well.

#301. NASA's secret weapon in the lunar space race may be ... procurement reform?

“The pace that’s occurring, and the nature of the procurements, is unprecedented.”

Senior NASA officials held a lengthy news conference last weekend, laying out a series of lunar contract awards, new contract opportunities, and mission decisions. They followed one after the other: landers, nuclear-powered rovers, scouting drones, power generation, storage, and communication satellites.

“Hopefully, we’re keeping industry on their toes trying to catch up to all the solicitations we’re doing,” said Carlos García-Galán, manager of the Moon Base Program, more than 90 minutes into the news conference. “This is what it takes to do the Moon Base in the timeline we have set forth.”

The rapid-fire announcement of awards tells us a few things about NASA’s efforts to return to the Moon and establish a durable settlement there during the next decade.

First, the agency is serious about this effort—the Artemis Moon Base program represents the most credible effort to establish a permanent outpost on another world in NASA’s history. Second, NASA is reaching out to all corners of the US commercial space industry, casting an extraordinarily wide net to reach companies that have yet to fly any hardware into orbit. Third, NASA is getting creative about how it’s buying these services, and this may matter most of all.

There was a lot packed into the announcements this weekend, so to make sense of it all, I called Mike Gold, a lawyer and former NASA associate administrator who set up the Artemis Accords, who is now the president of Redwire Space. He was at NASA at the beginning of Artemis and understands what it takes to get the government moving. He said he was blown away. During a period of seven days, Gold said, NASA put out five proposals for lunar contracts.

“The pace that’s occurring, and the nature of the procurements is unprecedented,” Gold said. “You are seeing an all-hands-on-deck approach. Big companies, small companies, new companies, old companies.”

What has impressed Gold most about NASA’s strategy since announcing the Moon Base in March, at the Ignition event, has been the agency’s evolving procurement strategy. The space agency has adopted a flexible procurement strategy similar to one used by the US Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency.

There are two components. NASA has largely used a mechanism called Broad Agency Announcements, which ask for a capability such as providing power on the surface of the Moon. This allows NASA to ask industry for ideas without being too prescriptive and levying a bunch of requirements. It’s rather like instead of telling industry precisely what it wants, NASA asks industry what it can do.

Critically, Gold said, the space agency has also used a DARPA-style preliminary proposal process. In response to a traditional procurement, interested companies would prepare a detailed proposal, often running dozens, if not hundreds, of pages. This required an extraordinary amount of time.

With the Moon Base, NASA is employing a two-step process. After NASA announces the need for a service, interested companies submit a short white paper describing their solution. After a short review, NASA identifies the concepts it wants to pursue further, and only then do companies invest the time and resources to prepare a detailed proposal.

Gold said several lunar opportunities interested Redwire, and the company submitted initial proposals in response. But it only had to prepare a full proposal for opportunities that NASA wanted to pursue, including using a variant of its roll-out solar arrays on the Moon to generate power.

“We were able to focus the lion’s share of our resources in what the agency really wanted and what we were best at,” he said.

From this shift, it seems clear that NASA is trying to move away from micromanaging every individual lunar-base subsystem and instead seeks to coordinate independently developed commercial capabilities. The space agency is sourcing them from everywhere, from major contractors like Lockheed Martin, SpaceX, Blue Origin, and Northrop Grumman to relatively new startups such as Impulse Space, ispace-US, Voyager Technologies, Quantum Space, and more.

With questions emerging about how committed SpaceX is to NASA work in the future, the space agency appears to be seeking to diversify the way it gets cargo to the Moon. For example, it is leaning into Relativity Space and its large (but unproven) Terran R rocket, as well as orbital transfer vehicles built by Impulse Space. NASA is prioritizing the creation of a whole ecosystem of potential contractors rather than relying on one or two primes.

In doing so, NASA is taking a big risk. It is attempting to accelerate its lunar program by buying services from companies whose hardware is not yet operational and, in some cases, exists only as advanced engineering designs. For NASA, ultimately, it is a high-risk, high-reward approach. But in a demanding contest with China to reach and establish settlements on the Moon, NASA’s post-Apollo timidity is no longer tenable.

So how will NASA pay for all of the new stuff? Some of the funding is being repurposed from the previously shuttered Lunar Gateway program, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman said. For example, as part of the “One Big Beautiful Bill” act passed in 2025, NASA received $2.6 billion for the Gateway. Some of the funding was already appropriated through the agency’s Commercial Lunar Payload Services Program.

Moreover, Isaacman said, some of these contracts are relatively small and will enable NASA to invest more down the road in successful services.

“By doing a lot of littles, and learning from our approach and also putting some good wins on the board, we’re demonstrating that we can be very efficient and responsible with taxpayer dollars,” he said. With this approach, he added, “You give future administrations the option of how they can dial up or down missions. Some of these missions in the, call it, $200 million range between launch, lander, and payload; potentially, you can add those or drop those inside a $25 billion approximate budget per year without putting the entire program at risk.”

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#300. Tesla to keep producing Cybertrucks until the end of next summer

The Cybertruck saga continues. Days after Tesla CEO Elon Musk said the company would wind down production of its electric pickup, he's backtracking that decision, but not by much.

Last week, Musk said Tesla will stop producing Cybertrucks at Gigafactory Texas "around the middle of next year." Given that it's the only factory where Cybertrucks are manufactured, that meant the end of the road for the Cybertruck, with the company using the production capacity for its autonomous taxi car, the Cybercab.

Musk quickly followed up by saying that the demand for Cybertruck is strong and that the order rate "far exceeds" the company's production rate. "We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months," he wrote on X.

Counting from the day he said it (early November 2026), that would mean ending production in early June 2027.

Now, however, Musk says Tesla will extend that deadline by a couple of months. "In order to work through more of the Cybertruck order backlog, production will be extended through the end of summer 2027," he wrote on X.

"This is not the smartest move from a profit-maximizing standpoint btw, but there must be some room in this world for sentimentality or what’s the point," he added.

It's odd to call the move "working through the backlog," as Cybertruck can still be ordered on Tesla's U.S. website, though only in select configurations. Moreover, the company actually raised prices of the Cybertruck on the day Musk announced it would be winding down production.

Still, this should mean that more people who have already ordered the Cybertruck will actually get their car. That's a good thing, right?

#298. Syrian refugee elected mayor of German town, years after fleeing war

A Syrian who arrived in Germany as a refugee in 2015 has won a mayoral election in the southwestern German state of Baden-Württemberg.

Ryyan Alshebl, who left his hometown of As Suwayda in Syria eight years ago, ran as an independent in the municipality of Ostelsheim. He won 55.41% of the votes on Sunday, beating two German candidates, Marco Strauss and Mathias Fey.

Locals cheered the 29-year-old when he welcomed his win, a victory he described as “sensational,” German local broadcaster SWR reported Monday.

“Today, Ostelsheim sent an example for broad-mindedness and cosmopolitanism for the whole of Germany,” he said, according to to German public broadcaster ZDF. “That’s not something that can be taken for granted in a conservative, rural area.”

Alshebl’s first call after his victory was to his mother in Syria, who was thrilled with the news, SWR reported.

The Association of Municipalities of Baden-Württemberg said Alshebl is the first man with Syrian roots to run for and win a mayor’s office. He will start his role in June.

'You are not a refugee.' Roma refugees fleeing war in Ukraine say they are suffering discrimination and prejudice

Ostelsheim residents have welcomed their incoming mayor. “The fairy tale has come true, and the right man has become our mayor,” Annette Keck, who lives in the village, told SWR.

Strauss, one of his opponents, congratulated Alshebl. “I wish you good luck and at the same time ask for support for Mr. Alshebl, for our shared Ostelsheim,” he said on Facebook.

The state’s Integration Minister Manne Lucha said that Alshebl’s victory showed that diversity is a natural part of Baden-Württemberg. “I would be very pleased if Ryyan Alshebl’s election encourages more people with a migration history to run for political office,” he said.

Not everyone has been so warm to the 29-year-old. ZDF reported the Syrian received hateful comments on the campaign trail.

The young politician went from house to house, promoting his election program, and “the experiences were predominantly positive,” but there was also a minority of far-right fringe voters in Ostelsheim that did not want to accept him due to his Syrian roots, Alshebl told ZDF.

Born to a schoolteacher and agricultural engineer in Syria, Alshebl described his life as carefree until the age of 20, according to his campaign website.

At the time, protests against the Syrian government that began in 2011 soon devolved into chaotic war. The fighting and later rise of ISIS forced 10.6 million people from home by late 2015 – about half of Syria’s pre-war population.

Alshebl faced the dilemma of being drafted for military service with the Syrian army or leaving the country, according to his website.

While many Syrians were displaced internally or fled to countries in the region, others like Alshebl made the dangerous journey to Europe. He was 21 years old at the time, and said he crossed from Turkey to the Greek island of Lesbos in a rubber dinghy.

Former German Chancellor Angela Merkel had implemented a brief open-door policy in 2015 that saw the country take in about 1.2 million asylum seekers in the following years, including Alshebl.

They were welcomed into British homes. Celebrating their first Christmas together, Ukrainians wonder if that hospitality will last

The move sparked a backlash in Germany and the sudden growth of the far-right, anti-immigration Alternative for Germany (AfD) in the wake of summer 2015.

Once in Germany, Alshebl lived close to Ostelsheim and said at the time he felt “there is only one thing you can do: get back on your feet quickly and start investing in your own future quickly.”

For the last seven years he worked in the administration of Althengstett town hall, in a neighboring town. He drew from his experience, he said in his campaign, and made digital access to to public administration services one of priorities. Flexible childcare and climate protections are also on his agenda.

Alshebl, who is a member of the Green Party and now has German citizenship, pledged during his campaign that once elected as mayor he would move to Ostelsheim.

#297. United is restricting AI agents from booking flights

The airline believes AI poses security risks and may violate DOT and FAA rules.

Giving something like your payment details or log-in credentials to an agent can expose sensitive information and lead to "unintended purchases, account changes or use of MileagePlus miles," United says. Allowing an AI agent to complete a booking could also violate existing airline regulations from the Department of Transportation and Federal Aviation Administration. For example, United says only a human can acknowledge that their bag doesn't contain explosives during flight check-in.

United would like to avoid association with any agent-based embarrassments, for instance when Meta's Muse leaked a user's address online. Like other companies, it also wants to keep AI from getting in the way of its relationship with customers. The Wall Street Journal reports that United CEO Scott Kirby has been open about the company's reluctance to jump into agentic commerce. "To be honest, I don't want to create another middleman," Kirby said at an event on Tuesday. Not every airline feels the same way: Southwest created a plugin so users can book flights with ChatGPT.

Luckily for United and other companies blocking AI agents, agentic commerce might not be taking off with regular people. During its recent third-quarter earnings call, Uber shared that it handled 320 million trips and deliveries during the week of Halloween; of those, AI agents were responsible for 500 Uber Eats orders and only four rides. Those aren't exactly industry-destroying numbers.

Handing an AI your credit card is very different from handing it your email login, and both are different from just asking it to send you a news summary every morning. Even if a large number of people are downloading apps like Muse, it seems like letting an agent shop for you isn't as frictionless or foolproof as it needs to be to catch on.

#296. Hands-on: The Apple HomeView is gorgeous, costs $299, and has no App Store

The Apple HomeView finally arrives on doorsteps (and in Apple Stores) on Friday, and I think this gorgeous little smart display is about to become a common sight in homes around the world. I've been testing the HomeView ahead of its release, and I would definitely recommend it to homeowners, parents, and anyone looking for Christmas gift ideas.

From the moment I saw the HomeView up close, I've had a premonition: Apple is going to sell a million of these things. And after adding it to my own home, I'm even more confident in that prediction.

As a product reviewer, you get a sixth sense for this. The last time I felt this way about a product? Back in March, when Apple debuted the MacBook Neo, which proved to be extremely popular.

For someone like me, who lives in a one-bedroom New York City apartment and tries not to think about whether or not I'll ever be able to afford a down payment, the Apple HomeView is an awesomely unnecessary sidekick. Personally, I'd buy an iPad stand before a HomeView, but YMMV. However, for my parents, my big sister, and my better-off friends who do own their own homes, I would definitely recommend this device.

For upper-income homeowners, the HomeView is a beautiful digital butler for the modern smart abode. (Side note: Apple products have always been high-end, but after the latest price increases, it feels more and more like a luxury brand to me.) If you plan on investing in Apple's other new smart home gadgets — the new Apple TV 4K, HomePod Mini 2, new home security cameras, and video doorbells — I'd even say the HomeView is a necessity.

Thanks to the Apple Home app, you can use the HomeView to adjust smart thermostats, view live security camera footage, dim the lights, and turn connected devices on or off. An intercom function lets you use connected speakers to send messages around the house. For this type of user, the wall-mounted model is ideal, though the mount is sold separately and brings the total to $378.99. For those of you who can afford all those smart home gadgets, why not get a HomeView for your entryway and your kitchen? I suspect many wealthy homeowners will do exactly that.

The HomeView doesn't just act as a smart home hub. You can also listen to Apple Music, watch Apple TV, FaceTime with family, browse for recipes in Safari, and check your Apple calendar. The App Store is conspicuously missing, so Safari is your only option for services like YouTube, Gmail, TikTok, and Disney+. (The HomeView runs on its own custom OS that Apple calls HomeView software.) That lack of apps is the biggest problem with the HomeView so far.

But if you're already a happy little pea in the iOS pod, you can get a lot done with Apple's own apps and widgets, and I think the HomeView justifies its $299 price for the right user.

After Apple announced the HomeView, I said on Mashable's Instagram that this was a 10/10 product design. I might revise that down to 9.5/10, as it does have larger bezels than most modern displays. But I'm still enamored with the design.

The HomeView actually comes in two models: one with a domed speaker base and an adjustable hinge for the display, and a wall-mounted version. Whether you set it on your kitchen counter or mount it in your entryway, it looks beautiful. For this review in progress, I'm going to focus on the speaker version I tested, which I also think will have a wider appeal.

The HomeView has a premium design, with a recycled aluminum body and a stainless steel adjustable hinge for the display. It has perforations on the bottom of the display and speaker base for audio, and a camera and sensor in the upper bezel. The 7-inch Liquid Retina display looks clear and bright whether you're catching up on the latest Silo cliffhanger on Apple TV or admiring the lava lamp-inspired home screen. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The HomeView defaults to a swirling abstract clock face that responds to light and movement. The shapes on the display will darken in low light and track your movements. Like the opening/closing animation on the iPhone Duo, it's the kind of subtle design flex that reminds you Apple does consumer tech design better than anyone. You can also cycle through a variety of widgets and clock faces at your leisure.

Apple's new smart display may lack third-party apps, but Apple taught it some pretty cool tricks. The HomeView recognizes up to six people in your household by face and voice (if they have a connected iPad or iPhone running iOS 27.2). So, when you step up to the HomeView, it instantly pulls up your messages, your calendar, and your preferences. But if your husband or wife walks up to it, they'll see their own information, or personal context, as Apple calls it.

The HomeView also has a custom recipe experience, which recognizes a recipe on Safari and breaks it down into simple lists of ingredients and steps. (I'm not sure how recipe writers will feel about this feature, but it's undeniably useful.) And if you don't have a smart thermostat, the HomeView has its own temperature and humidity sensors.

The HomeView also makes good use of the new Siri AI beta. When you say, "Hey, Siri...," the new Siri logo drops from the top bezel with a nifty teardrop animation. But if you've tried the new Siri AI, then you know it's still not done cooking. Siri sometimes makes stupid mistakes, though in general, the combination of broad world knowledge and personal context results in useful answers to my questions.

Siri AI has been improving with each OS update, and hopefully that continues to be true.

Like most new Apple products, the HomeView has a 12MP Center Stage camera for video calls. It works well enough for FaceTime calls, which is good, because that's its main job.

I did find the speakers a little disappointing. Because the HomeView is more speaker-shaped than a MacBook, I expected more from it. The new MacBook Pro PROLED has much better speakers, for instance. I found the bass disappointing, not in terms of volume, but in depth and mix.

The speaker system isn't bad exactly. It's just not quite as good as I expected. But for jamming out to music while you work or cook, it's just like the Center Stage camera: good enough.

#294. New York is forcing T-Mobile to stop putting data caps on low-income home internet plans

The company was throttling speeds for plans offered under the Affordable Broadband Act.

New York's attorney general found that T-Mobile was not following the state's Affordable Broadband Act, which sets requirements for offering service to low-income residents. Under the ABA, eligible households must be able to purchase broadband at speeds of at least 25 Mbps for $15 a month or 200 Mbps for $20 a month without additional taxes or equipment fees. Providers are also required to make "reasonable efforts" to promote these low-cost options.

The Office of the Attorney General discovered that T-Mobile had placed monthly data caps on its ABA plans. According to the press release, T-Mobile would throttle speeds to as low as 650 kilobits per second after a customer had used 50GB of data, which could happen in about four days of typical broadband use. The company was also only offering its ABA plans in areas where it had 5G wireless deployed and was preventing information about those plans on its website from being seen by search engines.

Following the investigation, T-Mobile will remove those data caps, make its ABA plans available in all places in the state where it offers home internet service, and will ensure that search engines can access details about ABA plans.

"Reliable and affordable internet service is a basic need for all New Yorkers, but T-Mobile flouted the law designed to help vulnerable New Yorkers access it," Attorney General Letitia James said. "My office is committed to seeing the Affordable Broadband Act realize its goal of ensuring that New Yorkers in need are not denied access to affordable home internet. I encourage all eligible New Yorkers to enroll in a low-cost home internet plan through the ABA."

In a statement shared with Engadget, T-Mobile the company said "We have a long history of helping customers in New York stay connected through low-cost broadband, and we're updating our plan to meet the state's requirements while maintaining the same low price. We've reached an agreement with the New York Attorney General that builds on that commitment, provides greater clarity around our offering and makes the plan available to more eligible customers."

Update, November 11, 6:20PM ET: Added a statement from T-mobile.

#293. Anduril says its first satellite can see adversaries maneuver in orbital night

“What’s unique is it’s got a huge dynamic range. We can see things in full dark.”

Anduril Industries has announced the successful demonstration of a new satellite-based sensor designed to track other spacecraft that may be hiding their maneuvers in Earth’s shadow.

Funded with Anduril’s internal capital, the company’s oven-sized satellite launched on a SpaceX rideshare mission in July with a package of tech demo payloads: an edge-compute processor, a power-management system adaptable to different mission types, and a long-wave infrared space sensor capable of detecting other objects in orbit.

Anduril reported on the success of the tech demo mission Tuesday. The company’s satellite, named Anduril-216, flies about 590 km (366 mi) above Earth, spending about one-third of each orbit in darkness. Anduril-216’s night passes allow its infrared imager to shine.

“This is a unique sensor that allows us to take imagery in long-wave [infrared],” said Gokul Subramanian, Anduril’s senior vice president of engineering. “What’s unique about that is it’s got a huge dynamic range. It has very low solar exclusion, so we’re blocked by the Sun very rarely, and we can see things in full dark.”

The US military and a growing number of commercial data providers use a network of radars and optical sensors on the ground and in space to monitor satellite traffic. Anduril acquired one of the commercial satellite-tracking companies, ExoAnalytic Solutions, earlier this year. But there’s a gap in optical tracking as satellites fly in Earth’s shadow, so space traffic monitors could temporarily lose track of a spacecraft if it performs a maneuver in orbital night.

The Space Force is charged with tracking satellites for two primary reasons. First, military commanders want to know if an adversary moves its satellite in a threatening manner. Second, the Space Force issues traffic alerts to global satellite operators if there’s a risk of a collision.

“Right now, we don’t have ground sensors in our adversaries’ sovereign lands, and then in the eclipse period where we are relying heavily on visible sensors, the satellites that are not illuminated by the Sun, we can’t see those,” said Adam Thurn, vice president and general manager of orbital systems at Anduril. “With the long-wave infrared space sensor, we’re looking to close that gap.”

Anduril’s long-wave infrared sensor works by detecting heat signatures from spacecraft, even in periods of eclipse. It provides a “unique capability for us to witness when an adversary or other resident space objects are maneuvering during those eclipse periods, where in the past we’ve been fairly blind,” Thurn said.

In creating the infrared space sensor, engineers applied imaging technology Anduril uses in its autonomous weapons for use in space. Anduril released a single image Tuesday from the sensor on Anduril-216 showing an infrared view of the Moon above Earth’s horizon. The Moon image offers just a taste of what the instrument can do, Thurn said.

“The long-wave infrared capability we have on orbit can do both unresolved and resolved imagery,” Thurn said, suggesting Anduril’s sensor could be used to inspect other satellites in orbit if it flies close enough. For now, Thurn said Anduril is focusing on optimizing its tracking sensor for low-Earth orbit, but it could be used in other orbits, and eventually in cislunar space near the Moon.

Apex Space, a satellite-manufacturing startup, built the spacecraft platform for Anduril-216. Subramanian said Anduril has no plans to start its own satellite production line. Instead, Anduril foresees buying third-party satellite platforms and integrating them with sensor and compute modules to perform a range of different missions in space.

“You’re seeing that today with Anduril-216, where we started with a simple mission, which was how do we track satellites, especially in these eclipse periods where the US currently does not have the ability to maintain custody,” Subramanian said. “And we designed a mission around that.”

The compute module on Anduril-216 is designed to fuse data from different kinds of sensors, such as infrared or visible imagers, and generate tracking solutions on its own, without intervention or analysis from the ground.

Subramanian said Anduril’s current focus is on acquiring space domain awareness data for sale to the US military, not selling entire satellites or sensors to the government. “At present, Anduril is not offering this system as a merchant supplier,” he said. “That could change in the future.”

#292. These are the animals people most want to see photographed

Editor’s Note:  Call to Earth is a CNN editorial series committed to reporting on the environmental challenges facing our planet, together with the solutions. Rolex’s Perpetual Planet initiative has partnered with CNN to drive awareness and education around key sustainability issues and to inspire positive action.

Originally a term used by trophy hunters in Africa, the “Big Five” described the animals most challenging to shoot and kill: the lion, elephant, leopard, rhino, and buffalo. Nowadays, it is more loosely used to refer to some of Africa’s largest and most iconic animals.

However, British photographer Graeme Green has reclaimed the narrative, creating a global “New Big Five” for wildlife photography. In 2021, 50,000 people around the world voted for the five animals they would most like to photograph, or see photographed, in the wild. Five animals were crowned the winners: the elephant, polar bear, lion, gorilla and tiger.

This week, sees the publication of “The New Big 5” photography book, which features images of those animals and other at-risk wildlife, from photography legends such as Ami Vitale, Steve McCurry and Paul Nicklen, and essays from famous conservationists and activists including like Jane Goodall and Paula Kahumbu.

Green says that the book celebrates wildlife and is a global call to action on issues impacting wildlife, including habitat loss, poaching, pollution and climate change.

Green was on assignment in Botswana at least a decade ago when he came up with the idea for a project to encourage people to “shoot with a camera, not a gun,” he says.

“I thought this would be a way to get people really focusing on wildlife, thinking about the wildlife they love, thinking about the animals that are at risk.”

Ten photographs that made the world wake up to climate change

In total, the book includes the work of 144 globally renowned wildlife photographers from Ecuador to India. Curating the images took nearly two years of work, says Green.

“I think these are some of the most beautiful and creative images that I’ve seen put together in one book,” says Green. “These are the species that we are at risk of losing.”

According to the UN, nature is declining at an unprecedented rate, with around one million of the planet’s animal and plant species facing extinction. The “New Big 5,” all of which are threatened, act as ambassadors for what is happening in the natural world, says Green.

As well as being a powerful reminder of what we stand to lose, the book also points people towards potential solutions. Featured essays explore the benefits of rewilding and the importance of indigenous communities in conservation.

A chapter on endangered species from bees to blue whales illustrates the alarming threat climate change poses to animals outside of the “New Big 5.” “That’s only the tip of the iceberg – I could have included thousands of pictures because that’s how serious the situation is,” says Green.

Why a wildlife photographer took 40,000 photos to capture these extraordinary birds

Jane Goodall, a leading conservationist who also wrote the afterword to the book, said in a press release that “we have a window of time during which we can start to heal some of the harm we have inflicted on the natural world, but only if we get together and take action now.

“I hope the photos will lead people into the wonderful worlds of these iconic species. Then, perhaps, other people will become involved in helping to create a world where wildlife can flourish for future generations to enjoy,” she said.

“The New Big 5: A Global Photography Project For Endangered Wildlife,” by Graeme Green, published by Earth Aware Editions, is on sale from April 4, 2023.

This story has been updated to reflect the range of issues covered by the book.

#291. Nintendo is ending Google and Apple sign-ins early next year

The company is asking users to set up a password or a passkey for their Nintendo accounts.

Nintendo will discontinue Google and Apple log-ins early next year, so you may want to update the way you sign into your account soon. Starting on January 26, 2027, it will no longer be possible to sign into your Nintendo account, or to create a new one, with Google and Apple as a log-in method. In addition, it is also ending the My Nintendo Platinum Points mission that rewards you for linking your Google with your Nintendo account.

The company had made the announcement in a support page, where it also said that the exact end time of Google and Apple sign-ins depend "on the progress of the system change work." Nintendo is asking users to set up a password or a passkey before January 26. To set up a passkey, go to the Accounts page, sign into your account, choose Sign-In and Security Settings, select Passkeys and then Edit. From there, you can choose Register New Passkey to start signing into Nintendo with your device's authentication method.

Nintendo didn't say why it's removing Google and Apple sign-ins, and whether it has security concerns with them. Whatever Nintendo's reason for it, you may want to get your log-in sorted if you want uninterrupted access to your games and to the eShop.

#289. Apple TV 4K (4th gen) review: Still great, but drastically more expensive

The $150 price increase is hard to swallow.

The Apple TV 4K has been our pick for the best premium streaming device since the third-gen model arrived in 2022. In terms of pure performance, it was undoubtedly the best you could buy, thanks to the combination of picture quality, speed and features you wouldn't find on any of the more affordable competition. That was true four years ago and it's still true now.

Unfortunately, the global tech landscape has changed massively over the last two years. Nearly every device is more expensive, especially if it needs RAM or its own processor. Apple's streaming box didn't escape that fate. For the 4th-generation Apple TV 4K, Apple made big upgrades to performance and added a slew of new features, and of course, it carved out a more prominent place for Siri AI in your living room. The compact streaming box is a lot more expensive than the previous generation cost at launch, though, and the new $299 price tag certainly dulls its shine.

If you were hoping for a new design, there are only subtle tweaks to report. The most evident is that Apple moved the light on the front of the box from the right to the center. If you dive into the spec sheets, you'll discover the new model is slightly larger than the previous version. I'm talking 0.24mm bigger in width and depth, and 3 grams heavier. You'll be forgiven for not noticing. The Siri Remote, however, is exactly the same size at 1.4 inches x 5.4 inches x 0.36 inches.

Apple upgraded the processor from the A15 Bionic to the A19 chip. In iPhone terms, that would be like going from an iPhone 13 to an iPhone 17, so the computing power increase here is significant. The company says that the A19 chip is capable of 50 percent faster CPU performance and up to 2x faster GPU performance than the previous Apple TV 4K. I don't have the tools to quantifiably check those claims, but I can tell you the device is super responsive, navigation is speedy and overall the UI is noticeably more fluid. Plus, the A19 delivers a dedicated AV1 decoder for HDR rendering efficiency.

Of course, the A19 offers a boost to Siri AI, which is playing a bigger role in the living room thanks to the new Apple TV 4K. For starters, the streaming box now has three far-field microphones built in, so you no longer have to use the remote to interact with the assistant. You can simply speak your queries while watching TV and Siri is off and running. Like before, Apple's AI can help with finding and playing live TV, movies, shows and sports from your go-to apps. But with Siri's recently expanded capabilities, it can now pull in personal context from Messages and other apps too. So when you want Siri to "play the show Jack texted me about," the assistant knows just what to do. Additionally, audio captured from the far-field mics is processed within the Secure Exclave on the A19, so neither the apps, the OS nor Apple get access to any of the data.

The new feature that will get the most use at my house is Precision Finding for the remote in the Find My app. I have two kids who are constantly leaving the remote in undisclosed locations, so the added ability to play a sound has already greatly reduced the amount of time I spend searching for it. And when I can't hear it, Precision Finding is better than the previous method for locating the remote with an iPhone. With the built-in mics for Siri though, a lost remote is much less frustrating since you can just tell the Apple TV 4K what to do.

There's also a noteworthy audio upgrade on the Apple TV 4K. Before, you could only use two HomePod or HomePod mini speakers as a stereo pair. Now you can use up to four of those, so long as they're second-gen HomePods or HomePod mini 2s, for a more complete home theater surround sound system. The only additional requirement is that the two front speakers and two rear speakers have to match. So you can have two HomePods in the front and two HomePod mini 2s in the back, which is the configuration I tested. If you have four of the same speaker, that works too.

Once you have all of the HomePods in place, you'll hear a series of tones as the Apple TV 4K locates the speakers and tunes the audio for the room. I suspected that a combination of HomePods and HomePod minis is the ideal setup and my testing proved me right. The HomePods provide the added bass needed for action scenes, or really just for a fuller soundstage most TV viewing will eventually require. The HomePod minis can't do that on their own, but they do well as rear speakers, providing dimensional backup to the larger speakers at the front. I think having four HomePods would be overkill, not to mention expensive. I also heard a four-mini configuration during a demo last month, and while it's certainly a boost compared to TV speakers, it's not nearly as potent as the arrangement I tested at home.

I'll also note that there's a feature called Whole Room Audio that will be great for parties and gatherings. This allows for all four speakers to play the same sounds, rather than the fragments each might deliver in a surround sound setup. This ensures that everyone hears everything, from vocals to guitars, or even the commentators of an F1 race. Whole Room Audio is also easy to activate as it's accessible from the Speaker Mode option in the Control Center.

Like it has in the past, the new Apple TV 4K can function as a smart home hub. This allows you to control HomeKit and Matter accessories like cameras, locks and thermostats, and Apple says "thousands" of such devices are compatible. Thanks to the HomeKit Secure Video, you can expect secure local processing on the streaming box, which encrypts clips before they're stored in iCloud. Apple Intelligence provides captions for camera feeds and you can control everything from the Control Center.

Siri voice control is available as well, as is the ability to run scenes from the Home app in the Control Center. If you have a HomeKit or Matter compatible camera (I don't), you can get a live view of those in the top right corner of your TV screen. If someone rings the doorbell, you can also choose to be automatically notified on the display you're already looking at. If needed, you can chat with them and unlock the door without leaving your seat.

My biggest complaint about the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K is the price. At $299, this model is $150 more than the equivalent version of its predecessor was at launch. Sure, Apple increased the price of the third-gen Apple TV 4K from $149 to $249 earlier this year, and a lot has happened since that model debuted in 2022. However, even with rising memory and component costs, tacking on another $50 to the already inflated price tag is adding insult to injury. It especially stings when you consider the company actually decreased prices when it launched these models in 2022.

To pour more salt in the wound, Apple got rid of the cheaper Wi-Fi-only option that also offered less storage space (64GB). In 2022, that version of the third-gen Apple TV 4K launched for $129, though it eventually went up to $199 this year. If you'll allow me to dig even deeper into Apple TV 4K history, that $129 model was not only $50 cheaper than the W-Fi-only version before it, but even doubled the 32GB onboard storage. Its original price was a drop from the $179 that the comparative version preceding it cost. Meanwhile, the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K only comes in one configuration and it has 128GB of storage; that's the same capacity as the priciest model of the previous generation.

After living with the Apple TV 4K's new home theater setup with four HomePods, I'm starting to question how many people will actually use it. Not only does it require you to have four of Apple's speakers, but you'll also have to put all four of them in the same space. When you consider one of the primary functions of a HomePod is to serve as a direct line to Siri, permanently putting all four in your living room seems like a waste. Not to mention you'll miss out on multiroom audio and features like Intercom, which are more helpful if your HomePods are spread around the house. After some testing, it seems to me like the quad-Pod speaker arrangement is a neat bonus — something you temporarily pull together for movie night or parties — rather than a substitute for a soundbar or traditional home theater setup.

I have another gripe that is more of a frustration with tvOS as a whole, but it's worth mentioning as it relates to the new Apple TV 4K. Even if you've added the streaming box to the Home app and assigned it to a room, the Home icon won't appear in the Control Center until you've either added a camera or created a Scene. So if you fire up your Apple TV and can't find your non-camera devices, that's probably why.

I appreciate the fact that camera feeds will be the most useful on a TV. But I think it's silly that I have to create a Scene (manually triggered saved settings) in order to see things like a thermostat or lights on a device that's advertised as a "smart home hub." At the very least, I want there to be a shortcut in the Control Center for Rooms I've set up in the Home app on iOS, where I can then access individual devices if needed. I should also mention that tvOS doesn't have a dedicated Home app; everything to do with controlling your smart home requires digging into the Control Center on an Apple TV. Or, of course, you could fire up the full app on your phone.

In terms of performance, the Apple TV 4K remains the best streaming box you can buy. That's been true for a while now, and the upgrades the company made for this version only cements its position at the top of the premium heap. However, with the current state of global technology, specifically the price of anything that uses RAM or a chip like the A19, Apple's streaming device isn't as easy to recommend as it once was.

If you're rocking with a third-gen model, I'd urge you to stay put. And if you're looking to invest in an Apple TV for the first time, try to hold out for a sale or catch one on the refurbished market. Heck, I'd even keep my eye out for a used third-gen model. Yes, you'll miss out on the newest features in that scenario, including the convenience of an always-listening Siri device that isn't a HomePod, but the core of what makes an Apple TV a great device is still there. And in late 2026, there's something to be said for saving significant money on a tech purchase.

#288. T-Mobile illegally imposed 50GB cap on low-income home Internet plan, NY says

Law requires 25Mbps home Internet. T-Mobile lowers speed to 600Kbps after 50GB cap.

T-Mobile violated a New York state law by imposing a 50GB-per-month data cap and reducing speeds to less than a megabit per second on home Internet plans it is required to offer people with low incomes, New York government officials alleged.

T-Mobile agreed to a settlement requiring it to scrap the data and speed limits, to offer the required low-income plans to more parts of the state, and to make it easier for customers to find information about the plans. The settlement requires T-Mobile to eliminate the speed limits and expand availability of the plans within 120 days.

The alleged legal violation and the settlement are related to T-Mobile’s wireless home Internet service, not the company’s mobile plans. Governor Kathy Hochul and New York Attorney General Letitia James announced the settlement on Monday, saying that a state investigation found T-Mobile violated the New York Affordable Broadband Act (ABA).

The state law doesn’t specifically mention data caps, but it requires home Internet providers with at least 20,000 customers to offer plans at specific prices and minimum speeds to people who meet income-eligibility requirements. T-Mobile’s policy of reducing speeds after customers exceed a monthly data cap violates the law, New York officials said.

“An investigation by the Office of the Attorney General (OAG) found T-Mobile’s offerings did not meet the ABA’s requirements, which mandate that home Internet providers offer high-speed broadband to income-eligible households for $15 per month at speeds of at least 25 Mbps, or $20 per month for speeds of at least 200 Mbps,” the press release said.

New York says in the settlement with T-Mobile that “after the data cap is reached, the customer’s maximum download speed is reduced from 25 Mbps to 600 Kbps.” New York’s press release said “a typical customer could hit the data cap after about four days of usage.”

Data caps and speed limits aren’t the only restrictions. T-Mobile also “only offers ABA Internet plans to customers in homes that are eligible for 5G home Internet,” preventing residents in 4G-only areas from accessing the plans, the settlement says. The company also prevented search engines from indexing the webpage that provides detailed information about eligibility for and enrollment in ABA plans, it says.

“T-Mobile did not fully comply with the New York Affordable Broadband Act,” New York said in the settlement.

T-Mobile’s ABA webpage confirms that customers are provided only 50GB per month and that speeds after using the allotment are merely “up to 600 Kbps.”

Although the settlement includes a list of the New York AG’s findings, T-Mobile did not admit to any violation. “Respondent neither admits nor denies the OAG’s Finding in paragraph 1-14 above,” the document says.

New York started enforcing the Affordable Broadband Act in January 2025 after winning a nearly four-year court battle against broadband industry lobby groups that tried to kill the law. The groups that sued New York in a failed effort to block the law included CTIA, the wireless industry association that represents T-Mobile and other carriers. The law finally took effect a month after the Supreme Court rejected the broadband industry’s challenge.

When contacted by Ars, T-Mobile did not explain why it imposed the restrictions on low-income plans.

“We have a long history of helping customers in New York stay connected through low-cost broadband, and we’re updating our plan to meet the state’s requirements while maintaining the same low price,” T-Mobile said in a statement. “We’ve reached an agreement with the New York attorney general that builds on that commitment, provides greater clarity around our offering and makes the plan available to more eligible customers.”

The settlement with New York applies to T-Mobile’s fixed wireless Internet plans, which can be used for home Internet service as an alternative to wired broadband. The fixed wireless Internet relies on T-Mobile’s cellular network but provides access at a single location, delivering wireless signals to receivers installed at customer homes.

It took almost 18 months for New York to investigate T-Mobile’s compliance and reach a settlement with the company. “In June 2025, the New York Department of Public Service contacted the OAG and relayed concerns about certain features of the plan that T-Mobile was offering to customers eligible for reduced-price Internet service under the ABA,” the settlement said.

It seems that ISPs have generally offered the required plans, though cable company Optimum failed to comply until Ars made some inquiries on behalf of a customer who could not obtain the plan he was entitled to shortly after the law took effect. ISPs knew about the law long in advance due to the multiyear court case that they initiated, but Optimum wasn’t ready to comply with the requirement and rejected the low-income man’s request twice.

New York officials said the AG’s office “found that T-Mobile was only offering ABA plans in areas where it had deployed 5G wireless service, even though it offered full-price Internet plans that could deliver 25 Mbps download speeds in areas where it had 4G service. In addition, T-Mobile specifically coded its website to hide its ABA plan information from search engines, making it difficult for New Yorkers searching for low-priced Internet to find their plans.”

The settlement requires T-Mobile to stop intentionally limiting download speeds, and says the “temporary reduction of download speeds due solely to network congestion shall not constitute a violation of this Assurance.” There’s also an exception in the state law, which says ISPs shall provide 25Mbps “subject to exceptions adopted by the Public Service Commission where such download speed is not reasonably practicable.”

Extending the low-cost service to 4G areas, T-Mobile agreed to provide the low-income plans “at every address at which Respondent offers residential fixed wireless Internet service.” Speeds lower than 25 Mbps would not violate the settlement if T-Mobile “would be unable to provide a minimum download speed of 25 megabits per second to a residential fixed wireless Internet service customer enrolled in a non-ABA Plan at the same address.”

The document says that “T-Mobile offers 5G home Internet to roughly 40 percent of homes in rural areas of upstate New York and 4G (LTE) home Internet to the remainder of homes in the regions where T-Mobile offers fixed wireless service.”

To make its ABA webpage more accessible, the settlement says T-Mobile “shall not include a noindex tag on the webpage, or otherwise prevent search engines from indexing the webpage or presenting a link to the webpage in response to search engine queries.” T-Mobile must also prominently link to the ABA webpage on the part of its website that lists or describes its standard home Internet deals.

New York could sue T-Mobile if it violates the settlement. The ABA’s enforcement mechanism requires the attorney general to sue a broadband provider on behalf of residents. Such lawsuits can recover restitution and civil penalties of up to $1,000 per violation.

ISPs that want to raise another challenge to New York’s law could get help from the Trump administration, which asserts that states must not enforce price regulations or net neutrality laws against any ISP that obtains grants from the $42 billion Broadband Equity, Access, and Deployment (BEAD) program. The grants have not been distributed yet, and California argues that the Trump administration does not have authority to preempt such laws.

In addition to this week’s settlement, New York struck a deal with T-Mobile last month over the carrier violating its promise to never raise prices on certain mobile plans that came with a lifetime price guarantee.

A state investigation “found that from 2017 to 2019, T-Mobile advertised ‘Price Guarantee Plans’ to its 4G customers, promising to ‘never change the price you pay,’” the New York attorney general’s office said on October 20. “However, in 2024, T-Mobile changed course and began raising the price of these plans by approximately $5 per phone line. New Yorkers enrolled in approximately 252,957 of these Price Guarantee Plans saw their phone bills increase.”

T-Mobile agreed to give affected customers several reimbursement options and a price lock until May 2029. The attorney general’s office estimated that the agreement would provide up to $38 million in value to customers affected by the price hikes. T-Mobile also agreed to pay the state a fine of $98,834.75.

T-Mobile provided a statement on the price-hike settlement.

“The $38 million cited in the press release is an estimate of potential benefits that a group of long-standing T-Mobile customers may choose to take advantage of, not the amount in refunds or payments,” T-Mobile said. “The agreement provides for approximately $98,000 paid to the state and a range of optional customer benefits. We’re glad to have this matter resolved and remain focused on delivering value to our customers.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#287. OpenAI acquires Odessia, travel startup created by Sonder founder

OpenAI has acquired an AI travel agent called Odessia, according to Odessia’s founder, Francis Davidson. Davidson says in a post announcing the deal that he’s now working for OpenAI, helping the AI lab with the future of travel.

Terms of the deal were not disclosed, but this looks like an acqui-hire. Davidson’s announcement focused largely on how he built his AI travel agent Odessia by directing teams of AI agents. The year-old Odessia raised a $6 million pre-seed round led by Sequoia and launched its AI travel agent around five months ago. Davidson says that it had helped early adopters plan millions of dollars’ worth of travel, but the tool has already been shut down.

An OpenAI spokesperson confirmed the hire to TechCrunch. “We’re thrilled to welcome Francis to OpenAI. Francis is a proven founder who has built multiple businesses to help people travel, and we’re excited he’s chosen to build with us.”

However the AI lab has not announced new travel-specific products or plans. It does, however, count such companies as Bookings.com, Expedia, and KAYAK as customers or partners. Travel is also one of the main areas that people are using personal AI assistants to help with. And, back in July 2025, when OpenAI launched its ChatGPT agent, it mentioned travel as one of its potential uses.

OpenAI’s spokesperson didn’t have more to share on its plans for travel-related products, except to say that travel is one of the areas that chatbot users frequently ask about. “1.2 billion people turn to ChatGPT each week for help with everyday tasks — from understanding their health, figuring out what to make for dinner, building a budget, and even planning a trip. We see a real opportunity to make these experiences even more useful,” the spokesperson said.

Acquiring Odessia is interesting for another reason. Davidson is well known in the travel industry after he founded a travel startup called Sonder as a college student in 2014. It was sort of like Airbnb, but it focused on short-term leasing of upscale apartments in major cities worldwide. The pandemic badly hurt it, and as it climbed out of that rut, it went public via a SPAC but didn’t raise as much money as it needed, Davidson later said in a post announcing his resignation as CEO in June 2025.

When a key partnership with Marriott fell through, the company went into bankruptcy and wound down. The bankruptcy trustee sold Sonder’s name, trademarks, and website domain in 2026 to TravelAI, and that website operates as a curated guide to the kind of travel rental properties that Sonder was once known for. Prior to its downfall, Davidson had built Sonder into a unicorn with over $600 million in revenues. While Sonder was not, ultimately, a success story, Davidson is the kind of battle-scarred founder that Silicon Valley often values.

So we’ll have to wait and see what OpenAI has in store with travel now that he’s joined the company.

Note: This story was updated with OpenAI’s comments and updated revenue for Sonder.

#286. Apple HomeView review: A versatile smart display and Siri-powered home hub

The company’s smart screen debut heavily relies on an iPhone or iPad — for better and for worse.

Apple's entry into the smart display category was rumored for years. By the time its debut home hub device arrives this week, the company will be nearly a decade behind its main rivals in the space. In true Apple fashion, it refused to rush new products just because most of the competition was doing it. Now that the HomeView ($299) is here, Apple is hoping the iPhone faithful will like its mix of elevated design and newly expanded Siri capabilities.

Unlike smart displays from Amazon and Google, Apple's approach relies on a companion device. That means you'll need either an iPhone or an iPad to mirror apps using technology similar to CarPlay. As you'd imagine, that means the HomeView's usefulness, like CarPlay's, is directly tied to how much you use Apple's core apps — things like Messages, Calendar, Photos, Reminders and others. You'll also need to have some smart home devices to make the most of the smart display and be willing to use Safari as your recipe box. If all of that is OK to you, and if you're willing to give Siri access to personal data inside some of those apps, the HomeView can quickly become a very versatile device with something for your whole family.

I have fond memories of the iMac G4. It was the first computer I used in a design lab in college, and I loved how I could reposition the display to suit the task at hand. The first time I saw the HomeView, those memories flooded back. Apple's smart home display doesn't exactly replicate the G4 styling, but it's close enough for a decent nostalgia hit for me.

The HomeView essentially consists of two parts: a cylindrical, domed base and a 7-inch Liquid Retina display. At least, that's if you get the tabletop version. The wall-mounted option is just the screen, and you'll have to pay at least $59.95 for a mount. Every version of the HomeView has two full-range drivers in the display itself. The base houses additional components, like a down-firing woofer and a pair of force-canceling passive radiators for fuller sound with more bass than the wall-mounted models.

That display has an LED-backlit multi-touch IPS panel that offers a 1,088 x 1,088 resolution at 200 pixels per inch. Apple says it also added an oleophobic fingerprint-resistant coating to cut down on grime from everyday use. There's an ambient light sensor that enables the HomeView to adjust both brightness and warmth as needed. If it's in direct sunlight, you can expect the maximum 500 nits, but at night, it can dial things down to just 0.3 nits — the lowest brightness ever on an Apple display. The company believes this makes it perfect for a nightstand, if you're willing to have a camera pointed at or near your bed.

The HomeView's display has bezels like an iPad and the shape of a large Apple Watch. Unlike either of those devices, though, it has no physical buttons for controls. All of the interaction is done via swipes and taps on the touchscreen itself, or, as I'll elaborate on in a bit, with your voice. And unlike that iMac I'm nostalgic for, you can only tilt the display — you can't adjust its height. While that will suit most of your needs, I'm sure there are a lot of people who would've liked the ability to change the height or rotate the screen as well. Once you do adjust the way it tilts, a built-in accelerometer detects the new angle and alters the audio performance of the display's two drivers to match it.

Another item to discuss in terms of design is the HomeView's lone port. Around back, there's a single USB-C jack for power. Apple ships the standard version of the device with a USB-C to USB-C cable and a 30-watt power adapter. If you opt for the USB-C wall mount, which already costs $59.99, you'll need to purchase the power adapter separately. The company offers a 40W dynamic power adapter (up to 60W max) for $39 while the minimum required power supply (20W) will cost you $19. Obviously, the AC wired and PoE (power over Ethernet) wired mounts won't need a power adapter.

And lastly, there's the matter of color. For the standard (table or countertop) version of HomeView, you can choose from Silver, Starlight, Rose Pink or Space Gray options. On those, the sides and back of the display match the base. For the wall-mounted version, you can only pick between Silver and Space Gray. And, just in case you need the reminder, both HomeView models are the same price ($299).

One of the HomeView's most helpful traits is its ability to put Siri AI front and center in your home. The smart device runs on Apple's A18 chip, the processor that debuted with the iPhone 16 line in 2024. The company is using it here for its Secure Exclaves which give it the ability to process camera and microphone feeds on-device in a manner that can't be accessed by Apple. The company explained that this enables the HomeView to recognize your face and voice, or that of other users in your home, without exposing any of the data to the operating system.

That's important, because Siri AI now has access to more of your personal data, like your conversations in Messages, info from your Calendar invites and other bits of "personal context" from various apps. Of course, you have to opt-in to all of this by enabling Apple Intelligence on your iPhone or iPad, and you can limit which apps Siri can index data from. If you do decide to use the system, I can attest that it works well on the HomeView, though it's merely another vessel for the larger AI system.

During a lengthy conversation about lighthouses in North Carolina, Siri gave me information about visiting a variety of locations in the state. It provided information that I didn't specifically ask for, like which ones were still active, and got more comprehensive when my follow-up query was a bit vague. For example, when I asked "Which lighthouse is the easiest to get to?" Siri responded with both a list of ones you can park close to and highlighted the one that requires the least amount of climbing.

Siri was able to check my calendar to see if I was free to take the trip on a certain date and provided travel time from my house (including follow-up questions about the cities I'd pass through). I was finally able to stump the AI when I asked about "good hotels" near the Currituck Beach Lighthouse in Corolla, but even still, things felt more natural and conversational than interactions I've had with Siri in the past.

At times, Siri showed text answers on the HomeView in addition to reciting the info. When I asked about driving time or my schedule, a preview of the best route and the specific day on my calendar popped up respectively. What's more, I got a notification on my phone from the Home app letting me know Siri responded to a request for personal content. But it's important to remember that HomeView is just a conduit for Siri AI and its newly expanded abilities. These same things can be done on an iPhone or other compatible Apple devices, meaning you don't really have to spend $300 on a dedicated display for these tools.

One final note on security: Since the HomeView is heavily reliant on an iPhone or iPad, those devices must be nearby and recently unlocked (and enrolled in the shared home) for Siri to access personal data. The HomeView uses facial and voice recognition to know who's speaking, and to know when they walk away, so prying eyes can't see your texts if they rush over after you've been looking at Messages or other potentially sensitive apps. Guests or family members who don't have an iPhone or iPad can still use basic functions (control smart home devices, check weather, play music, etc), including asking Siri rudimentary questions.

When I first spent time with the HomeView last month, the feature I was most excited about was the recipe integration in Safari. I'm happy to report that feeling remains, for the most part, but there's one caveat that does impact the overall experience.

Let's back up for a second. On the HomeView, you can view a recipe link in Safari from your iPhone by holding it close to the display (just like you do to pass audio from your phone to a HomePod). Once the page loads, you'll see a cooking icon (a spoon and recipe card) in the bottom right corner. Tapping that will launch the HomeView's streamlined mode for the kitchen, which shows a recipe image with the dish's name, creator, cooking time and servings. There are two options underneath: Ingredients and Start Cooking.

The Ingredients option gives you a checklist-style collection of what you'll need for the recipe. If you need to add things to a shopping list in Reminders, Siri can do that for you. I had no trouble getting the AI assistant to add various ingredients to different shopping lists I have in that app (Costco, grocery store, etc). The HomeView will show its work by displaying the new entry in the Reminders app, and a Live Activities shortcut at the top allows you to quickly get back to the recipe with a tap.

Once you hit Start Cooking, the recipe's steps are divided into swipeable slides with inline shortcuts for any timers you need. Or, at least that's how it's supposed to be. While testing the HomeView, I pulled in recipes from my go-to cooks, and while some of them worked just like Apple advertised, others did not. This isn't a massive deal breaker, as you can still read the recipe's steps, but once you've experienced the correctly formatted version, it's hard to go back.

Going back means you'll get all of the steps on a single slide, requiring you to scroll instead of swipe. This is a long block of text, which can be overwhelming, especially compared to the short, easy-to-follow slides that alleviate some of the stress from following complicated instructions. And if you ever need to peek back at that ingredients list, the properly formatted versions have an always-present shortcut in the bottom right corner. To be clear, the formatting isn't an Apple problem; it's entirely up to the creator or publisher to present their recipes a certain way. I'm just pointing this all out to say Apple is advertising the ideal scenario, which is honestly an excellent experience, but you may not encounter it every time you cook with HomeView.

Another big piece of the HomeView puzzle is its ability to act as a smart home hub. With the Home app and devices compatible with HomeKit and Matter, this display becomes the command center for your cameras, thermostat, lighting and more. There's a Home dashboard that shows everything you've connected to the system. You can then create scenes and automations, or ask Siri to handle those for you.

We'll get to HomeView customization in a bit, but it's important to mention two Home faces here. The Home Modular face puts your favorite smart home devices on the same screen thanks to customizable widgets. These give you control over each connected gadget, including the ability to check a camera feed, without opening any apps on your phone. Of course, you can always ask Siri to do things for you by summoning the assistant on the HomeView. I'm sure Apple's AI is tired of adjusting my thermostat at this point, as that's what I've primarily been doing during my testing.

There's also a Home Camera face that's all video, all the time. Here, you can livestream up to nine feeds at once, giving you the ability to keep tabs on things wherever you have cameras positioned. If a visitor is detected, you can just tap on the HomeView to unlock a smart door lock (provided you have one of those, of course). We are a Blink household, though, and those aren't yet compatible with HomeKit, so I wasn't able to test any of Apple's camera features.

If you have an iCloud+ subscription, HomeView works with compatible security cameras (again, HomeKit and Matter) to detect people, pets, packages and vehicles. When any of those are seen, you'll get a notification and a live feed on the display. HomeView can also detect smoke or carbon monoxide alarms and send alerts to your iPhone, iPad or Apple Watch — even when you're away from home. That feature, known as Sound Recognition, is also available on the HomePod and HomePod mini.

The HomeView's speakers make it a solid option for music, podcasts and other audio entertainment. But, despite its crisp, vivid display, the device is not well-suited for watching videos. That's OK though, because right now, the only video app that's available on HomeView is Apple TV. And unless you like watching your shows within a thin band that sits in the middle of a square display, flanked by thick black bars on the top and bottom, you, too, will find the video experience on HomeView underwhelming. I would love to see Apple bring F1 multiview to this display though, putting two small alternate feeds below the main broadcast. Yes, they'd be tiny, but at least that's a better use of the space and eliminates the awkward black bars.

The HomeView runs on the same platform as CarPlay, which is why the various video streaming apps you have on your iPhone don't show up on this device. Apple says third-party developers are welcome to enable their apps for use on the HomeView, but doing so would also mean optimizing them for CarPlay. Only time will tell if developers are going to go through the extra steps of making that happen.

When it comes to audio-only content though, the HomeView is great. I'd classify the sound quality here as the midpoint between the HomePod mini and the larger HomePod. I've been reviewing the tabletop HomeView with a base, so I've enjoyed the added woofer and passive radiators, which balance out full-range drivers with extra bass. Across a variety of genres, bass is tight and punchy, and vocals cut through the mix clearly. The system never loses other instruments either, as you can easily pick up the guitars in Anberlin's "Gravity." Switch to something more chill, like Sam Barber's "If I May," and the acoustic instruments lay a base for the singer's unique vocals. Drums are more subdued here, and the HomeView does well to keep them from overpowering the mix.

I should also note the HomeView can get pretty darn loud. I measured 87dB with my Apple Watch standing right in front of the device. But I should also warn you that it's not a comfortable listening volume if you're standing close to the display. It might be nice for a party, but you'll likely stay around 50 percent or less for the most part.

Spatial Audio with Dolby Atmos is available on the HomeView for compatible music, movies and shows. However, given the compact stature of the device and its limited sonic prowess, you're more likely to notice the excellent overall clarity and even-handed tuning more than any dimensional effects. While watching an episode of Masters of the Air, for example, I found the sound perfectly fine, but wouldn't classify it as very immersive.

Apple included a few more entertainment-related conveniences on the HomeView that add utility. For one, it works with the HomePod, HomePod mini and AirPlay-compatible speakers for multiroom audio, which allows you to play the same thing everywhere or pick specific songs for each room. When listening to songs from Apple Music, you'll see animated album artwork if an artist has it, and lyrics are available with a single tap. And just like you can with your iPhone and HomePod speakers, you can handoff content from your phone to the HomeView just by placing your handset in front of the display.

The HomeView may look like an enlarged Apple Watch's screen, but the two devices also overlap in another area: faces. These screens are what you see when the HomeView is in what I like to call rest mode, when it's just sitting there not being actively used. You can have it just be a clock, as there are three faces just for that purpose, or display your calendar or the weather. There's also an animated Snoopy face that updates based on the time of day, weather, holidays and sporting events. Then there are the two Home faces that work with smart home devices that I already mentioned.

Those all have their purpose, but my two favorites are the Photos and Modular faces. The former makes the HomeView a digital picture frame and you can get specific about what shows up there. You can opt for it to cycle through photos of specific events (Memories), people, pets or cities, for example, but you can also set up Shared Albums that it will source from. If you do that last one, new photos will automatically appear every time they're added to the collection. The Modular face is what I've used most, as its customizable collection of widgets puts the calendar, date, time and weather in one spot. Of course, you can pick and choose what shows up here based on your needs or preferences, you're not forced to use the same combination I do.

The best thing about faces is that you can easily switch between them by swiping horizontally on one. A long press brings up the face customization interface where you're free to fine-tune things like color and widgets (where available) to your heart's content.

One feature that Apple has spent a lot of time showing off on the HomeView is FaceTime. The smart display has a 12-megapixel Center Stage camera in its top bezel, which means you can conduct video chats without reaching for your iPhone. Of course, the Center Stage software will keep you centered in the frame as you move around, which will be handy if you need to take a call from the kitchen while you're cooking, for example. You can start FaceTime calls on the HomeView, or you can hand them off from your iPhone. I'll also note that you can take voice calls from the HomeView, and in general, the device works well for both types of chats.

Find My is also available on the HomeView, allowing you to locate everything from your iPhone to your AirPods, or anything that has an AirTag attached to it. Of course, you can also use this to find family and friends who are sharing their location with you. The intercom feature from HomePods is on the HomeView as well, which comes in handy if you have multiple Apple speakers spread throughout your house. It also works with the iPhone, iPad and Apple Watch, plus you can send and receive intercom messages from anywhere — not just inside your home.

Lastly, Live Activities displays real-time info for a variety of tasks on HomeView. Like it does on your iPhone, the feature puts your progress at the top of the display on a floating, tappable button. Live Activities on the HomeView are a bit limited though, only capable of showing active timers/alarms, FaceTime calls, Safari media playback and Now Playing controls for Apple Music or Apple TV. I found it also surfaced audio controls for my go-to podcast app Pocket Casts as well. One thing that doesn't show up there that I wish it did is Live Activities from the Sports app. It would be great if the live scores for my favorite teams or an update on who's leading the F1 grand prix were available at a glance.

Apple is years behind the competition when it comes to smart displays. The company's two main rivals in this category, Amazon and Google, have been honing their products since 2017 and 2018 respectively. The premise has always been similar to Apple's: a combination display and speaker that's a conduit for each company's virtual assistant — or in 2026 terms, each company's AI.

Amazon's current Echo Show lineup has six options, ranging from 5 inches up to 21 inches, with the largest models ditching the speaker base for a wall mount or stand. The most affordable, the Echo Show 5, is $100. Google's current slate of smart displays is much more modest, with only the aging second-generation Nest Hub and Nest Hub Max as the available options. Those are going for $100 and $229 respectively, and only the pricier of the two has a built-in camera for video calls.

Apple may have lagged behind the competition on introducing a smart display, but the HomeView checks a lot of boxes that make it a very powerful option for iPhone users. It's also more elegantly designed than all of its main competitors. The ability to automatically distinguish between different family members, and for Siri to pull in personalized context for each person, makes this a useful home hub. When you factor in the cooking assistance, smart home compatibility and FaceTime chops, the HomeView can easily become the command center for any household. Just be sure to keep your iPhone (or iPad) handy if you want to make the most of it.
