#285. Giving Marjorie Taylor Greene a platform isn't good for America

Editor’s Note:  Dean Obeidallah, a former attorney, is the host of SiriusXM radio’s daily program “The Dean Obeidallah Show.” Follow him @DeanObeidallah@masto.ai. The opinions expressed in this commentary are his own. View more opinion on CNN.

Last year, GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene of Georgia spoke at a white nationalist event organized by Holocaust denier Nick Fuentes that caused Republican leaders to denounce her.

Last week, Greene’s Twitter account was temporarily suspended by the Elon Musk-headed platform over a tweet with a graphic referring to a “Trans Day of Vengeance,” as she denounced a planned transgender rights rally.

And come Tuesday, Greene has announced plans to protest in New York City when former President Donald Trump is expected to be arraigned on an indictment of more than 30 counts, calling the proceedings against him an “unconstitutional WITCH HUNT!”

But on Sunday, Greene was featured on CBS’ “60 Minutes” in an interview the long-running show promoted on Twitter with the tease: “Republican Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene, nicknamed MTG, isn’t afraid to share her opinions, no matter how intense and in-your-face they are. She sits down with Lesley Stahl this Sunday on 60 Minutes.” The images attached to this tweet by “60 Minutes” include Greene and Stahl walking through the US Capitol, taking a stroll outside and Greene showing Stahl something on her phone.

It's time for Marjorie Taylor Greene to go

In the segment that aired Sunday night, Stahl noted the congresswoman had moved from the fringe to the GOP’s front row in two years despite a “sharp tongue” and “some pretty radical views” as well as “over the top” comments such as “the Democrats are a party of pedophiles.” Stahl also referred to video of Greene chasing a Parkland, Florida, school shooting survivor, still maintaining that the 2020 election was stolen and failing to criticize Trump over spending. (The interview was conducted before news of his indictment.)

But Stahl didn’t mention Greene spoke at a white nationalist event a year ago while a member of Congress or her extreme anti-Muslim views and her defense of January 6 rioters.

Criticism of CBS for amplifying Greene has been swift and well-deserved even before the program aired. Former GOP Rep. Adam Kinzinger of Illinois tweeted beforehand: “Wow. Insane that 60 min would do this.” (Kinzinger is a CNN senior political commentator.)

Journalist Molly Jong-Fast also slammed “60 Minutes” with the tweet: “Attention is currency and 60 minutes is spending its currency on the Jewish space lasers woman.” (Jong-Fast was apparently referring to Greene’s past claim that a massive California wildfire was started by “a laser” beamed from space controlled by a prominent Jewish banking family.)

David Hogg, who survived the 2018 horrific school shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland and has since become an activist against gun violence, responded, “I look forward to your questions about why she thinks school shootings are fake and why she’s supported QAnon.”

Greene amplified the conspiracy theory — two years before being elected to Congress — that the Parkland shooting that took 17 lives was staged. And in 2019, Greene appeared on video confronting Hogg outside the Capitol when he was championing laws to save lives from gun violence, screaming that the then-teenager was a “coward.” She also called Hogg “#littleHitler” on social media.

Greene’s own tweet promoting Sunday’s segment was not filled with her typical smears of “fake news” when she doesn’t like the coverage. Rather, Greene urged people to tune in as she praised “60 Minutes” host Stahl (and misspelled her first name): “It was an honor to spend a few days with the legendary icon Leslie Stahl and talented crew @60Minutes.” Greene added, “Leslie is a trailblazer for women in journalism. And while we may disagree on some issues, I respect her greatly.”

Now, it is true that “60 Minutes” over its 50-plus-year history has featured what the show has dubbed “controversial” guests. These include a 2000 interview with Oklahoma City bomber Timothy McVeigh, whose 1995 terrorist attack left 168 dead, including 19 children. And the show did a 1979 interview with Iran’s Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini.

Perhaps “60 Minutes” views Greene — who has repeatedly downplayed and even defended the January 6, 2021, attack plus called for the GOP to become the party of “Christian nationalism” — in the vein of its long list of controversial guests. (CBS had not responded to a request from CNN for comment at time of publishing.)

However, the choice of Greene as a guest instantly recalls the comments of then-CBS CEO Les Moonves during Trump’s 2016 presidential campaign when he admitted that Trump’s candidacy “may not be good for America, but it’s damn good for CBS.” Moonves added, “The money’s rolling in and this is fun. … (T)his is going to be a very good year for us,” concluding, “Sorry. It’s a terrible thing to say. But bring it on, Donald. Keep going.”

Moonves may be gone from CBS, but it appears his mindset continues at “60 Minutes.” And that may be good for “60 Minutes,” but it’s definitely not good for America.

#284. Borussia Dortmund's Felix Nmecha and the question of religion

Felix Nmecha's "Crowns down" celebration has drawn criticism from a Borussia Dortmund fan club, reopening debate over faith, inclusion and religious messaging in football.

Felix Nmecha's renewed use of the "Crowns down celebration" — where a crown is pretended to be removed from someone's head and placed at Jesus' feet — has sparked a reaction from Borussia Dortmund's official supporters' group.

"Faith does not justify exclusion. Faith does not justify division. Borussia Dortmund is a club for everyone," read a statement after the Bundesliga club beat Elversberg, a game in which Nmecha scored twice. Nmecha's first documented "Crowns down celebration" was at the 2026 World Cup, for which "The King's Return" campaign was launched.

"In the past, the the Borussia Dortmund supporters club has deliberately refrained from commenting on sports-related issues and the athletic performance of individual players. We remain committed to this approach," the statement continued.

"However, when it comes to our club's values, discrimination, and the question of what view of humanity is fostered within and around Borussia Dortmund, we cannot and will not remain silent."

The fan club states their issue is not with Nmecha being a devout Christian, but rather his public support for "The King's Return" campaign, which the supporters club said "gives us cause to reassess the situation."

This campaign, according to research by German public broadcaster ARD in the summer of 2026, was designed by the "Ballers in God" (BIG) network. BIG was founded in 2015 by footballer John Bostock, a devout Christian who wanted to create a community where professional footballers could strengthen each other in their faith, pray together, and read the Bible. In over a decade since, the BIG community has grown to nearly 800,000 followers on Instagram.

Nmecha issued a response on social media not long afterwards, writing:

"I've been told that my most recent goal celebration has led to some misunderstandings," Nmecha wrote.

"My message was never intended to spread intolerance, discrimination, or hatred in any form. As Christians, our message is one of love and respect for every person—regardless of where someone comes from, what they believe in, or what worldview they hold."

Reports suggest that both the board at Dortmund and Germany head coach Jürgen Klopp will speak to Nmecha on the matter in the coming days.

The fan club say their criticism is "directed at religious networks that, under the guise of faith, promote a worldview in which people are devalued or marginalized because of their sexual orientation, gender identity, lifestyle, or personal choices... When religious beliefs are used to deny people their dignity or equality, that is where our tolerance for intolerance ends."

Here, they are referring to the recent ARD investigation. That work revealed the homepage for "The King's Return" campaign featured a link to a live counseling chat run by US-based evangelical nonprofit Groundwire. During the investigations, counselors told reporters that abortion is "Satan's plan" and that homosexuality is a sin.

Borussia Dortmund's statement of core values commits the club to diversity, human rights, and inclusion regardless of gender, sexual orientation, faith, or background. The club's largest and most influential fan group also promotes progressive, anti-fascist, and inclusive values. It is also part of a wider fan landscape in Germany where club members are more involved in club decisions than in many other countries. This was evidenced when the club signed a deal with arms manufacturer Rheinmetall two years ago and in the case of Nmecha as well. When the midfielder signed for Dortmund in 2023, many of the club's fans protested because the player had previously shared or liked several transphobic and homophobic posts on his Instagram account. In response, Nmecha deleted some posts and instead issued a statement emphasizing "I do not discriminate against anyone."

Article 4 of German Basic Law guarantees that each individual is allowed to decide how strongly, why and which god to believe in. In football, players cannot display political, religious, or personal slogans, statements, or images on their jerseys, undershirts, or gear. However, physical gestures are allowed so long as they don't delay the game or antagonize the crowd.

The use of physical religious gestures has history in sports, as professor Paul Putz described in his book "The Spirit of the Game: American Christianity and Big-Time Sports". Putz describes how the the pioneers of the Christian athlete movement realized they: "could influence others to Christian ideals more effectively on the field than in the pulpit."

And yet, such gestures can also be at odds with the atmosphere of modern sports arenas, particularly football. In their book "Sports and Christianity: Historical and Contemporary Perspectives", Nick J. Watson and Andrew Parker explain how modern sport has become a substitute for traditional faith.

"Sport seduces the teeming 'global village'; it is the new opiate of the masses; it is one of the great modern experiences … Sport, for many, has replaced religion as a source of emotional catharsis and spiritual passion," they wrote.

With the 26-year-old Nmecha a key player for both club and country, the conversation around faith and football looks set to continue for some time yet.

#283. X launches its own clipping feature to spur influencer engagement

Over the past few years, X has released a number of features to make itself a friendlier hub for influencers. The latest effort to attract social media talent? Its own clipping tool.

Clipping is the pivotal social media technique by which longer videos are spliced into TikTok-attention-span length “clips” and then strategically posted to engage as many eyeballs (and thus derive as much ad revenue) as possible.

On Monday, X announced the tool, which, from what we can tell, appears to be little more than a straightforward video-trimming feature that lets users edit videos and post them to X.

The site says that every clip includes a “Clip from” label that links back to the original video from which it was derived, allowing X users to see more about the creator and the creator’s content.

“Grab your favorite moment from any video, add your take, and post it without leaving X,” the company posted.

The platform added that both creators and clippers can “earn from views on the clip through Original Content Rewards,” X’s rewards program. The program is only available for eligible creators and pays out based on specific levels of audience engagement.

In many cases, influencers pay special firms to handle clipping for them. More and more large companies are also turning to such services to run marketing campaigns, and the technique has been used in political campaigns as well.

When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence.

#282. The first batch of Android XR smartglasses made me rethink my stance on specs without displays

I still want models with built-in screens, but Google’s first run of intelligent eyewear has a lot to offer.

The first device powered by Android XR came out last year when Samsung released the Galaxy XR headset. But this fall, Google is taking its augmented reality platform in a different direction with the launch of intelligent eyewear from partners such as Warby Parker and Gentle Monster. This gives Google its own version of lightweight smartglasses to rival competitors like the third-gen Ray-Ban Meta line, and after testing them out, it made me reconsider my feelings about smart specs without built-in displays.

As this first generation of intelligent eyewear is a bit of a departure from previous Android XR devices, I think it's important to get some of the basics out of the way first. For this family of specs, Google is in charge of the OS while Samsung is handling much of the tech — including things like the cameras, mics, touch sensors and more. All of that then gets handed over to Warby Parker and Gentle Monster, who are in charge of the glasses design, lenses, general sales and customer support.

Right off the bat, this seems like a great division of responsibilities because it allows each company to do what they're best at. Google handles the software, Samsung takes care of the hardware and the real glasses makers ensure you have good-looking frames with the right prescriptions (you can even opt for transition/photochromic lenses). And right out of the gate, it feels like there's already a lot of interesting styles and color combos to choose from, regardless if you're a bit more fashion-forward or if you, like me, prefer a more traditional frame.

Another important thing to note is that while Gentle Monster's eyewear will be a touch more expensive than Warby Parker's models ($449 and $395, respectively), they will have the same general tech specs. Those include the same 2GB of RAM and 64GB of storage, a 12MP camera (that also handles up to 3K video), stereo speakers, far-field mics and a built-in touch sensor. Battery life is also the same across the board with around nine hours of use on a single charge, while the case for the glasses stores enough juice for multiple full charges.

Furthermore, you won't be required to have a paid Gemini plan to use any of the features of the glasses; it's all free. These glasses will even work with both Android devices and iPhones with feature parity across both OSes. That said, there won't be any options with a built-in display, which means Google won't have a true rival to the Meta Ray-Ban Display glasses, at least not yet.

When it comes to features, it's no surprise that these glasses have a lot of overlap with the Ray-Ban Meta specs. There's a physical button near the right temple for capturing images: A short press takes a picture while a long press records videos. There's also a touch sensor built into the right arm for doing stuff like controlling volume, pausing audio or dismissing Gemini. And of course, the glasses are connected to your phone wirelessly (either Bluetooth or Wi-Fi depending on the situation) so you have access to streaming music, notifications, messages, supported apps and more. However, the real lynchpin of the entire platform is Google's AI, which can be easily summoned via voice controls or by performing a long press on the touch panel.

Now I'll admit that I don't interact with Gemini all that often when I'm using a phone or a laptop. When I already have access to a screen, a keyboard and all sorts of dedicated apps, so I find I can often perform tasks faster manually than asking an AI assistant to do them. But when wearing smartglasses, it's a whole different story. If you want to respond to a text, get turn-by-turn directions or start some tunes, you can do all that hands-free with simple voice commands while keeping your phone in your pocket. Granted, that is sort of table stakes for smartglasses these days, but being able to walk around with your head up while dodging traffic or other pedestrians can't be understated.

But it goes deeper, because thanks to Gemini's intelligence and persistent memory between conversations, the AI in your glasses has a much deeper understanding of how to help you. Instead of constantly having to say a wake word all the time, you can have more natural back and forth dialogue. This allows you to get updates on your events, make adjustments to questions and pick up on previous queries even when they happen throughout the course of your day (you can specify how long you want Gemini to remember conversations).

Where things get really interesting is the combination of computer vision and AI, which allowed me to do things like pick up a physical invite card and simply ask Gemini to add it to my calendar. No need to grab my phone, open up an app and add details like the time, date and location myself. I was also able to do things like simply look at a bunch of different ingredients and ask Gemini to make a smoothie from what was in front of me. It can even do rough estimates for how much of a certain food has been added, so if you're not worried about being super precise, you can leave your measuring cups in a drawer. And if you run out of certain foods, you can easily tell Gemini to add something to your grocery list.

Then there are other features like live translation, which feels kind of magical. The glasses are designed so that the mics are directed in a cone-shaped space in front of you, so that even in a room with other conversations going on, the glasses don't get confused. Not only does this let you switch speakers naturally just by looking at someone else, Gemini will even adjust the output of a translation to sound similar to the original speaker. This allows you to follow along in real-time almost as well as someone who speaks the language naturally.

That said, the thing that impressed me the most about these Android XR glasses is the platform itself. Compared to some rivals where a lot of features feel like a work in progress, Google's OS feels much more mature. This includes little touches like dedicated sounds when Gemini accesses your camera, when the glasses take a photo or video, when Gemini leverages external AI and more. Google has done a pretty good job of translating a lot of the most important features you use on your phone to a medium without a screen.

The one caveat to all this is that you do have to buy into talking to and relying rather heavily on Gemini. I'm still not sure I'm 100 percent on board with that, but at least for me, these glasses make a better argument for always-on AI support than almost any other class of device I've used. So while I'm still waiting for companies to deliver streamlined specs with built-in displays, these audio-first options feel like much more than a stepping stone towards that. They're surprisingly full-featured, and I don't think it's a stretch to say that compared to rival assistants like Siri, Muse and others, Gemini is the smartest of the bunch. So even if you primarily want these for playing music, keeping tabs on your schedule and getting occasional help from AI, Google's first batch of Android XR-powered intelligent eyewear feels like it's already jumped to the front of the pack.

Pre-orders for the new family of Android XR intelligent eyewear from both Warby Parker and Gentle Monster begin today with official sales slated for December 10.

#281. Review: Apple's M6 iMac is pricey, but it's still a good choice for a clean desk

Apple’s M6 keeps the iMac current without shaking things up much.

The Apple Silicon iMac got its fourth refresh this month.

Every other Apple Silicon Mac has found a way to move forward over the last half-decade—we’ve gotten a more Pro design for the MacBook Pro, a mini-er design for the Mac mini, and a Mac Studio that has finally made Apple admit it doesn’t want to make the Mac Pro anymore.

But the Apple Silicon iMac has always been more interested in looking backward. It’s the thin, screen-on-a-stand iMac design by way of the original Bondi Blue G3 iMac. It’s a clean and pretty desktop computer, one I see all the time in small offices and on TV shows that depict small offices. We’re long past the days when it was Apple’s primary workstation computer.

So I wanted to write about this new iMac from the perspective of that entry-level iMac buyer, who chooses the iMac because it’s simple and then uses it until it breaks. What does a new iMac get you if you’re stepping up from the M1 model or comparing it against an older refurbished M4 model? Without any real external updates, what makes this iMac iteration interesting?

If you haven’t used an Apple Silicon-era iMac before, here’s a brief primer.

The new iMac is much thinner than the older aluminum Intel models. Its front is mostly one big 24-inch screen, surrounded on all sides by a relatively thick white bezel (I still wish black were at least an option, but Apple apparently doesn’t agree).

White bezel aside, it’s by far Apple’s most colorful computer. The iMacs ship with color-matched fronts, backs, stands, keyboards, mice, trackpads, and cables. Apple has tweaked the color offerings and changed the tints of each specific color a few times over the years, but the M6 iMac uses the same six color tints (plus silver) that the M4 iMac did.

The basic $1,499 iMac is essentially a MacBook Air in terms of ports—it has two Thunderbolt ports on the back and a headphone jack on the left side. Choosing this model also locks you into a lesser version of the Apple M6 with 10 CPU cores and 10 GPU cores instead of 12 of each, a maximum of 24GB of RAM, a minimum of 256GB of storage, and a maximum of 1TB. Its default keyboard doesn’t have Touch ID support, and the only way to get it is to buy the version with the built-in number pad, an $80 upsell.

The $1,699 version is the one to get if you still intend to use the iMac as a workstation. It includes the full-speed version of the M6 with all its CPU and GPU cores intact (though there are performance caveats here we’ll talk about later), plus options for 32GB of RAM and up to 2TB of storage. This version also includes four Thunderbolt ports on the back for connecting additional accessories. Both available keyboards on this version have Touch ID.

A 2.5 gigabit Ethernet port can be added to any iMac for $30. This port is located on the desktop’s power brick rather than the iMac itself, an elegant solution that keeps even a wired Internet connection hidden beneath your desk with the other cables. All iMacs also offer a VESA mount adapter option in place of the default stand at no extra charge.

I still really like the effect created by the color-matched keyboard, mouse/trackpad, and cables—even the white bezel complements the white keycaps and mouse. Just know you’re paying quite a lot for how the desktop looks. The $899 Mac mini is $800 cheaper than the iMac with the same chip, the same amount of RAM, and the same amount of storage. Sure, it would cost something to add a nice-looking 4K monitor, but not $800, even if you go for extra frills like a high refresh rate.

It’s fine that the iMac upcharges you for style and convenience. That’s the point of the computer. Just make sure that’s what you want to pay for.

Here are the basic specs for every entry-level iMac Apple has shipped, going all the way back to the last of the Intel models.

The iMac’s evolution has been slow and gradual during the Apple Silicon era. The computer has never gotten a base storage upgrade, and the base M4 and M6 models still only support a single external display (the upgraded models support two external displays, finally surpassing the old Intel model).

If you have any kind of Apple Silicon iMac and you’re happy with how it’s performing and you’re not hankering for more RAM or more displays, I don’t think the M6 model really adds anything worth upgrading for. It’s definitely faster, but even six years on, the Apple M1 remains perfectly serviceable for daily use, especially when it’s attached to 16GB of RAM.

And if you’re in the market for an iMac now, I think the M4 model still gets you the best bang for your buck if you can find one refurbished or on sale. That one still gets you a minimum of 16GB of RAM, Thunderbolt in all four ports for the four-port model, the 32GB RAM option, the nano-texture display option, and support for multiple external displays (again, in the four-port model).

The M6 iMac builds on that, and it comes with real performance benefits (more on that shortly). But the brightest dividing line in the Apple Silicon iMac model history is between the M3 and M4 model, not the M4 and M6 model. And that’s true despite the two-generation silicon upgrade.

A quick recap on the M6: a total of 12 CPU cores (two super cores, four performance cores, six efficiency cores), either 10 or 12 GPU cores, 16/24/32GB RAM options, and a doubled Neural Engine NPU with 32 cores instead of 16. For the iMac, the M6 also introduces some of the architectural tweaks introduced with the Apple M5, including the addition of neural accelerator hardware to the GPU cores. This boosts the speed of some kinds of local AI workloads compared to the M4, including MetalFX upscaling and frame generation in games.

The M6 in the Mac mini and the M6 in the 14-inch MacBook Pro both perform similarly within a roughly 28 W sustained power envelope. The version with 16GB of RAM will perform slightly worse in games and other workloads sensitive to memory bandwidth—the 16GB M6 offers 153 GB/s of memory bandwidth, and the 24GB and 32GB versions offer 170 GB/s. But that’s a pretty minor difference.

The iMac is slower than either of those two Macs, particularly in multi-core CPU benchmarks and graphics benchmarks, and the HandBrake video encoding test shows the likely culprit. The iMac’s M6 consumes an average of 18 W of power under sustained heavy load. It won’t throttle its speeds as aggressively as a MacBook Air because the iMac still includes an active cooling fan, but the reduced power envelope does mean that the Mac mini or MacBook Pro are 5 to 8 percent faster than the iMac in heavy multi-core CPU tasks and about 8 to 12 percent faster in graphics benchmarks.

One benefit of the smaller power envelope is that the iMac’s chip is even more power-efficient than the regular M6 and the iMac’s version of the M4, using much less power to do the same amount of work.

That still makes it a solid upgrade over the M4. The iMac’s M6 is 15 to 20 percent faster in our single-core CPU benchmarks, between 20 and 30 percent faster in multi-core CPU benchmarks, and between 20 and 40 percent faster in graphics benchmarks (the Blender GPU benchmarks do even better—they seem to like the neural accelerators in the M5 and M6 GPUs). Especially with 24GB or 32GB of RAM, it’s perfectly capable of being the heart of a multi-monitor workstation setup or a light photo/video editing rig.

The M6 iMac changes almost nothing about who the iMac is for, its position in Apple’s lineup, or what it’s technically capable of. It’s a box-ticking upgrade, and the box being ticked is “keep the insides up to date.”

That means today’s iMac is best for the same kinds of computer users it was good for in 1998: people who don’t want a laptop, people who need a friendly and approachable kiosk or office computer, and people who just want something pretty that doesn’t require a lot of fussing with screens or cables.

That’s the main thing I miss about being an iMac user. I like how my current Mac Studio desktop works, and I need flexibility and complexity in my specific setup to get the Mac and a gaming PC connected to all the same stuff. But despite my best efforts, the cable situation has always been a barely managed mess.

If someone you know has a cable management problem and doesn’t need the complexity, congratulations, you’ve found someone the iMac is for. I just wish Apple would use these kinds of pretty, saturated colors in a laptop or desktop that meets my needs, you know?

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#280. Nvidia’s crisis has corporate governance experts reaching for the popcorn

Nvidia’s stock sank 5.47% on Tuesday, following a 4% drop on Monday, a loss of about 9.5% in two days, even though there wasn’t much news out of the company. CEO Jensen Huang has reportedly been trying to conduct business as usual after admitting to an extramarital affair with Alice Huang, whom U.S. intelligence agencies believe was spying for China. The biggest development: the board hired the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an internal investigation.

What comes next is mostly a guessing game, even for the people who study how boards are supposed to handle crises. Two corporate governance professors, Ann Lipton of the University of Colorado and Todd Henderson of the University of Chicago, say there is no real precedent for a CEO caught up in a suspected honeypot. And while they have views on what the investigation should ask, like everyone else, they’re watching to see how it plays out.

“There have actually been affair cases and sexual harassment cases,” Lipton said, but she was quick to add that she doesn’t know of previous cases that also involve potential national security compromises. Henderson, whose research interests include corporations, securities regulation, and law and economics, said much depends on facts few people have yet, including, most importantly, what, if anything, Alice Huang got out of Jensen Huang. (Secrets about Nvidia’s fabs or chip designs would matter to the Justice Department. Ordinary corporate information probably would not.)

Lipton described the investigation led by Nvidia’s independent board members, with their own outside counsel, as perhaps the most “standard” aspect of the whole affair so far. She also observed that an investigation will be “exactly as thorough” as the directors running it want it to be, which is also standard. Henderson made a related point about incentives, saying that Washington law firms that conduct internal investigations “know who pays their bills.” He didn’t suggest that’s happening here.

Lipton said she sees three lines of inquiry, including whether the law was broken, whether Huang violated Nvidia’s internal policies, and whether the board was misled, including how forthcoming he was and when he was on notice. It’s notable, as Wired reported Monday, that Huang hired a PR crisis firm known for handling sex scandals a full year ago. Of course, the driver could have been about the affair alone. It’s unclear when Huang learned Alice Huang was a suspected spy. The board said in a Sunday statement about the matter that Nvidia “has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised.”

Henderson similarly stressed the importance of what Huang knew and when. He described two scenarios. In one, Huang hires a PR firm because an extramarital affair is embarrassing, without knowing who the woman is. In the other, he knows she is a suspected Chinese spy. “I think that’s very different,” he said. “If I were a board member and the CEO of a company with very sensitive technology was in that situation, I would be furious if they didn’t tell me.” Not telling the board that you have cancer “or that you’ve been sleeping with a Chinese spy,” said Henderson, “seems to me to be a fireable offense.”

Either way, Lipton expects shareholder suits, and she said two kinds are possible. Securities fraud, which she said is “only about lying,” is the most obvious, and as we reported yesterday, Nvidia is already facing a proposed class action lawsuit that alleges Nvidia and Huang misled investors by concealing Huang’s relationship with Alice Huang while describing dealings with her former employer as routine. “You can have as many affairs with Chinese spies as you like,” Lipton said. “You just can’t lie about it under the federal securities laws.”

A mismanagement claim under Delaware law, where Nvidia is incorporated, is much harder. Employees do “stupid or reckless or damaging things all the time,” she said, and courts don’t let shareholders sue each time. Shareholders bringing such a claim on the company’s behalf, known as a derivative suit, must first show the board is incapable of handling the problem, usually by proving directors are implicated or so beholden to the CEO that they can’t act independently.

Nvidia’s 10-member board has a track record of loyalty and deference to Huang, as outlined by a New York Times piece Tuesday morning. Still, the investigation those “independent” directors have announced with outside counsel makes that very hard. Lipton cited, for example, a shareholder suit over alleged mismanagement at McDonald’s several years ago, after its chief people officer was accused of harassment and its CEO had a relationship with a subordinate. Ultimately, the court found no reason the board couldn’t deal with the misconduct on its own.

Even if none of this ends up threatening Huang’s job, the episode raises other governance questions that don’t look great in retrospect. Nvidia is run with a famously flat structure, with more than 60 direct reports to Huang, and the company doesn’t appear to have a succession plan, which Lipton called “a danger for any company.”

Huang’s children also work at Nvidia. His son Spencer is a director of product management, his daughter Madison is a senior director of product marketing, and his son-in-law, Nico Caprez, works in business development. Some employees believe Huang is grooming successors, and when asked last month by Bloomberg whether Madison is in line for the job, Huang didn’t shoot down the idea, saying instead that it’s not something he’d wish on her.

Lipton called hiring the kids poor corporate hygiene, even though founders have long done it. (Kimbal Musk is on Tesla’s board because he’s Elon Musk’s brother, she noted.) Directors are supposed to act only for stockholders, but founders get more rope as long as the company is thriving.

Henderson similarly calls himself a strong “anti-nepotism person,” sharing that when his father worked at Westinghouse’s nuclear research lab, there was an absolute ban on hiring employees’ children. “Like, fine for them to be working there,” he said of Huang’s children working at Nvidia. But “the idea that one of his kids would succeed him” at a publicly traded company the size of Nvidia “seems crazy,” he added.

What Nvidia may have gotten more right, from a governance standpoint, is what it doesn’t have, which is dual-class shares. Huang reportedly owns about 3.6%, so he doesn’t control the vote. That makes him “easier to dislodge,” Lipton said, even though he is deeply integrated into the company in other ways.

Neither professor is rooting for or against Huang, each made clear. The simple truth is that with voting control on top of Huang’s holdings, the board would have even less flexibility. Without it, the board has a few more options.

#279. Google really needs you to know it cares about smart glasses privacy

Public trust in smart glasses is very important for selling lots of smart glasses.

The world has changed in the ten years since Snap first launched its camera-equipped Spectacles. Not only has the smart glasses market grown, the number of people sharing videos online has also jumped significantly. Nowadays, thanks to the rise of platforms like TikTok and Reels, anyone can be an influencer. And the possibility of innocent passersby inadvertently being included in a viral clip is far higher than it was.

It is in this climate that Google launches its intelligent eyewear devices today. This year has already seen Meta catch heat for what people called its "pervert glasses" or "creep glasses." In addition to making people feel uncomfortable about being spied on or filmed without their consent, the controversy has also discouraged existing users of those glasses from continuing to wear them, not to mention possibly putting off potential buyers.

Let's start with what's happening on the glasses. It's basically table stakes at this point, but they all have an LED light on them to indicate when someone is taking a picture or recording a video. The light, which sits on the left side of the frame while the camera is next to the right eye, has sensors to detect if it's covered or tampered with and will prevent the capture of pictures or video.

"Unfortunately, some of the glasses that have launched in the past launched without any kind of detection for those things, and that broke trust." Payne said. "And that's a problem," he added. He also said Google's methods are "pretty effective," though that's something that we'll have to test for ourselves to verify.

And just as there are bad actors who will continue to try to break security protections for any safe in the world or online, there will be people attempting to get around Google's anti-tampering measures here. Payne said "there will always be ways around what we build, and then we'll try to fix those holes," and that he expects there will be an ongoing back-and-forth. "But nonetheless, we're very happy with where we're starting on this one."

One of the main functions of Google's smart glasses is the ability for Gemini AI to use the camera to see what the wearer is looking at and provide more relevant information. During this process, the company says that the camera visuals won't be stored in your Gemini history. When there are faces in view, Google says "your glasses are also designed to automatically redact bystanders' faces before Gemini processes the visuals."

The camera stream also won't be used in AI training or ad targeting, and Google says any data that is sent off the glasses for processing, say in the cloud or on your phone, is encrypted for transit. It's also promising to bring the Private AI Compute processing platform to glasses, enabling these new devices to use the "hardware-secured sealed cloud environment" for greater processing.

While the camera may be the most obvious recording sensor on these devices, they also have four microphones onboard to allow you to speak to Gemini or get translations for people around you. Google uses beamforming to help focus on the individuals you're looking at when deciding whose speech to translate, and the company also said it "designed Gemini to focus on your voice by default, and not other voices around you, using directional microphones on the glasses."

Payne said that the microphones will filter out "all the world audio, as we call it, the bystander audio," It helps prevent accidental recording of people in your vicinity, but also improves Gemini's effectiveness by making sure it doesn't confuse someone else's heated argument as your query.

Of course, to make the glasses more useful, you'll have to grant it access to your apps and personal information. The intelligent eyewear can work with apps like Messages, Keep or YouTube Music, for instance, to send replies or create lists for you. Like on most phones, you'll have to grant permission to specific apps before they can access the camera on your glasses, and even after you do, you can still add or revoke access for every app.

Google also said you can view your past interactions with Gemini, delete individual chats or all of them. You'll also be able to decide if your conversations are used to train AI. And to make sure there's no confusion over whether you left the glasses on or off, and if the microphones or camera might still be tuning in, all the new eyewear have a power switch with a clear red indicator to tell you when they've been shut down.

Most intriguing are the measures Google is implementing across its entire ecosystem. The company said it has protections in place "across Google apps and services to deter misuse of the glasses," and they're pretty far-reaching.

For one thing, Google Shopping and Google Ads will "ban the sale and advertising of stickers or accessories designed to hide recording lights."

The company is also working to cull the demand for videos of a harassing or creepy nature. Payne said that one of the problems Google has seen is a small number of people "who use glasses to take creepy videos."

YouTube will "prohibit content that depicts things like harassment and unwanted sexualization. Any video uploaded to YouTube — whether it's recorded on a phone, a camera, or glasses — has to follow these policies, and videos that don't are removed."

There are also considerations for apps that request permission to use the cameras on these devices. "There's multiple approval layers if an app wants to use the camera," Payne said. "And there's actually some use cases that we're just blanket cutting off."

To that end, Google said that the Play Store and API policies will "prohibit apps from using the camera for social live-streaming or real-time facial identification, and from changing the behavior of the outer indicator light." This live-streaming restriction also applies to YouTube.

Of course, since the eyewear will be compatible with iOS devices as well, it's possible that someone finds an app of that nature in the App Store and uses it. And though Google may ban the advertisement or sale of physical goods that can be used to hide the LED indicator, people could very easily procure one on Amazon or Temu. There's more that needs to be done against this problem, and Google alone cannot cover all bases.

Sure, there's the honor system approach that most modern recording devices already use. Things like requiring an 18+ Google account to activate an intelligent eyewear device and asking users to agree to not recording others without permission or in sensitive places are in place here as well.

But the responsibility of prevention of misuse doesn't just fall to the user or Google. There needs to be greater collaboration across the industry for these measures to be effective. Payne said that Google is sharing these statements now in an attempt to set an example and send a message. "We hope that it resonates with people, and we hope that as a byproduct of that, some others in this space say, 'You know what? We should probably tighten up some of our policies.'"

If it's able to get the attention and cooperation of other players in the space, Google stands to benefit. "We think that that will then earn a lot of trust back in the category, then we can focus a lot on how cool and valuable these are."

#276. Plurai wants to help AI agents work better using the same approach that helped get self-driving cars to roads

AI agents are all the rage, but they’re not quite as capable as their purveyors would have you believe.

Efforts to benchmark agents doing complex tasks show that they aren’t typically reliable enough to function without close supervision. LiveBench 2.0 shows Claude Fable 5.2 and ChatGPT-6 Astra succeeding on 82.9 and 75.6 out of 100 tasks, respectively, and an effort by the AI infrastructure company EON to test agents in a simulated corporate environment found the most successful model tested, Claude Opus 4.8, performed tasks at 76.8% accuracy.

Now, a startup known as Plurai wants to boost those numbers with the same techniques that put self-driving cars on the street: Simulations using synthetic data based on a company’s own digital systems, meant to prepare models to tackle difficult edge cases.

Co-founders Ilan Kadar and Elad Levi previously worked for Nexar, which develops autonomous driving technology. Kadar worked with Waymo to simulate and evaluate its end-to-end deep learning driving software.

“The moment that we saw the wave of AI agents, we saw the same pattern: That it is very easy to build a demo, but [hard] to get something that will be reliable and cost effective,” Kadar told TechCrunch. “Since we saw the same pattern, the same pitch was that we want to build this simulation layer.”

On Wednesday, the startup said it had raised $13 million in seed funding to make that happen. The round was led by Team8, with participation from U&I, Velocity, and Mercer.

Like the end-to-end models trained to drive cars, AI agents are probabilistic systems operating in unpredictable environments. When pressed by customers, agents tasked with booking travel can ignore spending policies. Or, when tasked with querying one database, agents can reliably provide an answer, but combining information from multiple datasets tends to gum up the works. A long conversation with a customer service bot can overwhelm an agent with too much context and lead to wrong turns.

To fix that, Plurai creates a digital twin of the company’s electronic systems, including the knowledge base and tools that an agent will have access to in production. It then maps all the possible flows of the agents and uses that graph to prepare the model with evaluations and fixes for possible edge cases. The platform works alongside existing agentic tools like Claude Code, Codex, Copilot, and Cursor.

However, Kadar says the company’s real promise is using the data it gathers to post-train small, open-weight models tailored to individual customer needs. Tests performed in cooperation with Nvidia and Nebius showed the company’s models outperforming midtier frontier models at lower costs.

“We can take a large language model, we can take the task, decompose it into a smaller task using the simulation, train [an open-weight] model, and using the simulation, verify that the quality is higher or comparable to the frontier model, and an order or two orders of magnitude cost reduction,” he said.

Reah Miyara, a Google DeepMind researcher who backed Plurai as an angel investor, said the company’s edge comes from understanding failure modes in production, and generating synthetic data to tune models. As businesses try to optimize their AI expense, Plurai’s approach could prove a real advantage, since IT leaders are seeing that “frontier-capable models are out of distribution with my specific data, and the cost of these things isn’t sustainable in the long term,” Miyara said.

Plurai hopes analogies between AI agents in physical and digital environments can prove predictive: While powerful general AI models are imbuing robots with fascinating capabilities, they aren’t yet reliable enough to be deployed widely. Meanwhile, robots powered by AI models built for specific tasks are in the field right now, working for customers and gathering valuable real-world data.

“My view is that no matter how smart OpenAI and Anthropic model will be — and they will be much smarter — they will not know your business and your policy,” Kadar said.

#275. Hundreds of streaming services and nothing to watch

Most viewers wish there was a TV Guide for streaming.

Bruce Springsteen famously said those words during a concert and later in a song in the 1990s about the number of TV channels seemingly growing disproportionately to the availability of enjoyable content.

Within a few years, there would be hundreds of TV channels and still, some would argue in hyperbolic frustration, “nothing to watch.”

The advent of streaming services was poised as a potential solution to this problem. It granted viewers easy, on-demand access to thousands of movies and shows, which, theoretically, could ensure that viewers could always find something to watch.

According to a survey from Nielsen’s Gracenote, though, that’s not exactly the case today.

Gracenote, a Nielsen-owned content data business, surveyed 3,000 video streaming users in the US, Brazil, France, Germany, Mexico, and the UK who were at least 18 years old for its “2026 State of Play: Amid fragmentation frustrations, UX is the future of streaming” report. It found that many struggle to find something to stream. However, this is not necessarily because there’s nothing good on, but rather because they find it difficult to home in on good content on streaming services.

For example, over 51 percent of respondents said that “the abundance of content and [streaming] services is making it harder to find something to watch.”

Further, 19.2 percent of respondents said that, on average, they either “always” or “often” don’t know what to watch, can’t find anything, and then decide to do something else. And 27.4 percent of respondents said that, on average, they either always or often try to watch a specific program but end up not watching it because they couldn’t find it on streaming.

“Through the lens of catalogue size and streaming usage, connecting the 83.4 percent who don’t always know what they want to watch should be fairly straightforward, especially as companies increasingly use customer data to create personalized experiences that foster long-term engagement and loyalty. Audiences, however, aren’t finding what they’re looking for, and content discovery challenges are mounting,” the report reads.

Forty-seven percent of participants said they spend more time looking for something to watch than they did a year ago. The average amount of time spent is 12 minutes.

In recent years, numerous streaming services have paid large sums to air live events, including sports, to grow their advertising businesses. However, “nearly” 52 percent of streaming users that Gracenote surveyed said they “frequently don’t know which channel or service a specific sports event is on.”

“Sports events that are behind subscription paywalls are often hidden from traditional search, which inhibits discovery,” the report notes.

And in an example of streaming users yearning for experiences most closely connected to traditional, broadcast television, Gracenote reported that almost 35 percent of respondents spend more time watching free ad-supported streaming television services, such as Tubi or The Roku Channel, because subscription-based alternatives, such as Netflix and Disney+, have “become overwhelming.”

The on-demand nature of streaming services has degraded the need for live/virtual and print-out TV listings, such as a TV Guide. But challenges in finding content on streaming services are apparently leading many streaming viewers to yearn for that classic form of guidance.

In Gracenote’s survey, 72.3 percent of respondents said they want “a consolidated guide to the programming available across all of the streaming services that they use.”

Streaming service providers and analysts I’ve spoken with have pointed to content discovery as a streaming challenge for years. Some, like Netflix and Amazon Prime Video, have turned to AI to help people search for content by using casual terms or to analyze viewing habits to make new recommendations. Others, such as Tubi, have turned to user-generated content playlists and recommendations.

However, there is apparently still a deficit when it comes to good and accessible streaming recommendations, as 31 percent of survey respondents said they have yet to find a “good source of recommendations for their personal taste and preferences.”

Despite there being hundreds of streaming services—as analysis this month from research firm Parks and Associates this month states—many would agree with Springsteen that it still feels like there’s “nothin’ to watch.”

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#274. Jensen Huang is out as NVIDIA CEO

He has held the position since the company's founding.

On Thursday, NVIDIA's board of directors said that Huang had resigned his position as president and chief executive of the company, effective immediately. Huang also gave up his board seat. NVIDIA named Colette Kress, the company's current executive vice president and chief financial officer, as interim CEO.

"Kress will continue to serve as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer while serving as Interim Chief Executive Officer until a permanent successor has been named," NVIDIA said. "The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates."

As part of the shakeup, NVIDIA also appointed its first-ever chair of the board in Harvey Jones, who previously served as CEO and executive chairman of electronic design automation company Synopsys. "Colette has helped lead NVIDIA through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position," Jones said in a statement. "She is supported by an experienced leadership team that has been instrumental in executing NVIDIA's strategy and advancing its technology roadmap. The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption."

Huang's departure comes days after NVIDIA's board said it would investigate Huang after he admitted to having an affair with a former tech executive accused of being a Chinese intelligence asset. The board said at the time it had "no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised." The FBI is also investigating, according to President Donald Trump.

The New York Times later reported that Huang had also had an affair with an NVIDIA executive who reported to him, and that the two were seen "being affectionate" at a hotel during a company conference this year. A bystander who reportedly recorded the interaction was offered a nondisclosure agreement by Huang's security detail, according to the report.

Huang's downfall is a stunning turn for the company, which has become a linchpin for the AI industry. He has been CEO since co-founding the company in 1993. Since then, he has transformed NVIDIA from a 3D graphics company to an AI powerhouse worth more than $4 trillion.

As NVIDIA's stock price has exploded in recent years Huang has become an increasingly public face of the AI boom. He often appeared at events — usually wearing his signature black leather jacket — with fellow tech CEOs like Mark Zuckerberg and Satya Nadella. At a time when high profile AI researchers are raising the alarm about superintelligence, Huang has had a much more positive view on the technology. He argued against increased regulation of the industry and has said America should not restrict China's access to US-made chips.

Huang has frequently been seen with President Trump, and was one of several tech leaders to accompany him on a recent trip to China. Asked about recent revelations, the president said he believed Huang was "an honorable guy" and that he "probably has an excuse for it."

In a memo to NVIDIA employees earlier this week in which he apologized for his actions, Huang wrote that leading the company was "the privilege of my life" and that he was "committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security." He added that he was "fully committed to NVIDIA" and would "work to earn back your confidence." But the CEO who once seemed untouchable won't get the chance to do so.

#272. Databricks’ new AI agent tells CEO Ali Ghodsi the unvarnished truth

Databricks on Wednesday unveiled an updated version of its AI agent Genie, which can now perform multistep jobs that take extended work to finish. It does this through an ontology that Databricks built to give Genie the context it needs from the information that customers store on the Databricks platform.

Corporate leaders can now assign the agent a wide range of tasks, and it will complete them unsupervised, the company says. To prove the point, Databricks CEO Ali Ghodsi showed me how he uses it internally. Instead of a product demo, he fired up his actual agent and walked me through his most recent chats and charts.

He showed me how it analyzed the parts of an employee satisfaction survey where the company didn’t score well, and how it helped him rate (and discard) fixes for those low scores that some executives suggested. He showed me how it summarized a big sales pitch to a prospect that went poorly — including the features the prospect said it needed before buying.

It also prepped him for a podcast interview by reviewing his previous appearances and developing likely questions. While we talked, he even had it tell him what he should say to me. (And its advice on that wasn’t bad.)

The fascinating part was that Ghodsi was using Genie to find the stuff that he’s now unlikely to hear as the big boss. It was a source of unvarnished truth about what wasn’t going well that he needed to address.

“Have you heard of Dunbar’s number? The magic number is 150,” he said when I pointed this out. Named for British anthropologist Robin Dunbar, it’s theoretically the maximum number of people with whom humans can maintain stable relationships. In a small startup, a CEO knows everyone, has direct relationships with them, and they feel safe to tell him negative info straight up.

With 12,000 employees, Ghodsi is the first to admit he can’t know everyone. At that size, a salesperson isn’t likely to confess a disastrous sales call to the CEO. An HR manager isn’t likely to confess that their idea for increasing employee satisfaction will probably fail.

“Anyone who has more than 150 employees, which is a lot of companies, a lot of people can get value out of this,” he said of AI agents generally and his product, Genie, specifically.

Ghodsi’s own use of Genie (often called dogfooding, from the phrase “eating your own dogfood”) is one reason why Databricks is now pitching it as an always-on agent for business leaders. He says he also uses it in his personal life, rather than a security-questionable alternative like OpenClaw or Instinct or Muse.

He believes Genie can do for the business side what AI, agents, and harnesses (the software that wraps around an AI model and lets it use tools) are doing for programmers. As for the risk that agents will ratchet up corporate AI costs, Ghodsi has an answer for that, too. He said costs can be controlled through Databricks’ Unity Gateway product. It allows companies to set usage limits and to swap in less expensive open-weight models.

#271. What OPEC's surprise oil cut means for gas prices

OPEC and its allies’ surprise move to slash oil production will soon be felt at US gas pumps.

The group known as OPEC+ announced Sunday it would cut oil production by more than 1.6 million barrels a day starting in May, running through the end of the year. The news sent both Brent crude futures, the global oil benchmark, and WTI, the US benchmark, up about 6% in trading Monday.

The production cut announcement also had an immediate impact on gasoline futures, which will be passed onto US drivers much more quickly than the spike in oil prices. RBOB, the most closely watched wholesale gasoline price, was up about 8 cents a gallon, or about 3%, in morning trading.

“I think OPEC is reawakening the inflation monster,” said Tom Kloza, global head of energy analysis for OPIS, which tracks gas prices for AAA. “The White House has to be shocked and major-time pissed. It certainly alters the calculus for a while.”

The national average for US gas prices stood at $3.51, on Monday, according to AAA. Kloza said he could see it getting up to $3.80 to $3.90 in relatively short order thanks to the move by OPEC.

“We’re not going to get back to $5 a gallon. I don’t think we’re even going as high as $4,” he said. But he said by the end of the summer US drivers could be back above year-earlier prices, especially if there is a hurricane or other storms affecting production along the Gulf Coast.

The average US regular gas price a year ago stood at $4.19 a gallon in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and the disruption that caused to world’s energy markets. Prices eventually reached a record $5.02 a gallon on June 14, before starting a slow but steady decline over the course of more than three months during which the average price fell every day. The decline was partly driven by the release of oil from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve, and partly by concerns that there could be a US or global recession that reduced the demand for gasoline.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Even at $3.51, US gas prices were just below the $3.53 average on Feb. 23, 2022, the day before Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Kloza said one thing keeping prices from getting anywhere near the record levels of 2022 is that the US plans additional releases from the SPR, and US oil production and refining capacity are both up. But a cut of 1 million barrels a day of oil by OPEC+ will not be easy to make up.

“They have ability to cut production and they seem motivated to do so,” he said.

#270. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has resigned

The FBI is investigating as Nvidia’s board holds urgent talks over Huang’s future.

Originally published November 12, 2026

Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang has resigned following revelations that he had two extramarital affairs, plunging the chipmaker at the heart of the global AI boom into crisis.

The 63-year-old will be replaced by chief financial officer Colette Kress, who will serve as interim chief executive.

His exit comes after he admitted to having a relationship with a woman that US authorities believe was a Chinese spy. It also emerged on Wednesday that Huang had an affair with a senior Nvidia executive.

Nvidia shares were down more than 10 percent in pre-market trading, extending earlier losses after the announcement of Huang’s resignation.

Huang, who cofounded Nvidia in 1993, led it to become what was until recently the world’s most valuable company.

Huang’s departure marks a startling end to his more than 33-year tenure, during which Nvidia evolved from a small video-game chip company into the linchpin of the global AI infrastructure boom.

Huang has become one of the most recognizable figures in tech, as Nvidia’s semiconductors became integral to training and running AI systems such as OpenAI’s ChatGPT and Anthropic’s Claude.

Nvidia stock extended its pre-market decline after the company announced that Jensen Huang had resigned as chief executive.

Shares were down 10.2 percent in pre-market trading, a move that would wipe off more than $400 billion in market capitalization.

#269. Siri co-founder backs Helix for its voice ID tech

For years, websites have relied on technology like CAPTCHA to prove that the user on the other end is human. More recently, social media platforms have turned to photos or videos to verify users’ identities. Now, a startup called Helix is betting that voice technology can do the same job, without requiring users to hand over sensitive personal information.

The voice technology, which can run directly on a user’s device, has attracted backing from Siri co-founder Dag Kittlaus, who will also act as an adviser.

The company raised $6.2 million in funding in a round led by Chicago Ventures, with participation from BAM Ventures, Aglaé Ventures, Corazon Capital, Press On Ventures, Tusk Ventures, Yonder Ventures, Rule 30, and Brown Angel Group.

The startup was founded by Alex Oberg, Anna Dorofiyenko, and Mike Hall in 2025. Oberg previously worked at Ticketmaster, which acquired his startup; Dorofiyenko led data science teams at Live Nation and Zillow; and Hall has worked at Nike and Tinder, where he helped develop the initial matching algorithm.

At Ticketmaster, Oberg played a role in creating Verified Fan, which is designed to keep bots out of the platform. As AI advanced, he started thinking along the same lines for a new solution.

“As AI started to come up in the last two years, I was realizing that it’s going to make it increasingly difficult to determine who’s human and who’s not on the internet. Identity on the internet’s always been about proxies for identity, right? It’s your email, it’s your phone, it’s your IP address, it’s your device fingerprint, it’s your device ID,” Oberg said.

He noted that with the rise of AI agents like Instinct and Muse, the industry needs to rethink human identity on the internet and rely on biometric signals like voice, which is where Helix comes in.

Its core technology can run directly on a device to determine whether someone is human and estimate their age. The company is also running pilots to explore whether voice can be used as a biomarker to detect conditions such as drunkenness or distress.

The company can deploy its technology to replace or supplement CAPTCHA and help block bot attacks. Helix said that because its model can run locally even on an iPhone 13, the cost of deploying the technology is minimal. The startup said one of its customers is Zeel, an in-home healthcare provider that uses Helix’s tech to verify the identities of providers visiting customers’ homes.

The CEO also emphasized the limitations of Helix’s technology, noting that it cannot reliably detect whether someone is lying. He added that its voice technology doesn’t identify individual speakers — while verifying them being unique humans — and claimed that even if the company stores a voiceprint, it cannot be used to reconstruct a person’s voice.

AI-powered fraud is also a growing problem in phone calls. But Helix believes its technology isn’t yet capable of reliably detecting AI agents during live calls.

Siri co-founder Kittlaus said that voice-identification technology has finally reached a point where it works reliably, positioning Helix to scale as AI-powered fraud increases. He also said that Helix’s work to compress its models gives the startup a cost advantage.

“What folks at Helix are doing is, instead of teaching computers how to understand speech, these guys are understanding what the speech of the speaker actually means, what’s going on with them, if they are lying, are they impaired? That side of it is fascinating to me within the larger picture that I just described,” he told TechCrunch.

#268. United won’t let your AI agent book your flight

AI agents can’t attest that you don’t have explosives in your bags.﻿

AI agents can’t attest that you don’t have explosives in your bags.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The company says it hasn’t yet seen many people try to use AI agents to book their flights, The Wall Street Journal reports, in line with observations from other businesses like Doordash. But United is concerned that agents could expose customers’ information or make purchases they didn’t intend, and potentially skirt federal rules about the kind of information airlines need to share with customers — like having them attest that their bags don’t have explosives.

While agents can still show users United flights, they won’t be able to actually complete the transaction on their behalf. The decision, and others like it, could limit the potential usefulness of newer personal AI assistants like Meta’s Muse and OpenAI’s Dots. “To be honest, I don’t want to create another middleman,” United CEO Scott Kirby said at an event on Tuesday, according to the Journal.

Across sectors, businesses are grappling with whether to let AI agents transact on their sites, and United is not alone in blocking access for unauthorized agents. eBay appeared to block agents from browsing the site, and several major banks have also placed restrictions on them. United suggests it could reconsider its decision based on future advancements. “As third-party AI agents advance, we see the opportunities they present. However, at the same time, allowing them to interact directly with a United account or booking could involve sensitive actions, and we want to make sure we have established protections for travelers first.”

#267. OpenAI’s quarterly revenue reportedly crosses $10B for the first time

OpenAI is bouncing back from the doldrums it experienced earlier this year. The company’s quarterly revenue crossed $10 billion for the first time, thanks to strong demand for its recent AI models, Bloomberg reported, citing anonymous sources.

If true, that would represent an almost 50% jump from the $6.7 billion in sales the company reportedly recorded in the second quarter. That would also mark a sizable increase in its revenue growth rate, which was previously said to be about 18% between the first and second quarters of the year.

OpenAI’s profitability picture also appears to have improved, with its adjusted operating margin narrowing from -150% in the second quarter to -109% in the third quarter, the report said.

OpenAI, one of the first to commercialize AI models, wasn’t exactly struggling to continue growing its top-line expansion — 18% growth on top of $5.7 billion in quarterly revenue is massive by any measure, especially compared to most software businesses.

Still, the company lagged Anthropic in catering to enterprises, a market where the Claude maker has pulled ahead, at least in terms of revenue growth.

OpenAI has recently benefited from a combination of tighter pricing, discounts, improving models, and a greater focus on building more enterprise-focused products and AI agents that can help people complete tasks.

OpenAI has now reached an annual revenue run rate of $50 billion, according to Bloomberg. Meanwhile, Anthropic, which is seeking a $1.4 trillion valuation in an IPO likely to happen soon, said yesterday that its annual revenue run rate had increased by $10 billion within a month to $81 billion as of the end of October.

(Note that OpenAI and Anthropic calculate their annualized revenue differently: Anthropic counts sales made by its cloud partners, while OpenAI only reports its share of some sales through its partners.)

None of those figures are small, of course, but the companies are also competing with models from Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, and Google, as well as a plethora of cheaper but still highly capable models from open source AI labs, especially those from China.

#266. Scientists film deepest ever fish on seabed off Japan

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Cruising at a depth of 8,336 meters (over 27,000 feet) just above the seabed, a young snailfish has become the deepest fish ever filmed by scientists during a probe into the abyss of the northern Pacific Ocean.

Scientists from University of Western Australia and Tokyo University of Marine Science and Technology released footage of the snailfish on Sunday filmed last September by sea robots in deep trenches off Japan.

Along with the filming the deepest snailfish, the scientists physically caught two other specimens at 8,022 meters and set another record for the deepest catch.

Previously, the deepest snailfish ever spotted was at 7,703 meters in 2008, while scientists had never been able to collect fish from anywhere below 8,000 meters.

“What is significant is that it shows how far a particular type of fish will descend in the ocean,” said marine biologist Alan Jamieson, founder of the Minderoo-UWA Deep Sea Research Centre, who led the expedition.

Scientists are filming in the trenches off Japan as part of a 10-year study into the deepest fish populations in the world. Snailfish are members of Liparidae family, and while most snailfish live in shallow water, others survive at some of the greatest depths ever recorded, Jamieson said.

During the two-month survey last year, three “landers” – automatic sea robots fitted with high-resolution cameras – were dropped into three trenches – the Japan, Izu-Ogasawara and Ryukyu trenches – at varying depths.

In the Izu-Ogasawara trench, footage showed the deepest snailfish hovering calmly alongside other crustaceans on the seabed.

Jamieson classified the fish as a juvenile and said younger deep sea snailfish often stay as deep as possible to avoid being eaten by bigger predators that swim at shallower depths.

Another clip shot at between 7,500 and 8,200 meters in the same trench showed a colony of fish and crustaceans munching at bait tied to an undersea robot.

Images of the two captured snailfish – identified as Pseudoliparis belyaevi – provide a rare glimpse of the unique features that help the deep sea species survive the extreme environment.

They have tiny eyes, a translucent body, and their lack of swim bladder, which helps other fish float, works to their advantage, Jamieson said.

The professor said the Pacific Ocean is particularly conducive to vibrant activity due to its warm southern current, which encourages sea creatures to go deeper, while its abundant marine life provides a good source of food for bottom feeders.

Scientists would like to know more about creatures living at extreme depths, but cost is the constraint, Jamieson said, adding that each lander alone costs them $200,000 to assemble and operate.

“The challenges are that technology has been expensive and scientists don’t have a lot of money,” he said.
