#264. Hands on with Home Assistant’s new ‘smart home multi-tool’

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Home Assistant Connect Aux-2 helped me track my fridge temperature and finally set up my in-wall speakers.﻿

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Nabu Casa — the commercial side of the Open Home Foundation, which runs Home Assistant — just launched the Home Assistant Connect Aux-2, a multi-function box designed to help you bring more non-smart devices into your smart home. It’s part of Home Assistant’s ongoing efforts to become more user-friendly. And it’s working.

I’ve been testing the Aux-2 for a couple days and I’ve found it unsurprisingly useful and surprisingly easy to set up. The Aux-2 is a $69 box about the size of a deck of cards. It has 2.4GHz Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, Ethernet, USB-A, USB-C, a digital/analog audio jack, a Grove port for interfacing with peripherals like sensors, buttons, and LEDs, and a pair of RS-232 serial ports.

It can bridge Bluetooth devices into your Home Assistant setup. Its USB port lets you plug in a Nabu Casa Z-Wave or Zigbee antenna in case your Home Assistant hub isn’t in a good spot. You can control devices like TVs, AV receivers, solar inverters, and more via serial connector. And you can connect an amplifier or powered speakers to its audio jack to stream music to older audio equipment.

All of these things leverage Home Assistant’s capabilities as an open-source platform. The Aux-2 is based on ESPHome, an open platform for making DIY smart home devices from cheap, flexible ESP boards, and it uses existing Home Assistant/ESPHome integrations for all its features. The serial integration was developed earlier this year; Music Assistant and SendSpin, which power the audio-out feature, are in active development.

I have a Home Assistant install, but even after several years it is not my primary smart home platform simply because of friction and time constraints. Instead, I have a hodgepodge of Google Home, Apple Home, and a few devices that are only in Home Assistant. The situation is not ideal.

But the Aux-2’s capabilities perfectly intersect with two things I’d been meaning to do anyway, one of which I’m already using Home Assistant for. First, I have a garage fridge, because I live in Texas. I also have children, which means sometimes the fridge or freezer door gets left open. I want to know when that happens before all my food goes bad.

A couple years ago, I set up a few Aqara temperature sensors, connected to Home Assistant via a now-discontinued Zigbee dongle, and set up an automation to alert me if the fridge or freezer get too warm. But they frequently drop connection (probably because they’re inside a fridge in the garage), they chew through coin cell batteries, and they have no displays, which means I also have a cheap Acu-rite hygrometer in the fridge so I can see the temperature without pulling out my phone. Someone tipped me off to Govee’s Bluetooth hygrometers, but I’d need a Bluetooth proxy to get them into Home Assistant. That happens to be a feature of the Aux-2.

I also have a set of in-wall speakers in my living room that I’ve never used. I’d been meaning to get an amp, but analysis paralysis set in. Do I get a Wiim Amp? A stereo receiver? A cheap Bluetooth amp? Do I try to get them to work with my four Sonos speakers? Maybe my ancient Google Home speaker? Turns out I do nothing for five years. Maybe the Aux-2 could help.

Setting up the Aux-2 was much easier than I expected. I plugged it into my computer via USB-C and was able to download and install the latest firmware right from a browser. I could also add my Wi-Fi credentials. Then I unplugged it, moved it over to my media console and connected it to power (it takes USB-C or power over Ethernet), and did the rest of the setup in the Home Assistant interface.

In HA, I was prompted to install ESPHome, which discovered the Aux-2 and sent me to a “set up your ESPHome device page” that walked me through the capabilities. Enabling Bluetooth proxying took a couple of clicks; I added the Govee Bluetooth integration and put batteries into the sensors and they showed up automatically. Mercifully, they maintained their Bluetooth connection through three walls and a fridge, and now my fridge monitoring setup works again.

The audio capability took a little longer, but most of that was spent hooking the speakers up to the amp I ended up buying and setting up Music Assistant, which I didn’t have installed. Music Assistant lives on your Home Assistant hardware and can play from many different services — from Spotify to YouTube Music to local files and more — and to many different outputs, like Sonos, Wiim, AirPlay, Google Cast, and others, but they all need to be configured, and that took a beat. Not a long beat: less than half an hour total.

I can now play music to the in-wall speakers using Music Assistant. Sendspin, the whole-home audio standard (also developed by the Open Home Foundation) is supposed to be able to let you group speakers from different protocols under certain conditions, but I haven’t gotten that working yet. The standard is still in development, and I’ve also been busy, so I’m not sure if that’s a me problem or a them problem.

I would also like to see the Aux-2 show up as an AirPlay or Spotify Connect target, which (again) is probably just a thing I need to set up.

Serial control is the most intriguing feature of the Aux-2, and it’s unfortunately the thing I haven’t yet had time to test. But the ability to hook in things like solar inverters, TVs, and receivers is fascinating and one I want to look into further when I get the chance. Maybe I’ll hook it up to my LG TV.

The Aux-2 doesn’t do anything you couldn’t do already with an ESP32 board and various accessories, or even other ESP-based devices. The Apollo Automations Cast-1, for example, costs $35 and supports the same music-casting and Bluetooth proxy features I’m actually using from the Aux-2 at the moment. You could bodge together something even cheaper with DIY parts.

That’s not a knock against the Aux-2. That’s the whole point. The ecosystem is open by design, which I find endlessly refreshing. Both Nabu Casa and Apollo fund the Open Home Foundation, which develops Home Assistant and ESPHome (and Sendspin and Music Assistant), and both provide hardware that’s a little easier to use than DIY options. But there’s nothing stopping you from making your own devices. It’s actively encouraged.

I bought a three-pack of ESP32 boards for $16 three years ago. I could have built a Bluetooth proxy from one of those. I could have built an LED controller. I could have built a soil sensor, or a media player, or any of a dozen other things. I didn’t do anything at all.

But now that I’ve used the Aux-2 and dipped my toe into the ESPHome waters, maybe I will.

#263. Is Jensen Huang’s bad behavior more commonplace than it seems?

The AI industry is still reeling from Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s honeypot scandal, but some in the industry see it as an issue that’s much larger than one man.

The Chinese government has been active in economic espionage for decades, and with semiconductors at the center of the AI boom, there’s intense pressure to infiltrate any company involved. At the same time, many of the executives getting rich off that boom have made themselves easy targets.

As CEO of investment intel firm Heron Intelligence, Nate Singer has a unique view into the problem. His firm works on corporate due diligence: Essentially, making sure companies know the relevant dirt before making a major acquisition or executive hire. And while the Nvidia case is particularly severe, Singer says there are lots of similar cases in the semiconductor industry that have stayed under the radar.

“We’ve got former employees talking about behavior at senior executive levels,” Singer told TechCrunch, saying executives are “really crossing lines that should not be crossed.”

In a widely circulated LinkedIn post, Singer detailed one case in which a CEO’s wife made an unexpected board room appearance, “screaming about her husband and another company exec flying around in the corporate jet with a couple young ladies (who also turned out to be state-sponsored actors).”

It’s an alarming story, but an unfortunately common one. Singer says he’s seen at least three reports of similar cases, and suspects many more have gone unreported.

“There’s a lot more than this one woman,” Singer said. “They are embedded in these companies.”

Semiconductor companies are already big targets for international trade secret theft. There have been dozens of prosecutions for similar cases in recent years, including a case involving the fabrication powerhouse TSMC in 2007.

In 2017, four executives at an unnamed “Bay Area semiconductor equipment manufacturer” were charged with stealing trade secrets for use at a competing company in China. According to the indictment, the technology in question “related to the high-volume manufacturing of semiconductor wafers to be used in lighting and electronic devices, such as flat screen televisions and smart phones.“

Any sexual elements in such cases rarely become public, in part because of the severe consequences for executives and their companies. But according to Singer, the ongoing threat has become a kind of open secret in the industry.

“There are a lot of senior people in the industry that know this is happening, and are effectively condoning it,” he told TechCrunch.

#258. What pet cemeteries reveal about being human

Editor’s Note:  Eric Tourigny is a lecturer in historical archaeology at Newcastle University, UK. His research interprets osteological and material culture remains alongside historic texts to examine changing human-animal relationships in Europe and North America over the past 500 years. The views expressed in this commentary are his own. Read more opinion on CNN.

Why do we bury our departed loved ones in a cemetery? A primary purpose is to provide survivors with an opportunity to grieve and gain a sense of closure. The cemetery is a place for the living as much as it is a place for the dead.

But what about when the dearly departed are not human – but our pets?

The Spanish city of Barcelona recently announced that it will be investing in the establishment of the country’s first public pet cemetery. Set to open next year, it will offer both burials and cremations – with an estimated 7,000 carried out each year.

For me, as someone who has spent years researching the development of pet cemeteries elsewhere in the world, this news came as a shock. Barcelona is a densely populated city with limited privately owned land – one where 50% of families own a pet.

How did a city that is home to 180,000 dogs not already have a public pet cemetery? Until now, the service was only provided by the private sector, according to Barcelona’s Councilor for Climate Emergency and Ecological Transition, Eloi Badia. He added that the municipal-funded initiative was sparked by “constant public demand.”

After all, public pet cemeteries have been around in Europe and the Americas since the late 19th century. Britain’s first public pet cemetery appeared in London’s Hyde Park in 1881. New York’s Hartsdale pet cemetery was founded in 1896, followed a few years later by Paris’ ornate Cimetière des Chiens in 1899.

I became interested in the history of modern pet burial practices while investigating the archaeological record of a centuries-old house in Toronto. I came across a (very) large dog buried in the back garden which, according to the historical record, was occupied between 1840 and 1870.

This dog survived to an elderly age but, sadly, suffered from degenerative joint disease and severe infections during his last months. His ailments progressed to such a state that suggest he received some level of care in his final weeks. He then was buried in a personal plot behind the family home.

This elderly dog led me to think about the different ways people interact with the bodies of their pets after death. Could this behavior be reflective of the relationships they held with their animals in life? In this instance, why take the time to carefully bury a dog in its own space when other, arguably easier options existed?

This, after all, was an era when people often disposed of their dead pets in the river, or might have sold their bodies for meat and skin.

Good hygiene is an obvious reason to choose a burial – no one wants decomposing animal bodies in the street or their garden – but that wouldn’t immediately warrant a personalized, dedicated burial and gravestone.

The most straight forward option would be to dispose of a deceased animal with the household waste. But such treatment would evidently feel less ceremonious and would not offer appropriate emotional closure to what was likely an important relationship.

Like the burial of people, the burial of pets is an intimate cultural practice, one that changes over time and is reflective of the changing relationships that a society has with its cherished creatures.

My study of historic gravestones and epitaphs in Britain from the Victorian period to today shows this changing human-animal relationship. In the 19th century, gravestones were often dedicated to a “loving friend” or “devoted companion,” suggesting pets were mostly considered important friends.

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

Society’s changing attitude towards the role of animals in the afterlife can also be found. Fast-forward a few decades later, and gravestones were more likely to reference a reunion then earlier ones. For example, the owners of Denny, the “brave little cat” buried in an east London cemetery in 1952, wrote on his epitaph “God bless until we meet again.”

I wonder what the epitaphs in Barcelona’s new cemetery will reveal about modern Catalan relationships with animals.

Over time, our ways of treating our animal dead appear to reflect an even closer relationship in life. Once strictly forbidden by law, the last decade saw many jurisdictions, like the state of New York, allowing the co-burial of cremated animals and people, which will undoubtedly lead to changing funerary and commemoration practices for both humans and animals.

For me, the most remarkable similarity between modern and historic pet cemeteries is the striking evidence for the heartbreak and taboos around grieving for animals.

The connection that someone has with their pet can be just as strong and just as significant as their relationships with other humans. Yet today, as in over 100 years ago, individuals continue to struggle to find the appropriate outlet to express their pain, hiding heartache for fear of the social repercussions that might come with public acknowledgment of the existence of such a bond.

The RSPCA reassures the public on its website that they should not feel shame for their grief. In the UK, charities like the Blue Cross and Rainbow Bridge Pet Loss Grief Center offer counseling to bereaved humans.

Close relationships between people and animals have existed for millennia, but in western European cultures, there were few acceptable ways to mourn that relationship. As society becomes more accepting of the importance of human-animal relationships to our collective wellbeing, it is not surprising to see us follow rituals resembling those we use to mourn the loss of our closest human relationships.

At an estimated cost of €200 ($217) per service at the new Barcelona cemetery, it is important to acknowledge that this opportunity to grieve will not be financially available to everyone in the city.

This will not be a space for all of the city’s pets. Pet owners may opt to keep cremated remains within their home or spread out ashes in a meaningful location instead. Online forums and digital pet cemeteries also provide other opportunities to commemorate the relationship and express grief.

Whether one chooses a pet cemetery or not, there are many acceptable ways to express your grief – and to remember your relationship with the important animals in your life.

When (or if) Ring’s security camera drone flies into our homes in January, you won’t get the option to pilot it yourself. The Ring Drone Cam relies entirely on an “AI Pilot” system to follow pre-programmed routes and navigate autonomously around obstacles, with no manual control option available — and an embarrassing demonstration with CBS News might reveal why.

The video, broadcast on CBS Mornings and published on Threads, shows Ring founder Jamie Siminoff allowing technology reporter Jo Ling Kent to manually fly an early version of the drone via a mobile app with integrated controls.

Siminoff: “Yeah, so we can load manual controls.”

The demo then shows the drone crashing twice while being manually piloted. In the first instance, Ring reportedly told CBS News that the drone’s collision avoidance technology malfunctioned, and declined to share the video recorded by the drone prior to crashing. CBS News caught the second crash on camera, with the footage showing the drone colliding with a wall after failing to respond to manual controls. According to Siminoff, this occurred because the drone lost internet connectivity — which it also relies on to fly autonomously.

“If it loses connectivity, it’s supposed to stop and land,” Siminoff said in the video. “It did not there.”

Ring declined to show us the app in our own demonstration of the Drone Cam, and said it only works on pre-programmed flight paths. It seems that while manual flight controls were included at some stage of development, Ring won’t be making the feature available to customers when the Drone Cam launches in January.

“We explored a lot of features throughout the development, but autonomous flight to patrol points using the AI Pilot is the best customer benefit and experience,” Ring spokesperson Sam McGee said in a statement to The Verge. Ring didn’t respond to our question regarding whether the decision to not include manual controls was made following the CBS News demo.

#254. With $720M round, Britain’s Fractile gets a leg up in the race for AI chips

Fractile, a British startup developing AI chips, has raised a $720 million Series C backed by Britain’s Sovereign AI Fund with hopes of “ensuring the U.K. has a greater stake in the hardware that will underpin its future economy,” its government stated.

Alongside the likes of Axelera, Etched, and OLIX, this makes Fractile one of the top-funded startup players in the race for purpose-built AI chips. Such chips are one of several avenues being explored to reduce the cost of inference — aka using increasingly demanding AI models in production.

However, Fractile seeks to go beyond hardware with a full-stack approach that also includes a machine learning compiler to maximize the speed at which LLMs will get executed on its chips.

As a result, the company frames itself as a frontier AI company, and its latest round led by Thrive Capital, Founders Fund, Lightspeed Venture Partners, and Redpoint was priced accordingly. According to the British government, the valuation was “over £5 billion” — quite possibly $7 billion, a figure reported both by the Financial Times and a Reuters source.

Still, Fractile’s main differentiator will arguably be its hardware design. After a pivot, its approach now revolves on DRAM, the most common type of RAM, but stacked on top of each chip in such a way that it is hoping will bypass the usual trade-off between memory and capacity.

This trade-off has become more problematic with the rise of agentic AI, as companies seek to perform longer chains of tasks with large models without compromising on speed. As compute struggles to keep up, hopes are high for AI chips, despite their limited availability to date.

In a LinkedIn post, Fractile CEO Walter Goodwin wrote that “there is still a lot of work ahead” for the company, and that the new funding will enable the startup to “ship [its] first chips and systems next year.”

#253. CEOs are tired of being held responsible for gun regulation

Americans have grown used to corporate executives treading the well-worn paths of the Northeast corridor to convene alongside elected officials in Washington, DC, and discuss geopolitics, policy and all that’s in-between.

In 2017, major CEOs from across the country came together to oppose North Carolina’s transgender bathroom law. In 2019, they called abortion bans “bad for business.”

After the deadly attack on the US Capitol on January 6, 2021, many of corporate America’s biggest names denounced the rioters and pledged to halt their political giving.

Recently, more than 1,000 companies promised to voluntarily curtail their operations in Russia in protest of Moscow’s war on Ukraine.

Dick’s Sporting Goods stopped selling semi-automatic, assault-style rifles at stores and Citigroup put new restrictions on gun sales by business customers after the mass shooting at a high school in Parkland, Florida, in 2018.

A year later, after mass shootings at a Walmart in El Paso, Texas, and a nightclub in Dayton, Ohio, Walmart ended handgun ammunition sales.

Corporate leadership has long been vocal on the issue of gun control – in 2019 and again this past summer nearly 150 major companies – including Lululemon, Lyft, Bain Capital, Bloomberg LP, Permanente Medical Group and Unilever – called gun violence a “public health crisis” and demanded that the US Senate pass legislation to address it.

That’s why corporate America’s silence in the wake of the latest mass shooting at a school in Nashville is so jarring. The United States has come to rely on the increasing power of large corporations as political advocates.

But Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, a vocal advocate of corporate social responsibility who has a direct line to major CEOs around the globe, said that top executives are forlorn. Their previous efforts haven’t done much to push the needle on gun control legislation and without more backing, they don’t know what else they can do at the moment, he said.

Before the Bell spoke with Sonnenfeld, who runs Yale School of Management’s Chief Executive Leadership Institute, a nonprofit educational and research institute focused on CEO leadership and corporate governance.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Before the Bell: CEOs have been quiet about gun reform since the latest mass school shooting in Nashville, have you heard anything about plans to speak out?

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld: Where is everybody else? Where is all of civil society? CEOs are just one group of people and it’s like we’re turning to them to be our saviors on every topic. They’ve joined causes with valor and nobility but they can’t just be taking cause after cause as if there’s nobody else in society. The social change that happened in the 1960s wasn’t being led primarily by CEOs. Social changes really happened when we saw the interfaith activity of clergy locking arms and canvassing legislators. We saw campuses alive and aroused. Where’s all the student activism?

The CEOs are still the most active even if they’re less active than they were six months ago. They’re not there as hired hands of shareholders to fill the role of politicians and civic leaders. They’re there to join that chorus, but they don’t want to be the only one singing.

So is this what you’re hearing from top CEOs? Have they gotten tired of advocating?

I just got off of a CEO call on voting rights and this morning we had a forum on sustainability – CEOs are still the most active on these fronts. It’s the same thing on immigration reform. If a CEO was working an 18 hour day on a 12 day week, they still couldn’t address all of the issues that need addressing.

The nation’s CEOs are waiting for everybody else to join them. They don’t need to restate something they’ve already stated. They’ve jumped in the pool, where’s everybody else?

So what do you think has led to this complacency amongst Americans and the growing reliance on CEOs to advocate on our behalf?

They’ve taken a very strong stance and they’ve gone out further than the general public. They are where the general public is on surveys, but they’re not where the general public is on action in the streets. So we’re ready for others to now do something. Enough already on saying ‘what are the CEOs doing?’ Social capital is as valuable as financial capital. CEOs understand that in their soul, they want there to be social capital. They want there to be public trust, but they need the rest of civil society to join them. And that’s their frustration.

But don’t these CEOs hold the purse strings in terms of donating to powerful politicians?

You would think that, but since the 2020 elections much less of campaign contributions have come from big business. Since the 2021 run on the Capitol, a lot of businesses either had an official moratorium or they’ve given mere pennies to politicians. The common impression on the street that CEOs are controlling campaign purses strings is 100% wrong.

Tesla reported. a modest 4% rise in sales in the first quarter compared to the final three months of last year, despite a series of price cuts on its lower priced vehicles and talk by CEO Elon Musk about strong demand at those lower prices.

The first quarter also marked the fourth straight quarter that Tesla has produced more vehicles than it has delivered to customers. Some of that may be due to the ramp up in production at two new factories, one in Texas, the other in Germany, which opened last spring, and a lag between that increased production and sales.

Tesla said there was an increase in the number of its more expensive models, the Model S and Model X, in transit to Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as to the Asia Pacific region.

But it does mean that over the last 12 months Tesla has produced 78,000 more cars than it has sold, suggesting that talk of strong demand by Tesla executives may not be backed up by the numbers.

“Early this year, we had a price adjustment. After that, we actually generated a huge demand, more than we can produce, really,” said Tom Zhu, Tesla’s executive in charge of global production and sales. “And as Elon said, as long as you offer a product with value at affordable price, you don’t have to worry about demand.”

#251. CoreWeave turns to customer prepayments, shorter contracts to fund AI growth

CoreWeave is rewriting how it pays for the AI boom, according to the company’s third-quarter earnings, reported on Tuesday. CoreWeave, a neocloud that rents out the computing power behind AI, said it’s increasingly asking customers to pay up front, borrowing against specific contracts, and even pursuing short-term deals that could lead to higher prices.

The new strategies could help CoreWeave finance its growth more efficiently while squeezing more cash from existing infrastructure. But these bets only hold if today’s demand and pricing power continue.

CoreWeave reported $3.66 billion in revenue, up 168% from the year prior, but it also posted a $709 million net loss and $846 million in quarterly interest expense.

“We will not use the unsecured debt market to fund our business at current yields, nor do we need to,” CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator said during Tuesday’s earnings call.

Instead, CoreWeave’s plan is to rely on loans backed by specific customer contracts, drawing on funds as needed to finance most of the infrastructure it still has to build to deliver on its significant backlog of $132.5 billion.

CoreWeave also said it expects about $9 billion in customer prepayments and deposits over the next three months, including more than $3 billion tied to signing new customer contracts for capacity it has already built, which requires no additional spending on hardware.

Rivals Nebius and IREN have also relied on hefty Microsoft prepayments to pay for computing capacity, signaling that the industry is moving toward asking Big Tech to pay up for their buildouts.

Upfront payments could alleviate CoreWeave’s immediate borrowing needs, but they also commit the company to providing services at a price that might be less attractive down the road if compute demand continues to surge.

CoreWeave is also looking to capitalize on that growing demand by signing more shorter-term contracts that can command much higher prices.

The company raised prices across its offerings by about 25% in July and by another 10% later in the quarter. Prices are still climbing this quarter, in a way that “more than offset[s] cost increases,” said Intrator.

“We are also allocating more capacity to short-term opportunities, where the pricing justifies it, selling capacity at more than $40 million per megawatt annually, while pricing for new infrastructure on four- and five-year contracts is moving to $20 million to $25 million per megawatt,” the chief executive said.

CoreWeave’s financing strategy now revolves around those opportunities. In August, the company closed a $2.6 billion loan, its first designed specifically for shorter-term customer contracts. Known as DDTL 5.5, it lets CoreWeave borrow in installments as it needs the money.

While short-term deals let CoreWeave charge higher prices and change them more frequently, it’s a trade-off. Lenders give more weight to longer-term contracts, even if they’re priced lower, because they value predictable cash flow and consistency to repay the loan above all.

Even though Wall Street reacted positively to CoreWeave’s earnings, bond investors are much less sanguine. Last month, yields on some of the company’s bonds hit above 15%, highlighting that investors are concerned about the debt needed to fund data center buildouts before those investments see a return.

In short, CoreWeave is finding new ways to expand without relying on unsecured debt. The strategy it has chosen could boost profits, but it also carries greater risk around contract renewals and pricing — and it just goes to show how dependent the AI infrastructure boom is on sustained, and as yet unproven, demand.

TechCrunch has reached out to CoreWeave for more information about its loan facility.

#250. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns amid spy, affair scandals

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After reports of Huang’s two extramarital affairs with an alleged spy and an Nvidia executive, he’s out, and Nvidia CFO Colette Kress is in.

After reports of Huang’s two extramarital affairs with an alleged spy and an Nvidia executive, he’s out, and Nvidia CFO Colette Kress is in.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Nvidia’s board announced that Jensen Huang — its longtime co-founder, president, and CEO — has resigned after reports of two extramarital affairs, one with an alleged Chinese spy and one with an Nvidia executive who reported to Huang. Following a late Wednesday night meeting, the board accepted Huang’s letter of resignation as CEO, president, and board member, effective immediately, with current CFO Colette Kress appointed interim CEO. Kress has been with Nvidia since 2013, with previous stops at Cisco and Microsoft.

Nvidia is also changing its corporate structure in Huang’s absence, appointing former Synopsys CEO Harvey C. Jones as the first board chair in its history. The statement does not include a statement from Huang, while Kress is quoted as saying:

NVIDIA’s strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years. We will continue advancing the work that has made NVIDIA a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders.

Now Nvidia says, “The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates,” and reports about who that might be have already surfaced, putting Kress, Apple hardware chief Johny Srouji, Nvidia VP Ian Buck, AMD CEO (and cousin of Jensen Huang) Lisa Su, and Arm CEO Rene Haas at the top of the list.

Huang had been at the helm of what’s now known as the most powerful company in AI since its founding in 1993. In the years since, it’s become the main purveyor of the chips fueling AI advancement and deployment in the US, and it has enjoyed near-unbridled power and influence — demand for Nvidia’s chips constantly outpaces supply, and AI CEOs are constantly putting in requests to buy more, sometimes to no avail. Nvidia’s investment portfolio — worth about $99 billion and spanning AI companies from frontier labs to neoclouds — has also granted it a lot of sway in the AI space. And in recent years, Huang himself has enjoyed a close relationship with President Donald Trump.

Over the past week, that influence has been a double-edged sword for Huang, after news broke that he had been involved in a romantic relationship with Alice Huang (no relation), an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based cloud provider and big customer of Nvidia itself. The Megaspeed executive was also allegedly a surveillance operative passing information along to China’s Ministry of State Security. She was focused on gaining information about advanced AI chips in the US and trying to influence the technology being made available to China, The New York Times reported. Huang admitted he had been “unfaithful” to his wife and apologized to his family and shareholders in a memo to Nvidia employees over the weekend.

As The Verge wrote earlier this week, “questions are swirling around how the affair may have influenced Huang’s reported push to sell advanced chips to China — and potentially even influenced President Trump’s own decisions surrounding AI export controls.”

As of Monday, analysts, venture capitalists and even Trump had expected Huang would come out the other side of the scandal with his chief executive status intact. Silicon Valley insiders, including Elon Musk, had backed him despite the news. But on Wednesday, news broke that Huang had had a second extramarital affair — this time with an executive reporting directly to him. The New York Times, citing five people who worked at Nvidia, identified the relationship as being with Nvidia’s VP of healthcare, Kimberly Powell. Soon after the news broke, The Wall Street Journal reported, citing people familiar with the matter, that Nvidia’s board was meeting as soon as Wednesday evening in order to decide Huang’s future at the company and potentially oust him from his role.

#248. Olaf Sakkers is betting he can beat the emerging-manager squeeze

Entering into the world of venture capital has never been easy, especially as a first-time fund manager. But it’s especially daunting right now.

According to PitchBook data, firms raising their fourth fund “or later” captured a whopping 88.2% of U.S. VC fundraising dollars through the first three quarters of this year, while first-time funds captured a measly 4.5%. Just 81 first-time funds closed in the first nine months of 2026. That puts the year on track to be the worst for first-time managers in at least a decade, according to PitchBook’s estimate.

Olaf Sakkers has spent the past five years proving that difficult doesn’t mean impossible. He founded his firm RedBlue Capital in 2021 and closed its first fund with roughly $5 million in capital commitments in 2023. He has now held a first close on a second fund that he says will total at least $50 million.

Raised in South Africa, he studied anthropology at Princeton, focusing on global health and health policy, and wrote his thesis on HIV in his home country. He moved to Israel after graduating and was drafted into the Israel Defense Forces, where he says he had a desk job liaising with the U.S. military, and where he became interested in autonomous vehicles.

An introduction led to Michael Granoff, founder of the venture firm Maniv Mobility and an early backer of electric-vehicle startup Better Place. Granoff, who had money to invest, hired him on the spot, Sakkers says. Neither had been a venture investor at that point, so they built a fund “almost by accident,” learning as they went. Sakkers says he ran the investment process day to day, backing Israeli founders who were bringing cybersecurity skills into the automotive world, and later expanding to the U.S. and other markets.

By 2021, Sakkers says, he was an equal partner in Maniv’s economics, but he wanted something different — more formal and scalable — so struck out on his own. Today, RedBlue backs what he calls the “real economy,” including robotics, logistics, manufacturing and energy.

Getting to this point has been a test of endurance as much as salesmanship, seemingly. Sakkers says that institutional limited partners, outfits like endowments and pension funds, have been the toughest to win over because his checks are small (and theirs are comparatively huge) but also because their risk model is built to “avoid getting a zero,” which is why they tend to gravitate toward established brands.

Says Sakkers, it’s akin to founders who come to be seen as “inevitable,” after they’ve previously founded another company. Meanwhile first-time founders often battle against the “gravity of perception,” meaning a more dominant brand can bend how people perceive a newer one.

His answer has been to let the results speak, but it takes time. “It’s a long-term game of building trust, demonstrating performance over time,” Sakkers says.

The first fund was a proof of concept, backed by LPs he had known for a while, including family offices. The second draws on family offices and institutions.

RedBlue writes small checks, $50,000 to $100,000, mostly joining deals led by other investors rather than leading them. That lets it do more deals and be “the last investor that they take” on by promising practical help, including introductions to lead investors like Andreessen Horowitz and Thrive Capital, and to customers. Founders expect little from VCs, in Sakker’s view, so RedBlue tries to overdeliver on the support part.

By his account, the results are strong. Of 28 investments, he says that 15 have been marked up, and he cites a net internal rate of return of “significantly above 50%.” There has been just one exit — an acqui-hire — so most gains from the tiny fund remain on paper and unrealized.

Public records support part of Sakker’s story. HappyRobot, which RedBlue backed early, raised a $150 million Series C at a $1.2 billion valuation in August. Westmag, which makes motors for drones and robots and which Sakkers backed at pre-seed alongside Nat Friedman, emerged in June with an $11 million seed led by Andreessen Horowitz. Flai, an AI startup for car dealerships, followed its 2025 seed with a $27 million Series A. And Humble Robotics, a driverless-truck startup in whose $24 million seed round RedBlue participated, announced a $51 million Series A led by Obvious Ventures earlier this month.

Sakkers argues that discipline is his edge. RedBlue publishes thesis essays, such as one on robotics, and runs on a custom database that he built with Claude Code to log relationships, meeting notes and introductions.

That lets him and his lone full-time principal, based in New York, work asynchronously. AI agents, he says, are “useless if they just have to guess,” so everything gets written down. He contrasts that with Benchmark’s more artisanal model, which he says suits a firm with a record he lacks. Long exit timelines for physical-world companies don’t worry him, either: even a $50 million to $100 million fund, he says, can sell stakes on the secondary market.

The data isn’t uniformly grim. Smaller venture capital funds between $1 million and $10 million consistently outperform larger funds of $100 million or more at the top end of net TVPI (total value to paid-in capital, which compares a fund’s total value to the money invested in it) across recent vintages tracked by the software outfit Carta.

In the meantime, Sakkers is out there knocking on doors, making introductions between disparate parties, and calling up reporters like this one to tell his story. It’s not easy to make a mark in a world where the spoils accrue to the few, but he says he never seriously considered a safer job. “Life is short, and regrets last forever,” he says.

#246. Apple’s HomeView is my new favorite smart display — but not my daughter’s

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Apple’s first smart display is a gorgeous, speedy smart home hub and a great kitchen companion. It’s when it tries to be the family iPhone that things get awkward.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Photography by Hunter McRae for The Verge

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The HomeView nails everything I want from a smart display. It’s superb at controlling my smart home; it’s an excellent hands-free kitchen companion, a good speaker, and a gorgeous digital photo frame. It’s also the first Apple smart speaker with Siri AI, which is an improvement, if still slow at times. But Apple has fallen into the trap that hampers other smart displays I’ve tested: not content with doing its core functions well, it tries to do more, and that’s where the friction creeps in..

HomeView is both a smart display everyone in your home can use and an extension of your iPhone

The special Apple sauce here is that HomeView is both a smart display everyone in your home can use and an extension of your iPhone or iPad, providing access to your texts, calendar, and reminders when your phone is at home and on the same Wi-Fi network. This makes it a communal device with personal data, and switching between the two is where HomeView stumbles. Clunky authentication checks and being sent to my iPhone too many times interrupt the hands-free experience. Not to mention that I could easily read my 15-year-old daughter’s text messages, and vice versa.

Her verdict? “I’ll just use my iPhone.” But my largely tech-averse husband fell in love with HomeView, and immediately asked for one for his office.

Speedy smart home controlExcellent guided cookingSiri AI is helpful in the homeGorgeous customizable facesGreat sound for its size

I could read my daughter’s text messagesSiri AI can be slowThe app / widget situation is funkyWall mounts cost (a lot) extra

For households deep in the Apple ecosystem, especially those with younger kids who only need access to lights and music and aren’t interested in your text messages, this may be the first smart display your whole family will actually use. But for some, the idea of their texts appearing on a large screen in the middle of the kitchen will send them running.

Apple is late to the game here. Amazon and Google have had smart displays for close to a decade. But the company has debuted with a product that elevates the category in nearly every way — with a few quirks.

The HomeView is much smaller than I expected, but it’s a great size for my needs. The star of the device is a 7-inch square touchscreen that displays a gorgeous collection of “faces” that I can customize to fit wherever I put it — the kitchen counter, bedside table, or the wall. It comes in tabletop and wall-mounted versions, and both cost $299, making it Apple’s least expensive screened device after the Apple Watch SE.

The tabletop HomeView fits neatly on my kitchen counter and is light enough to move around for cooking, or when my husband wants to check his schedule over breakfast. The long USB-C cable allows plenty of maneuverability, though after a few days of spinning, it did get a bit twisty.

The screen is clear and bright and looks good in most lighting situations. It’s large enough for reading messages, following a recipe, and video calling while in the kitchen. It’s super responsive, too, showing no lag or stuttering. There’s also a Center Stage camera above the display for taking a FaceTime call while moving around, staying in frame in every corner of my small kitchen. Callers could hear me clearly, although the farther away I was, the more tinny it started to sound.

Sound is very good for the size, with speakers on both the display and in the base of the tabletop model. It’s close to a HomePod Mini 2, not as room-filling, but with a touch more bass. Playing music on the wall-mounted model wasn’t as much of a downgrade as I expected in a device with one less speaker; it was richer and cleaner than on the Echo Show 15 wall-mounted in the same entryway. Siri responded well to voice commands using the device’s microphones, and as long as I remembered to put my phone face down if it was nearby, Siri replied from the correct device.

The one hardware miss is physical buttons: there aren’t any. When I want to adjust the volume quickly or mute Siri, I have to tap the display or pull out my phone.

I have a good spot on my kitchen counter for HomeView, but if you don’t, wall mounting is an option. Apple sent me two mounts for this review, both from Schneider Electric: a USB-C wall mount ($59.99) with a 2-meter / 6.5-foot cable and neat cable management, and a hardwired AC mount ($79.99). There will also be a power-over-Ethernet option for $129.99. The USB-C mount is easy to install: just two screws in the wall, then the display snaps on with magnets so strong that no one will accidentally knock it off the wall.

However, the wall-mounted HomeView ends up costing more than the tabletop version because it’s priced the same but doesn’t include a mount or a power adaptor. You have to buy those separately, and with Apple’s $39 brick, you’re starting at around $400 (until the inevitable third-party options arrive). That’s an extra $100 for a device with fewer speakers. It’s a weird move; Apple should have included a wall mount and adapter or dropped the price.

HomeView nails the smart display essentials: controlling my smart home, monitoring my video doorbell, providing hands-free help in the kitchen, playing music and podcasts, and displaying photos.

Apple put a Thread radio and Matter controller in the HomeView, so it acts as an Apple Home hub, the first with an always-on screen. The Home app is infused throughout the device’s software, giving you multiple ways to control connected smart lights, thermostats, locks, and cameras. This is where HomeView shines. It can connect to any HomeKit or compatible Matter smart device, and it’s impressively fast at controlling them, turning on the lights in my entryway as quickly as a regular light switch. It also offers tons of options for customizing its controls.

I set up the device’s Smart Home face with a large button to turn the lights on and off in the hallway it was in, plus smaller buttons for my Goodnight and Good Morning scenes, along with a live view of the Aqara video doorbell, and controls for my front door lock and Nest thermostat. In the morning, as I walk past, I hit the Good Morning scene and the house wakes up; then I do the reverse at night. Even better, my husband, who has never once opened the Home app, now turns on the lights from the HomeView every morning.

The Camera face, which streams up to nine HomeKit Secure Video cameras at once, is also very fast, and when someone rings the video doorbell, HomeView pulls up a live feed instantly. I can then unlock the door right from the display. I love that I can add multiple Smart Home and Camera faces in different configurations, giving me swipeable access to all the controls I want.

Smart home is my favorite use case, but I also loved cooking with HomeView. You can pull up a recipe on your phone and, using Handoff, send it to the HomeView, which brings up a guided cooking experience. This feature elicited several “Wow, that’s really cool” reactions from visitors (and my editor!).

It was easy to follow the on-screen steps while cooking, as well as use Siri AI for help. While viewing a Creamy Tuscan Chicken recipe, I tapped the cherry tomatoes and said, “Siri, what can I substitute for this?” It suggested some useful options and some not-so-good ones (cucumbers?). I also liked how the HomeView automatically inserted timers into steps like “Cook the chicken for about 8 minutes on each side.” Tapping the timers popped them up into Live Activities at the top of the screen, making it easy to switch back and forth between them and the recipe.

This switching with Live Activities also works for phone calls and music, two things I used HomeView for a lot. Answering a call on the large display while cooking is much easier than reaching for my iPhone, and the audio is loud and clear, so I could keep cooking while chatting from anywhere in the room.

Music and audio playback are great; it’s nice to see album art while blasting Taylor Swift, and the screen makes it easy to pause, skip, or navigate to a new song, album, or playlist. The built-in speaker is good, but I prefer to AirPlay to compatible speakers, and I like that the HomeView remembers which I used last so I don’t have to select them each time.

There are some disappointments. The HomeView has a built-in temperature and humidity sensor, like its HomePod siblings, but it can’t use its onboard 12MP camera as a motion sensor to trigger an automation. HomeView can also only control devices compatible with Apple Home, so that means no Ring doorbells or Nest cameras (without workarounds), or categories like lawnmowers or appliances that Apple Home doesn’t support. I also couldn’t get it to run some of the Siri shortcuts I use to have the voice assistant control unsupported devices. When I say “Hey Siri, feed Smokey” to any of my HomePods, they can trigger my Aqara pet feeder, but HomeView told me it couldn’t do that.

Apple Home still isn’t powerful enough for advanced home setups with complicated automations or a robust home energy management system; if that’s you, you’re probably already using Home Assistant or Homey and could bring your setup into a HomeView. Which you may want to do, because HomeView’s simple, intuitive interface is great for everyone else in your home. For example, my long-suffering spouse, who just wants a way to turn on the smart lights or adjust the thermostat without pulling out his phone, asking a smart speaker, or having me do it for him.

In practice, HomeView recognized me instantly, and I could swipe up from the home face to read my texts and access my calendar without any delay. But when it came to switching between profiles, it was too easy to access family members’ personal content. When I tested it with my teenage daughter, HomeView let me stay on her profile and scroll through her texts several times — even though it recognized me and popped up a prompt asking if I wanted to switch.

While this didn’t happen every time, it happened often enough to be a problem, mostly when I used the device right after her. There’s a grace period where the device stays on your content even after you’ve moved away; and if I hopped onto the device within five seconds or so of my daughter leaving, I could sometimes scroll for up to 10 seconds before it checked again and locked me out. (This was purely for testing purposes and with her consent!)

The other time we could reliably read each other’s content was when we were sitting side by side at the kitchen counter near the device. If my daughter was using it, walked away, and sat on the couch behind us, I could scroll through her messages for as long as I wanted because the camera still saw her in the room and didn’t switch to me, even though I was closer to it.

Swiping out of Messages to a face and leaving the room was the most reliable way to protect our stuff — but it wasn’t foolproof. (Watch my video review for more on this.)

I was impressed at how well HomeView switched between us on busy mornings. (Yes, my household does sometimes resemble that HomeView ad, minus the hole in the counter.) My daughter asked when her school project was due, then I jumped in to ask when to leave for the airport. HomeView answered each of us correctly, pulling from our respective calendars and texts — though we had to swivel the screen to face us, since it didn’t always recognize us by just voice. We could have used our phones, but HomeView’s large screen and louder speaker made hands-free use easier.

HomeView was stricter with phone calls than text messages, ringing only when I was directly in front of it. When my phone rang while I was on the other side of the counter, HomeView stayed silent until I spun it around.

This flow scuppers my hopes of using it as a family phone or putting it by my bed so I could banish my iPhone (and TikTok) to the living room at night. This is because it doesn’t show text notifications and won’t ring if it can’t see me, meaning I might miss a late-night call or text from my college-age son or teenage daughter.

Tying all of these features together is Siri AI, which can answer general knowledge questions, control smart home devices, play music for you, and tap into personal context. I also didn’t have to repeat the wake word constantly; the Siri bubble animation on the screen clearly indicates when it’s still listening, so I could keep talking. Siri AI on the HomeView is an improvement over the older Siri on the HomePods, especially for controlling my home, and it’s mercifully much less verbose than its competitors. But it can be frustratingly slow to pull up personal context answers, sometimes makes mistakes, and regularly errors out. It is still in beta, so hopefully things will improve.

For smart home requests, Siri is very fast, since those run locally. It now responds to conversational requests like “dim the lights in here,” then “make it a bit dimmer,” making it easier to control devices. After it executes a voice command, it shows the accessory on the screen for additional control, which is handy.

I could also create automations with voice, such as “Hey Siri, open the shades in my office at 8AM every morning” and chain commands like “turn on the lights in here and in the mudroom.” But these only work with one device type at a time, so I couldn’t control lights and the shades with one ask.

I found Siri AI Personal Context handy in several instances, and it’s a feature that makes HomeView stand out from the competition — neither Alexa nor Google offers anything as deep. But here Siri can be slow, because it relies on pulling from your phone, and it struggles with anything taxing, like multiple requests in a row from different people. It frequently exclaims, “Uh oh, something’s wrong,” and gives up.

Then there was the time Siri tried to send my husband in the wrong direction. He asked it about his weekend schedule, and it read out the events in his calendar at the Georgia Bushcraft Fall Gathering he was heading to. When he asked how long it would take to get there, it confidently told him one hour and 47 minutes. He then asked it to show him directions, and it couldn’t because it didn’t actually know the address. Siri had guessed the location based on something in the event that said “Dogwood Stage,” and had made up the travel time. The gathering was actually a five-hour drive away. The event’s address was in his email, but Siri didn’t connect those dots.

When he prompted it to check his email, it found the correct address and calculated the drive time, but couldn’t pull up the route because there’s no Maps app on the HomeView. He asked it to send the route to his phone, and it said: “Done.” But all it sent him was a message with the drive time, no map attached. When he made the same request on his iPhone, it worked great. “It’s like your iPhone, but not as good” isn’t a great pitch here.

Despite these hiccups, I did find myself using Siri AI on HomeView a lot more than I do on my phone, where touch is my first and easiest instinct.

Beyond the core functions of a smart display, HomeView starts to struggle.

The built-in apps and widgets, which include Music, Calendar, Weather, Timers, Safari, and Intercom, work well. But if you want to use any other apps or widgets, they have to come from your phone, with some strange restrictions. They only work when your phone is at home, and only audio and video apps that support CarPlay and iPhone widgets that are compatible with the Mac show up. Because widgets point to apps that aren’t on the HomeView, they only work for glanceable info at best — tapping most of them gets you nowhere.

This means the New York Times and Apple News apps that appeared on the device were just podcast feeds, not news apps, and the Ring widget just told me to use my iPhone. A few of the widgets I tested offered some on-screen info, and some could perform one or two pre-programmed actions — there’s potential here, but it’s frustrating on day one. The only video app that works on the HomeView is Apple TV, but the screen is letterboxed to essentially iPhone size, so I wouldn’t recommend anyone buy this for a video-watching experience.

Safari is a workaround, and it’s not a terrible experience. I could browse websites by asking Siri to pull up The Verge or The New York Times in Safari, and I could log in to Netflix and YouTube using the pop-up keyboard. While I won’t be binge-watching the last season of Killing Eve on this, a Gordon Ramsay steak tutorial on YouTube while I cook, works fine but isn’t noticeably better than an iPhone.

There’s also no Maps or Notes app — and I felt their absence. A share sheet and the ability to copy and paste from Safari are also MIA, so sharing a grocery list or texting a link meant going back to my phone. HomeView made me do that a lot, which underscores how many features of this product are an extension of my iPhone rather than a standalone device.

I use and test all three major smart home platforms — Google Home, Amazon Alexa, and Apple Home. After a week with HomeView and Siri AI, I rank Alexa Plus as the best smart home assistant, Gemini for Home as the smartest assistant, and HomeView as the best smart display hardware and personal assistant.

Apple’s 7-inch square screen fits nicely into my home, and while it has similar dimensions to the Nest Hub, it has a smaller footprint. The Nest Hub Max, which launched in 2019, is showing its age and is significantly slower and laggier than HomeView.

The newer Echo Shows 8 and 11 are closer to HomeView in capabilities, but not as snappy. The just-announced Show 16 has a new UI with similar features to HomeView, but Amazon’s penchant for sticking ads on its smart displays has rightly put off many users. The HomeView has no ads.

When it comes to the AI inside, Alexa Plus is the most capable in the smart home. It can run multi-device, multi-room commands in seconds, and you can create complex automations with voice alone. Gemini can also handle more complex multi-step requests and is better with natural language and inferring intent from vaguer statements, like “I’m cold,” which will have it turn up the thermostat.

Where HomeView wins here is Personal Context. Google and Amazon offer similar profile switching, but neither is connected to your phone the way Siri AI is to your iPhone, so they have far less personal information to draw on. (Gemini for Home could certainly access much of it, but Google hasn’t gone there yet).

If Siri AI were a human personal assistant, I’d fire her for being too slow.

Of course, this is also a drawback: you need an iPhone or iPad to have a profile on HomeView. However, guests or visitors can use the smart home controls, timers, weather, Safari, music, and Siri for general knowledge.

Siri AI is also the only one of the three you don’t have to pay for to get the full voice assistant experience. Alexa Plus costs $20 a month or comes with Prime, and Google’s more advanced voice assistant, Gemini Live, is behind a paywall. Apple has said some Apple Intelligence features “are subject to daily usage limits,” with expanded access “available for a fee” in the future, but I didn’t run into any limits during my testing. However, Alexa Plus and Gemini Live are available on older smart speakers and displays; Siri is currently gated to the newest models.

Alexa and Google also support many more smart home devices; I can’t control my Bosch coffee machine in Apple Home, and my Ring doorbell and Nest floodlight camera don’t natively work with HomeView. Which is a shame, as HomeView is the best smart display for cameras I’ve tested. Ring devices work almost as well on an Echo Show, but Nest cams are slow on a Nest Hub, and third-party cameras struggle on both. The catch is only a handful of brands work with HomeKit Secure Video — including Aqara, Eve, and some Anker Eufy and TP-Link Tapo models. Apple’s own cameras, made with LG, arrive next year.

HomeView is a very good first smart display from Apple: powerful enough to run my smart home but simple enough for anyone in it to use. If you’re an Apple household and want it for any of its core jobs, you’ll be pleased, and at $299 it’s very competitively priced. But it’s not the full-fledged family organizer some households might want; Skylight’s Calendar still owns that niche.

For Apple Home users, the only question is how many do you want?

Where it gets complicated is when it tries to be your iPhone. Siri AI and Personal Context bring something no other smart display currently offers. But potentially seeing each other’s texts will be a deal-breaker for some. Too many tasks send you back to your phone, and if Siri AI were a human personal assistant, I’d probably fire her for being too slow.

This is all fixable: the option to turn off some personal features on the HomeView, a few more functional widgets and apps, and better options for displaying shared personal content like calendars could bring HomeView closer to the family computer I’d like it to be.

Today, HomeView offers enough for its price to be a good choice for anyone with an iPhone or iPad who is intrigued by any of its core features — particularly the smart home. For households already deeply invested in the Apple ecosystem and Apple Home, the only real question is how many are you going to buy?

#244. Meta is losing its longtime head of infrastructure

A key figure in Meta’s growth into a social media and tech industry powerhouse is leaving the company.

Santosh Janardhan, the company’s longtime head of infrastructure and co-head of engineering, will be stepping down, Meta told TechCrunch. He will be replaced by Dave Brown, who joined the company as vice president of infrastructure earlier this year, the company added. Previous to coming aboard at Meta, Brown spent 19 years at Amazon, where he focused on compute, cloud, and AI, his LinkedIn shows.

It sounds like Janardhan will remain at Meta for a little bit, although he is expected to leave next year. “Santosh will stay on for transition and he is moving into an advisory role on Meta Compute,” said a Meta representative.

Janardhan, who originally joined the company in 2009, helped the firm grow into the massive internet presence that it is today. Bloomberg originally reported Janardhan’s departure, citing an internal memo that had recently been circulated. It’s unclear why Janardhan is stepping down from the role. The memo did not specify the reasons for his departure and Meta didn’t immediately respond to a request for clarity on the issue. However, Janardhan’s memo apparently cited the need to “pass the baton.”

In recent years, the company’s investment in infrastructure has exploded, as it seeks to keep up with its competitors in the AI race. Earlier this year, Janardhan was tasked with leading Meta Compute, the company initiative to scale its data center investments and strategy.

#242. I tried Google’s AI glasses and Meta’s finally got real competition

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s a weird time to launch a new pair of smart glasses.

But in the past week, I’ve been demoing some new smart glasses from Samsung, Warby Parker, and Gentle Monster. Some are retro chic frames. Others have the same Y2K vibe as the Matrix films. All were made by Samsung and have Google’s Gemini built in via Android XR, Google’s platform for smart glasses and mixed-reality headsets.

The AI demos are better than I expected. In one, I’m sitting in an avant-garde art installation as creepy heads float above my head. On the loudspeaker, words blare in French, Spanish, Korean, and German. Pressing two fingers to my temple, Gemini starts translating each language seamlessly in real time. In another demo, a TV chef cooks a “monster pasta” recipe in Korean while people have a conversation behind me. Music in another language is playing overhead. This exact scenario tripped up the Meta Ray-Ban Display’s translation capabilities when I was on vacation. Here it’s no big deal. I don’t even have to specify the languages I need translated or, as with Meta’s glasses, download a specific language pair to translate. I sit at another table with an English speaker, a Thai speaker, and the glasses translate everything they say to me — correctly — into Japanese.

Meta’s glasses also can’t do this. But what’s striking here is that Google is making an active choice to take facial recognition completely off the table.

“We’re not going to target ads on it. We’re not going to train Gemini on it. Humans aren’t going to be able to use it to improve the models. We’re also redacting faces before they actually go to the server in the first place, which then gets ahead of certain things that can obviously go sideways if you don’t take those steps proactively.”

It’s not just faces that’ll get redacted either. Payne tells me that ambient noise — like bystander conversations — will also get scrubbed. The glasses will only process audio from the glasses-wearer using beam-forming mics. Camera and mic access will also require opt-in permissions.

The one area Meta truly nailed with smart glasses has been style. The company, through its partnership with EssilorLuxottica, has over 100 combinations of frames and lenses. Samsung’s partnerships with Warby Parker and Gentle Monster aren’t too shabby to look at. Warby Parker has three designs and three colors: green, brown, and black. Gentle Monster has six frames — including two that are very Y2K — with bolder designs, colors, and tinted lens options. The Warby Parker collection starts at $395, while Gentle Monster starts at $449.

Style-wise, I found both collections were fetching. Warby Parker was a bit more everyday, whereas Gentle Monster felt a bit more editorial and runway-ready. Most people should be able to find a look that fits their needs.

The glasses are fairly lightweight (for smart glasses) at 41g. Battery life is estimated at around nine hours, while the cases support up to seven extra charges. Sound quality is pretty good, and the 12MP ultrawide camera takes decent photos and video with image stabilization. So, head tilters rejoice! Your accidental Dutch-angle photos will be straightened.

Where Android XR excels is that Gemini on the glasses is, so far, more capable than Meta AI on the Ray-Bans. I was able to look at an event flyer for a concert in Mumbai, have Gemini add the details to Google Calendar, and then look up flight details to India. It gave me turn-by-turn walking directions while also telling me the event schedule for the Whitney Museum. Beginning a live translation doesn’t require me to download a specific language pair onto my phone like with the Ray-Bans. I was also surprised to see Gemini occasionally correct itself in real-time when it made a mistake, or ask me to get a different view of an object I was trying to identify so it could deliver a more accurate answer.

Low-vision and blind users aren’t left out either. The glasses feature Google’s Guided Vision, which offers conversational audio descriptions of environments and surroundings. Android XR is also collaborating with Aira, which allows users to stream what they’re seeing using the camera to a real-time human interpreter. At launch, this particular feature will be for Android users only, but otherwise the glasses will work regardless of your phone.

You can preorder Gentle Monster and Warby Parker smart glasses starting today, and the devices are expected to ship starting December 10th.

#241. Why the data center you dread might never even be built

The data center boom is running into serious real-world obstacles, from widespread local opposition and permitting delays to a scramble for electricity. Before those challenges even come into play, though, there’s a more basic problem: A lot of the projects being announced may never get built in the first place. With billions of dollars in AI infrastructure investment at stake, separating realistic projects from speculative ones is becoming increasingly important.

On this episode of TechCrunch’s Equity podcast, hosts Rebecca Bellan and Tim De Chant are joined by Brent Nelson, senior managing director of markets and strategy at Ascend Analytics, to separate the data center projects that are likely to get built from the ones that may never get off the ground.

Theresa Loconsolo is an audio producer at TechCrunch focusing on Equity, the network’s flagship podcast. #240. British counterterrorism police arrest US soldier accused of spying on behalf of Russia

A member of the US Army has been arrested near Gloucester in England. Police said he was suspected of espionage activity in connection with Russia. Investigators from the US and UK are involved in the probe.

A 41-year-old member of the US Army was arrested near Gloucester on Tuesday in England, the Metropolitan Police said in a statement.

Authorities searched two locations in the Gloucestershire region, police said, adding: "The country to which the allegations pertain is Russia."

US authorities, the US Military and the FBI were closely involved in the investigation, police said.

The man was in custody at a London police station, authorities said on Tuesday evening as the news emerged.

Deputy Assistant Commissioner Vicki Evans, Senior National Coordinator for Counter Terrorism Policing called it a "significant arrest" as a result of "close collaboration between UK and US authorities."

"The allegations are serious and our investigation is very much ongoing," Evans said.

FBI Director Kash Patel said the case was of "utmost concern" to the US and its allies.

"While this investigation is continuing, I want to send a clear message — this FBI has no tolerance for anyone who breaks their oath to the United States and whose actions endanger our national security and that of our partners," Patel said. He also expressed thanks to UK investigators across various branches and the US Military Counterintelligence Command "for their outstanding work."

Brigadier General Sean Stinchon, the commanding general of US Army Counterintelligence Command also said the case demonstrated the "resolve of the counterintelligence community."

"The alleged conduct of a senior soldier, entrusted with sensitive information, is a sobering reminder that insider threats are real and persistent," Stinchon said.

The area around Gloucestershire in the west of England, around two hours' drive from London, is home to several sensitive military and intelligence sites as well as outposts for various British and US companies with ties to the defense industry.

RAF Fairford, which was in the news earlier this year amid a security scare at the vast airbase used by both UK and US planes, is not far southeast of Gloucester.

Gloucester's neighboring town, Cheltenham, is one the UK's intelligence hubs outside London. It is home to the headquarters of the country's signals intelligence agency GCHQ.
