#239. The pricey new Apple TV 4K is still the best streamer around

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Apple knows how to make a great streaming box to be the center of your home theater, but there are some misses with the new model.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Photography by Hunter McRae for The Verge

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I’m sitting in front of my TV, puppy nuzzled up next to me, with four HomePods spread around my living room — two flanking my TV and one at either end of my couch — connected to the brand new Apple TV 4K. Young Anakin Skywalker hits the throttle and flies through the room. There hasn’t been an Apple surround sound system until now, and since it was announced, I was excited to try it out. And let me tell you, watching a pod race with an all-Apple audio setup is a whole lot of fun.

Surround sound across four HomePods is one of the flagship features of the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K, which is out tomorrow for $300. It’s an expensive streaming box — double and in some cases quadruple the price of competing options — that tries to earn its place in your living room with premium and convenient new features: a powerful chip, lots of storage, a trackable remote, and lots of AI. If you’re deep in the Apple ecosystem and willing to splurge, the box is a solid choice. But for everyone else, I’m not convinced the added features do enough to justify that steep new price.

Easy to use and fast interfaceSupports both Dolby Vision and HDR10+Four HomePod system sounds great

Expensive for a streamerSiri AI is inconsistent

From the outside, it’s hard to tell what’s new about the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K. Compared to the 2022 model, it’s the same size, in the same finish, with the same number of ports (in the same configuration), showing the same interface, with the same HDR support. Where things are upgraded is largely on the inside: there’s a more powerful chip — the A19, which first showed up in the iPhone 17 — and 128GB of storage by default, which was previously an upgrade. The flip side is that there is no lower-cost 64GB model anymore, which is a shame because I don’t think most people need any more than that.

The included aluminum remote is visually the same as the previous one. It charges via USB-C, has a voice-activation button on its side, and its clickpad is touch-sensitive, which can thankfully be disabled in the menu. There is one small quality of life change, though. The remote now has the Find My feature. It shows up in the Find My app on your phone, which does a great job of pinpointing the remote’s location; it can also make a sound when you can’t spot it. For a busy family like mine, this is great when one of us unintentionally brings the remote into the kitchen or bedroom (or when it’s buried in the cushions or pillows of the couch).

Siri AI is here, too, though it’s also coming to second- and third-gen Apple TV 4Ks with tvOS 27.2. The new Siri can be pretty great, when it works, especially when using personal context to understand your requests. If Apple Intelligence is enabled on your iPhone or iPad and it’s on the same network as the Apple TV 4K, the box can recognize your voice and securely connect to your device to help personalize answers to you. My friend had recently texted me about a show to watch, but I couldn’t remember what it was. When I asked Siri to find the show he had recommended, it searched my messages and opened it up on the TV (it was Pluribus).

But Siri had occasional issues with personalized answers. I asked this request a few times over my days of testing, and it didn’t always give the same answer. While it opened Pluribus on a few occasions, it also once opened The Bondsman which, to be fair, he did recommend to me… just way back in October of 2025. But even when I clarified, “No, the show he messaged about yesterday,” Siri wasn’t able to find it.

The Apple TV 4K also had trouble recognizing my voice from one moment to the next. A few minutes after asking about Pluribus, I put on an episode of Bobby Flay’s The Line. I asked Siri about his competitor Marcus Samuelsson’s restaurant Hav & Mar — where it is, what type of food it serves, if it had any accolades (I found out it was awarded two stars by The New York Times), and when it would be open during an upcoming trip to New York City. But when I changed the topic to a movie my wife and I were texting about, Siri suddenly couldn’t recognize my voice to use personal context, even though it had answered my Pluribus question minutes before. My iPhone was still on the coffee table in front of me and on the Wi-Fi network, so the connection between it and the Apple TV 4K should have been strong enough for personal context to work without issue.

Requests can be misunderstood, too. My days of testing overlapped with the F1 Brazilian Grand Prix, which is broadcast on Apple TV. When live coverage of the race started, I asked Siri, “Play the F1 Brazilian Grand Prix.” Instead of the race, it started the music track “Hymn of the Racers” by the 1995 band F1. “Play the São Paulo F1 Grand Prix” played the song “São Paulo,” a collaboration between The Weeknd and Anitta. I spent more time trying to get Siri to understand what I wanted than it would take to use the remote to start it.

When it hits, Siri AI is great. “Play Empire Strikes Back” started the Star Wars film on Disney Plus in seconds without having to mention the streaming service. Getting Pluribus to open from a question relatively scarce on details was awesome (when it happened). But as it stands right now, Siri AI is still very much a beta project and needs some work and refinement to be consistently useful. That’s not to say it’s much worse than alternatives like Alexa and Gemini, but right now it’s not doing enough to stand apart.

You can talk to Siri using the Apple TV remote, but the new model also has far-field microphones built into the box, so you can talk aloud from anywhere. One of the concerns I had when it was announced that the Apple TV 4K was getting far-field mics was whether it would even be able to hear me while in my home theater stand, but that wasn’t an issue for me. Whenever I said “Hey Siri,” I could see it activated on my TV screen. Now, my credenza has an open back for ventilation, which might have helped, but having a HomePod as part of the system also solves the issue. When one (or more) is part of the TV 4K system, the HomePod mics are listening for the “Hey Siri” requests, communicating through Bluetooth, and determining which device should respond.

Then there’s the new feature I had the most fun with: home theater surround. Apple added the ability to use two second-generation HomePods as a stereo pair (with Atmos) a couple of years ago. But as the hub for a home theater, the Apple TV 4K can now connect with four HomePods for a more immersive experience than what you can get with just your (almost certainly terrible) TV speakers.

This could be four second-gen HomePods, four HomePod mini 2 speakers, or a pair of each, as long as the two up front are the same and the two surrounds are the same. This is the setup I tested, with a pair of second-gen HomePods in front and a pair of HomePod Mini 2s in back.

Calibrating the system is easy. With all of the speakers connected to your Apple Home, each speaker emits a gentle pinging sound and a wash of pink noise that sounds like ocean waves, while a ripple of color moves across the TV screen corresponding to the color on the top of each HomePod to make sure positioning is correct. The process takes only a couple of minutes.

The speakers and the Apple TV 4K need to be a part of the same Apple Home and set up by the same user. My wife’s Apple account is the primary on our Apple Home, and even though we’re all a part of the same Apple family, when I set up the 4K TV with my account, it couldn’t see any of the speakers. This is not a major issue, but it can make setup more difficult.

As a unit, the two second-gen HomePods and two HomePod mini 2 speakers worked together really well. Any of the speakers can be tapped to pause the screen, and the volume controls affect the entire system. The big HomePods have far more bass response than expected for their size. I felt the massive Dreadnought’s cannon fire on the Rebel base in The Last Jedi through the floor. And the car explosions as The Penguin attempts to escape pursuit in The Batman felt deep and impactful. It’s not the same heart-rattling rumble a dedicated subwoofer can provide, but for a smaller space (and one with neighbors) it’s a good amount of bass. (I do hope Apple decides to make a standalone wireless subwoofer for the setup, though.)

The front HomePods create a very convincing phantom center channel, making dialog sound like it’s coming right from the screen. The entire spatial sound placement at the listening plane from the four-speaker system is excellent. The calibration sets the surrounds’ volume at a good level. There’s an option to boost or cut the volume of the surround speaker pair from 8dB to -8dB (in 0.5dB increments), but I didn’t think tweaking was necessary.

The different iconic cars in the opening race of Ready Player One zoomed around the room — as did King Kong as he chased after Parzival and Art3mis. Podracers in The Phantom Menace bumped one another and skittered off in pieces into the desert of Tatooine. The speakers deliver a great surround experience.

Atmos isn’t as convincing. There’s still some height to the sound, thanks to the larger HomePods, but it doesn’t form a full enveloping canopy. As the sandstorm envelops Paul Atreides during the rescue of the spice harvester crew in Dune, there’s an audible shimmer as the spice takes hold and whispers encircle him. As with Ready Player One and The Phantom Menace, the sand swirls around the listener, but doesn’t extend above.

I found the same with Dolby Atmos music, such as “bury a friend” by Billie Eilish. The backup vocals in the surrounds sounds nice and supportive, but there are sonic elements (like a rhythmic drill) that are supposed to fly overhead and instead sound like they move by in front of me. The music mix still sounds great, but it isn’t quite what is intended.

And the expense extends to the home theater system. The entire price of the system I used — two second-gen HomePods, two HomePod mini 2s, and the new Apple TV 4K — costs $1,300. If you already have a couple HomePods in your home and want to repurpose them for your home theater (or you’re already using a stereo pair as your TV speakers), adding in the mini 2 speakers and the new 4K TV is a viable prospect. But if you’re looking to start fresh with a surround system and don’t mind the aesthetics of a soundbar, the Sonos Beam Ultra paired with two Era 100 speakers is an excellent alternative, both for its flexibility of adding more speakers and its superior handling of Atmos content. Plus, you can add a subwoofer later and use a cheaper third-gen Apple TV 4K instead of the new one.

There’s no doubt that Apple still makes one of the best streaming devices available. It’s fast, has a great user experience, and the additions of Find My on the remote, far-field mics, and the home theater surround capability add good value (especially if you already have some newer HomePods).

But the new Apple TV 4K is $300. And Siri AI isn’t there yet to consistently make it worth using. Right now, the Apple TV 4K is an excellent, premium-priced streaming device that doesn’t set itself apart enough from its previous model. And with every other streaming device a fraction of the Apple TV 4K’s price, most people would be well served by a cheaper Google Streamer, Fire TV stick, or Roku stick. Yes, the Apple TV 4K is better than all of them. But as it currently stands, especially with Siri AI still in beta, I don’t think it’s twice as better.

#238. Jensen Huang accused of having another affair — with an Nvidia exec

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is already under investigation after having admitted to having an affair with a woman accused of being a Chinese spy. Now the New York Times is reporting that Huang is also suspected of having another extramarital affair, with a direct report of his at the company — Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of healthcare. Powell, like Huang, is married, the outlet says.

The Times says it was told of the alleged affair by five employees at the company. The sources said that Huang and Powell were captured on video “being affectionate at a San Jose, Calif., hotel” during a conference earlier this year.

Like many companies, Nvidia has rules discouraging superiors from having relationships with employees in subordinate roles, the Times notes. TechCrunch reached out to Nvidia for comment.

The company’s board has initiated an investigation into Huang’s publicly admitted affair. Additionally, two House Republicans said Wednesday that Congress is gearing up to subpoena Huang over the spy allegations if he doesn’t voluntarily respond to their summons to a closed-door briefing. Late Wednesday, after the reports of the second affair were published, the Wall Street Journal reported that the Nvidia board was preparing to oust Huang, who co-founded the company in 1993.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Hi! I’m Jen Tuohy, The Verge’s senior smart home reviewer. I’ve tested every smart display Amazon and Google have ever made, and for the past week I’ve been testing Apple’s first: The $299 HomeView.

I put the tabletop and wall-mounted versions through their paces, on my kitchen counter, bedside table, in my office, and on my coffee table. I also let my family loose on it, and my tech-averse husband was a big fan. My teenage daughter, less so. (Ask me why).

#233. Nvidia’s stock drops as board reportedly preps to oust CEO Jensen Huang

Nvidia is reportedly planning to oust its co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang, who has led the company for the last 33 years, the Wall Street Journal reports. Sources told the newspaper that the Nvidia board is meeting as soon as Wednesday to determine Huang’s future at the company.

On Sunday, Huang admitted to having an extramarital affair with a woman purported by U.S. intelligence to be a Chinese spy. On Wednesday evening, the New York Times reported that Huang also had an affair with an executive who reports to him.

Nvidia has become one of the most valuable companies in the world as it designs the chips that power the AI boom, but in the midst of Huang’s ongoing scandal, Nvidia stock fell about 8% in after hours trading on Wednesday. The stock is down about 11% in the past five days.

Huang famously delegates very little and has about 60 direct reports, he has said. The company also has not had an obvious successor or a succession plan. According to Bloomberg, Nvidia is working with an outside firm to identify candidates that could potentially replace Huang as CEO.

SpaceXSI CEO Elon Musk late on Wednesday posted on X that hiring Huang “would be an honor.”

#232. Earliest evidence of psychoactive cannabis use found in ancient Siberian tomb

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Archaeologists say a 2,800-year-old tomb in southern Siberia contains the earliest direct evidence yet that people used cannabis for its mind-altering effects, based on preserved seeds and human hair. The find comes from an elite Scythian burial and adds new detail to the plant’s role in ancient ritual practices.

#231. Lego’s BrickLink marketplace has been offline for a week following ‘unusual activity’

BrickLink was taken offline on November 5th but Lego doesn’t know when it will return.

BrickLink was taken offline on November 5th but Lego doesn’t know when it will return.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Bricklink, a popular online marketplace that Lego bought in 2019 where fans can buy and sell parts and sets, has been down for a week and the company doesn’t know when it will return. The team who runs the site has been sharing updates over the past week including estimates of when it will be back up. It was last expected to return on November 11th, according to Jay’s Brick Blog, but a statement Lego shared to the site yesterday says there’s currently no estimate of when BrickLink will be back online.

Lego hasn’t explained exactly what happened with the marketplace, but says it “took BrickLink offline after identifying unusual activity on the platform. Our immediate priority was to secure the site, protect our members and ensure the integrity of BrickLink.” There are also no specifics on when BrickLink will return, as Lego works to contain what happened. “Before bringing the site back online, we need to fully understand what has happened. That means containing the issue, investigating how it happened and checking for related issues.”

Three years ago, BrickLink experienced a similar issue around the same time of year resulting in the marketplace being offline for several days due to a cybersecurity incident. Lego shut down the site “out of an abundance of caution” after receiving a “threat and ransom demand.” When BrickLink returned, users were prompted to change their passwords with stronger requirements, but were assured their accounts had not been compromised during the incident.

#230. Who could replace Jensen Huang? A highly unscientific list of possible successors

It’s almost inconceivable that Nvidia would fire its rock star CEO. Yet here we are, on the precipice of this happening. Over at Kalshi, Jensen Huang’s odds of surviving the week are sitting at a lowly 30%. Even though he built one of the world’s most valuable — and, many would argue, dangerously powerful — companies, the tides have completely turned against him. He has confessed to an extramarital affair, but as of Wednesday afternoon it isn’t even clear whom he meant: Alice Huang, the Chinese executive suspected by U.S. intelligence of spying for her country, or Kimberly Powell, an Nvidia executive who reports to him and who, five sources told The New York Times in an explosive new report, was also a paramour.

Even if Huang is pushed out as CEO, the board members he has made very rich are unlikely to cut him loose entirely. Barring another shoe dropping, they could keep him on as chairman, a role Nvidia doesn’t currently have. But someone still has to run the company, and there’s no apparent succession plan, which leaves us, like everyone else, to speculate while we wait to see what happens next. While Huang’s 60 direct reports undoubtedly get a closer look, consider this our own parlor game, featuring some obvious and less obvious candidates:

Lisa Su. The AMD CEO runs Nvidia’s most credible rival, as well as a distant cousin of Huang. The Information floated her name this week, but why would she leave her job for his? Her company stands to benefit from every day that Nvidia spends distracted by an investigation, congressional briefings, and all the mini dramas that are sure to follow. Her best move might be to send a nice note and keep her current corner office.

Eric Schmidt. He was the pick of Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, senior associate dean for leadership studies at the Yale School of Management, who we talked with earlier this week. Schmidt could “parachute in tomorrow and do a very good job,” Sonnenfeld said, and perhaps he could. He ran Google from 2001 to 2011, chaired the National Security Commission on AI from 2018 to 2021, and now runs the rocket company Relativity Space. Chips, software, government, rockets — it’s an impressive range. Then again, Schmidt would bring a lot of baggage when it comes to his own extramarital dalliances. It’s hard to see him clearing the board for that reason.

Lip-Bu Tan. Intel’s CEO knows chips, and right now he might welcome a very soft landing. Last month, Intel reported third-quarter revenue up 21% to $16.5 billion, then warned its fourth-quarter results would fall short of Wall Street’s forecasts. The AI boom has caused a memory-chip shortage, pushing up PC prices and cutting PC sales. That’s hurt Intel (PC chips are a big part of its business), and its stock plunged 20% in after-hours trading on the warning, having already slid more than 30% since September. A move to a company that’s eating everyone’s lunch would be a pretty amazing escape hatch.

Bret Taylor. The former Salesforce co-CEO and OpenAI board chair now runs the AI startup Sierra, and his resume includes a lot of the biggest brands in Silicon Valley. He co-created Google Maps, served as Facebook’s CTO, and chaired Twitter’s board through its sale to Elon Musk. He then took over as OpenAI’s board chair after the “Blip,” Sam Altman’s firing and fast return. When companies need an unflappable, likable adult in the room, they call Taylor. He probably knows nearly every customer Nvidia has, too.

Ian Buck. If the board feels strongly about having a “builder” at the helm, they could do worse than the Nvidia VP who helped develop CUDA, the chip software that has become central to Nvidia’s dominance in AI. Analysts and former employees have floated him as a possible successor, and Kalshi traders give him a middling shot. He’s no household name, but the Stanford PhD seems more viable if Huang can can keep that chairman title and push for a successor he can work with.

Someone else down the hall. Andrew Bell, Nvidia’s senior vice president of hardware engineering, arrived 25 years ago as a fresh Cornell grad and has been on an upward trajectory at the company ever since. Like Buck and dozens of other executives who are presumably being scrutinized, he reports directly to Huang. Nvidia CFO Colette Kress is the insider most often named, but analysts say she isn’t necessarily a natural successor as an innovator and visionary, and Bell has the chip pedigree.

The wild card? Lori Huang. Nobody has floated her, but hear us out: Jensen met his wife as lab partners at Oregon State, so she has been working with him since before Nvidia existed. After graduating, she worked at Silicon Graphics as a micro chip engineer (making more money than her husband, he has said), then scaled back to raise the couple’s family, and she now co-leads the family trusts. If the board wants continuity, they could do worse than a humbler Huang who is also a brainiac. There’s a reason they say that behind every great man is a great woman (and vice versa).

#228. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#226. Nine Korean startups vie for a spot in Disrupt 2027’s Startup Battlefield 200 competition

TechCrunch’s yearly Startup Battlefield 200 competition sees hundreds of companies from across the world trying every year to get a chance to pitch in front of VCs and the international tech community at the Disrupt conference.

This week, nine early-stage South Korean startups pitched at DETO 2026, a technology conference in Seoul, to earn a spot in next year’s Startup Battlefield 200.

The startups work across the deep tech sector, including healthcare, fintech, energy, semiconductors, and AI. All nine startups are looking to enter the U.S. market as part of the broader international expansion plans.

Voinosis, which develops AI to detect changes in brain health through voice analysis, won the competition. The other eight startups are listed below in alphabetical order.

Voinosis is developing an AI tool that analyzes speech patterns such as pitch, pauses, rhythm and response times to detect changes linked to brain health. Each voice check takes less than five minutes.

The company says its technology has been tested in Greek, Japanese and English, and its product doesn’t require separate training for each language. In a clinical study involving 146 participants, the startup claims its tech achieved 88% accuracy in screening for potential cognitive issues.

Voinosis targets hospitals, senior living facilities, insurers and individual consumers. It also plans to link voice checks with cognitive training, assessments, and treatment provided by healthcare professionals.

The startup was founded by Grace Shin, who spent more than 25 years as an otologic surgeon. The startup is currently raising a Series A round and has already secured a South Korean pharmaceutical company as a strategic investor.

Awesome Lab, based in Seoul, makes ThermoFlux, a heating system that uses water electrolysis and ionization to generate heat. The system can manage heat production for buildings, water supply, and industrial processes. The company says its technology can reduce energy consumption and carbon emissions.

For its international expansion, Awesome Lab is taking a different approach in each market. In the U.S., it plans to supply core OEM modules, and work with boiler, water heater and HVAC companies on product development.

Crosshub helps travelers and international visitors verify their identities and set up payment methods abroad. Its platform, IDBlock, combines identity verification and payment services using Self-Sovereign Identity (SSI) and zero-knowledge proof.

The company says its platform helps users prove their identity across borders without sharing unnecessary personal information. Crosshub is also integrating this technology with payment systems to improve access to financial services for travelers.

DeepFusion AI has developed a 4D radar-based imaging system with additional sensors for autonomous mobility, robotaxi, maritime surveillance, and defense applications. The system combines RF sensors with radar and lidar to detect objects, movement, or distance without a direct line of sight. All sensors are supposedly packed into a single, customizable unit.

EFLOW develops special motors that can produce more torque, or rotational force, while taking up less space for use in robots, drones, satellites, and other mobility applications. The startup also develops actuators that control how robotic joints move.

Otition Medical makes two medical devices: one specializes in measuring body temperature, while another can capture ear canal images and analyze the eardrum. The company says its AI was trained on about 55,000 images of eardrums, and the device has received medical certification in South Korea.

Riibotics says it can deploy robots into warehouses within a day. Riibotics wants to reduce deployment costs for warehouses by streamlining setup, which includes infrastructure setup, warehouse mapping, system integration through custom middleware, task programming, and safety checks.

Ternell, a semiconductor startup based in Ulsan, is developing a new type of memory to help AI chips use less power.

Unlike conventional two-state memory, Ternell’s T-SRAM system adds a third state for more efficient data storage. Founded in 2019 by KyungRok Kim, a former Samsung Electronics engineer, Ternell reports that tests with 28-nanometer chips showed a 33% to 102% increase in memory capacity per area, and a 55% to 71% reduction in energy needed to access data, compared with conventional SRAM.

Testbank is a Korean edtech startup developing Solve, an app that converts printed study materials, such as workbooks and test-prep books, into interactive digital versions.

The startup uses AI to identify question and answer spaces in publishers’ PDF files while preserving the original layout. Solve lets you write answers directly on the page and be graded automatically.

#223. First Thing: Mississippi turns blue as Scott Colom unseats Republican Senate incumbent

Defeating Cindy Hyde-Smith makes the 43-year-old district attorney the first Black Mississippi senator elected since Reconstruction. Plus, the South Korean scarf vendor who has gone viral for telling people not to buy

Good morning. In a stunning upset after more than a week of vote counting in one of the country’s most reliably red states, the Democrat Scott Colom has defeated Cindy Hyde-Smith, the incumbent Republican senator, in Mississippi. He becomes the first Black senator elected in the state since Reconstruction.

Colom’s election capped a national wave of anger at Donald Trump’s administration, sweeping away Republicans in places where Democrats had not been competitive in decades. With the addition of Colom, Democrats will hold at least 55 seats in the Senate, the party’s largest midterm gain since 1986.

What did Colom say about his victory? Colom, a 43-year-old district attorney, said there had been times when his long-shot campaign felt defeated but “I had to have faith”. He told supporters: “My commitment to you, each and every one of you, is that first I’m going to treat your children like I want my children to be treated. I’m going to treat your parents the way I want my parents to be treated. I’m gonna treat your future the way I want my future to be.”

How have Democrats responded to a White House threat? Robert Garcia, the ranking member of the oversight committee in the House of Representatives said it appeared Donald Trump had “something to hide” after the White House threatened criminal investigations over the party’s planned scrutiny of the administration. Garcia said in a statement that Congress’s authority came from article I of the constitution, “not from the permission of the White House”.

Nvidia’s board of directors has met to decide whether to oust Jensen Huang as CEO of the world’s most valuable chip company, according to reports, after a tumultuous week in which the executive and co-founder of the company admitted to having a romantic relationship with a woman who is alleged to be a Chinese spy. Huang’s position became more tenuous late on Wednesday after it was reported by the New York Times that Huang had a separate extramarital affair with a company employee. Our global technology editor, Dan Milmo, explains what is happening in our Today in Focus podcast.

Democratic lawmakers expressed outrage at nearly a dozen of their colleagues for missing a vote Tuesday evening to impeach the defense secretary, Pete Hegseth, allowing Republicans to defeat the effort. Eleven Democrats missed the key procedural vote on the impeachment effort, which failed by just seven votes. “You should show up and do your job,” Abdul El-Sayed, the senator-elect of Michigan, said on CNN, while Richard Ojeda, the representative-elect of North Carolina, said: “Shame on them for not showing up … they had an opportunity to finally get rid of this buffoonery.”

Why has a video clip of Hegseth gone viral? A US army sentinel guarding the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier loudly reprimanded the defense secretary and his top staffers on Wednesday after some in the group were heard laughing and talking near the memorial following a Veterans Day ceremony. You can watch the video here.

Firefighters dug through the rubble of a three-story building in Brooklyn on Wednesday after it collapsed at the edge of a construction site, killing at least eight people and trapping others beneath the wreckage. Firefighters were called to the scene in the borough’s Flatbush section at about 4pm.

Russia’s ambassador to the United States has died aged 66. Alexander Darchiev took up the role last year.

The US could lose its measles elimination status under RFK Jr’s leadership, with the country in the middle of worst measles outbreak since 1991.

Average US heating bills are set to rise 7% this winter, according a report by the National Energy Assistance Directors Association.

Starbucks baristas, students, elected officials and allies are holding rallies at more than 40 college campuses across the US to support the Starbucks Workers United boycott campaign as the union nears five years of deadlock with the company over contract negotiations.

Two skulls that likely belonged to enslaved people are being returned to Brazil after Harvard used them in eugenics studies. The remains had been outside Brazil since the 19th century.

A coyote that officials thought they had killed after it attacked a child has been seen alive in an LA neighborhood.

Ethics watchdogs have questioned the necessity of the country’s top diplomat shuttling so often between Washington DC and Gainesville, where his son Anthony has a walk-on role as a running back.

Lauren Gould has been one of the protein-nibbling guinea pigs for this research task, and with the rise of GLP-1s making protein the crown jewel of the health and wellness world, here is the verdict on what tastes best. And if you want to round out your protein snack with a coffee, here is a guide to six ways baristas make great coffee at home – and one they all hate.

The incomparable Jane Wiedlin has written a memoir that even she herself suggests contains a bit too much information. Jim Farber speaks to the former Go-Go about industry sexism, living with bipolar disorder and the freedom of sex dungeons.

The British actor took up writing and producing to have control over what she stars in after a scandal involving some of the most powerful men in the film business. Emine Saner interviews her.

Lorenzo Tondo reports on how, since the US war on Iran began in February, there have been hundreds of oil spills, posing a “generational” threat to wildlife.

Follow the latest from Cop31, where Greta Thunberg and John Kerry have been speaking today, with our live blog.

Seoul’s “scarf uncle” has become a South Korean sensation for a sales technique that consists of talking customers out of purchases. The combination of his affectionate bluntness and amusing selling style – coupled with praise for his looks – has led to him becoming a huge star on social media.

#222. Nvidia’s Jensen Huang resigns, chipmaker names CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO

Jensen Huang has resigned from his position as president and chief executive of Nvidia, the company said on Thursday, just a few days after he admitted to a romantic affair with an alleged Chinese spy, sparking a U.S. national security crisis.

In a regulatory filing, Nvidia said its board accepted Huang’s resignation on Wednesday.

Nvidia said its current chief financial officer and executive vice president, Collete Kress, will take over as interim CEO until the company finds a permanent successor. Kress will continue to serve as the company’s CFO.

Nvidia’s shares were down nearly 12% in early morning trading on Thursday. Since Friday, the company’s stock has fallen over 19%, resulting in a market cap loss of more than $855 billion.

Huang founded Nvidia in 1993 and has led it as CEO ever since. Nvidia essentially invented the graphics processing unit (GPU), a specialized chip that takes care of graphics processing for PCs, as we know it today, bringing together transforms, lighting and rendering into one chip. The chips were not an immediate hit, and the early days of Nvidia were characterized by struggle and layoffs, but eventually, it struck on a GPU design that performed so well, it became the standard for graphics processing.

These chips also turned out to be extremely good at doing large numbers of calculations simultaneously, which has made them popular for computation-intensive tasks like crypto mining, and more recently, training and running neural networks, which are what AI models are at their core.

Nvidia was initially focused on making GPUs aimed at gaming, but it had already moved into general-purpose computing by 2006 with its CUDA architecture that let developers use the chips for more purposes. After AI researchers began using the company’s GPUs to train neural networks over a decade ago, Huang leaned in towards developing chips for AI and then data centers, and grew it from there into its dominant position.

The potential implications of Huang’s departure for the AI and data center industries are vast, given how active Huang, and by extension Nvidia, has been in furthering the AI boom. In particular, it raises questions about the future of Nvidia’s sprawling AI investments, which have helped bankroll everything from startups developing new technologies to the enormous data centers needed to run them.

Under Huang, Nvidia deftly used its financial muscle to support companies buying its chips, effectively helping to finance the market for its own products, as well as lobbying Washington to relax potential rules around AI and data center regulation.

As of the end of its second quarter, Nvidia reported, Nvidia reported $99 billion worth of investments and another $25 billion of commitments. It also has $36 billion in cloud service commitments, which are arrangements that help AI cloud operators finance the GPUs and data center equipment they buy from Nvidia, with the chipmaker saying it will buy compute from them and potentially earn a share of their operators’ revenue.

Nvidia has also been investing directly in neoclouds, the companies serving a lot of the AI processing needs. In January, the company invested $2 billion in CoreWeave, and another $2 billion in Nebius in March. The chipmaker was said to be considering being a guarantor for Nscale’s planned West Virginia data center project. It’s also signed up to provide up to $105 billion worth of credit guarantees for an OpenAI data center campus in Ohio.

Nvidia’s also a major investor in the labs building the AI models that run on its GPUs, putting $30 billion into OpenAI, and $10 billion into Anthropic. It’s also backed Mira Murati’s Thinking Machines Lab as part of a deal involving at least 1 GW of its Vera Rubin GPU systems. The chipmarker has even invested $3.5 billion in MediaTek to help the Taiwanese company develop custom AI chips that work with Nvidia’s systems.

Investors were already uneasy with the enormous amounts of debt companies in the space were raising to sustain the AI infrastructure buildout, and Huang’s exit throws another uncertainty-shaped wrench into the works. CoreWeave’s shares were down about 9% on Thursday, reversing part of a 25% rally on Wednesday, while Nebius is down about 16%. IREN fell 6.6%.

Nvidia’s board has also appointed Harvey Jones, a managing partner of Square Wave Ventures and former CEO of Synopsys, as the company’s first-ever chair of the board of directors. Jones has been on Nvidia’s board since 1993.

Huang is the single largest personal shareholder at Nvidia, but he owns only about 3.5% of the company’s shares. And, unlike most of Silicon Valley’s tech giants that were founded far later, he does not hld supervoting rights. That means that the board had the power to vote him out at any time

“Jensen’s contributions to NVIDIA are profound. NVIDIA is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. The Board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry,” the company said in a statement.

#221. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#217. Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO after alleged affairs

Move follows admission of affair with woman alleged to be Chinese spy and reported relationship with employee

Jensen Huang has resigned as chief executive of Nvidia after reports of extramarital affairs with an alleged Chinese spy and a company employee.

He will be replaced on an interim basis by Nvidia’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, until a full-time successor is named.

The statement from Nvidia’s board of directors confirming Huang’s departure did not mention the spying scandal and personal conduct revelations that have enveloped the company, instead paying tribute to Huang’s “profound” contribution to the company he co-founded.

“Jensen’s contributions to Nvidia are profound. Nvidia is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. The board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry,” the statement said.

The chipmaker’s board earlier this week appointed leading law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct a “thorough and independent” investigation into the relationship with Alice Huang, who is no relation to the Nvidia boss. The firm directed Jensen Huang to continue to helm Nvidia as the investigation was ongoing.

However, late on Wednesday, the New York Times reported that Huang had a separate extramarital affair with a company employee named Kimberly Powell. She was Nvidia’s president of healthcare and was reportedly captured on video being affectionate with Huang at a hotel during a company conference in March.

The report added to the pressure on Huang as the board met late on Wednesday.

Huang’s exit from the company he co-founded in 1993 removes a senior figure in Silicon Valley at a time when the AI arms race between the US and China is at an apex. Huang owns more than 870m shares, or about 3.6% of the company and is worth $145bn (£109bn), according to Bloomberg, making him the world’s 13th richest person. Unlike many of his contemporaries at other leading tech companies, Huang has dozens of direct reports, an unusual management structure that emphasises his control over the company.

The entire artificial intelligence ecosphere relies on the company, which engineers advanced chips used to train AI models and power companies such as OpenAI and Anthropic. It is also one of the most valuable companies on Earth, with a market cap of more than $4tn.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

Nvidia and Huang have both taken a financial hit as the scandal unfolds: company shares slid about 7% in pre-market trading on Thursday after the Wall Street Journal reported Nvidia’s board was readying Huang’s ouster.

While Jensen Huang admitted to having an affair without directly naming the suspected spy, he denies any knowledge that Alice Huang allegedly worked for the Chinese government. The pair were seen together around the world, dining in restaurants and attending meetings, over the past few years. Alice Huang is a businesswoman and head of the Singapore-based datacentre firm Megaspeed, who US intelligence services suspect of being a Chinese asset according to multiple reports.

Jensen Huang, who has been married to his wife, Lori, since 1985, admitted in an email to employees on Sunday after days of speculation about his relationship with the Megaspeed executive that he had an affair and that he would cooperate with an internal investigation. Nvidia’s board of directors put out a statement the same day that the company was launching an investigation regarding reports that Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort.

Donald Trump has backed Jensen Huang, saying he had done “an incredible job” and is “an honorable guy”. Big tech industry players, including Elon Musk, have also thrown their support behind Huang. However, Nvidia’s stock has tumbled with the news, and is down more than 11% in the past few days.

#214. DC shooting injures at least 5 in Logan Circle neighborhood as police respond to ‘active’ scene

Local council member says police are looking for suspect, while reports say shooting occurred at or outside of a gym

Multiple people have been shot in north-west Washington DC on Thursday morning, local police confirmed have confirmed, prompting a “barricade situation” in the area.

A spokesperson for the Washington DC police department told the Guardian in a brief phone call on Thursday morning that the scene was “still active”. No further details were immediately available.

The DC police department said in a social media post at about 8.20am ET that officers were on the scene responding to an “active shooting in the area of 15th and P Street, Northwest”. The department’s emergency response team was also at the scene. The department urged the public to avoid the area.

In a separate statement at 9.21am ET, the DC police said that the incident has “resulted in a barricade situation in the 1500 block of 15th Street, Northwest”.

“Please avoid the area as we work to resolve the incident,” they added.

The Metropolitan police department has also issued a shelter-in-place order for those in the immediate vicinity of the 1500 block of 15th Street NW. They have urged residents to “go inside to the nearest building, stay away from windows and remain indoors until further notice”.

Local council member Brooke Pinto also posted on social media on Thursday morning that there had been a shooting “at and outside Vida fitness this morning in Logan Circle”.

“Do not go to this area,” Pinto said. “Police looking for suspect.”

A reporter for the Washington Sun said that they spoke with a witness who was in the gym and “heard one gunshot and saw one person down in the gym itself, followed by more shots”.

The person told the reporter that the gym “cleared out” and that people were running from the scene with their “arms raised” as police yelled to clear out.

Live broadcasts from the area show heavy police presence, dozens of police vehicles, and helicopters overhead.

A Vida Fitness manager told ABC7News that shots were fired outside the business. The local news station reported that as of 9am local time on Thursday morning, the business was still under lockdown and there is police inside the building.

The manager said that they feel safe with police inside, and that the gym will be closed the rest of the day.

Several schools in the area are on lockdown on Thursday morning.

#211. Ur-Nammu

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Ur-Nammu (c. 2112–2094 BCE) founded the Third Dynasty of Ur and ushered in the Ur III period, often called the Sumerian Renaissance. He is remembered for the Code of Ur-Nammu, the oldest extant law code, and for commissioning the Great Ziggurat of Ur while promoting building projects, irrigation, and cultural revival.

#210. ‘I had the courage to believe in Mississippi’ says Scott Colom after historic Senate win – US politics live

Colom adds to blue wave of Democrat victories across the US and becomes Mississippi’s first black senator since 1875 after defeating Cindy Hyde-Smith

Hello, and welcome to the US politics live blog.

Mississippi has elected Scott Colom, a Democrat, as its senator, adding to a wave of Democratic gains across the country in this year’s midterm elections.

He defeated incumbent Republican Cindy Hyde-Smith in the closely contested Senate race to become the state’s first black senator since the post-Civil War Reconstruction era, marking a historic moment for Mississippi and a major breakthrough for the Democrats in a traditionally Republican state.

“All I did was have the courage to imagine a night like this,” Colom said in his victory speech last night.

“I had the courage to believe in Mississippi. I had the courage to understand that we can show the country that Mississippi matters.”

Just over a week since the midterm elections, the Associated Press called the race after absentee and provisional ballots were counted.

“While this is not the outcome we worked for or hoped for, I respect the decision of Mississippi voters,” Hyde-Smith said.

You can read more about Colom’s grassroots campaign, his viral advertising strategy and how he reached voters at Trump rallies despite limited early backing from national Democrats here:

US defence secretary Pete Hegseth was admonished by a military guard after he and his entourage were seen laughing and talking near the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier, where visitors are required to remain silent. The incident came days after Republicans blocked an attempt to impeach him.

An active shooter has reportedly barricaded himself after shooting at least five people in Washington DC near Logan Circle. Local police have put a notice out for a “white male, heavy built” wearing gray clothes. There is a shelter in place order for those in the immediate vicinity.

One local independent news outlet reported that the suspect has shot at police through the barricades.

#209. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#206. The unfolding Jensen Huang scandal: a timeline

Nvidia co-founder and (now former) CEO Jensen Huang has never been as flashy or controversy-prone as AI leaders like OpenAI’s Sam Altman or SpaceXAI’s Elon Musk. Now, the 63-year-old industry kingpin has admitted that he cheated on his wife of 40 years in affairs that could have ramifications beyond Huang’s personal life and U.S. national security.

U.S. officials believe that Huang’s likely romantic interest, Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, is a Chinese spy who has been feeding information back to Beijing. (Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen, is of no relation to Jensen Huang.)

Megaspeed is a Singapore-based AI infrastructure company that reportedly purchased $4.8 billion in Nvidia chips over the last two years. The U.S. and Singapore have both previously investigated Megaspeed over suspicions that the firm was illegally sharing chips with China. The Justice Department has since taken over the federal investigation into the company, The New York Times has reported.

The story took a sudden new turn on Wednesday. Citing five people who worked at Nvidia, the Times reported that Huang also had an extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, the company’s vice president of health care, who reports directly to him. Nvidia, Huang and Powell have not confirmed the report. Reached by a Times reporter, Huang reportedly said, “I don’t think you should be talking to me directly,” before hanging up.

Separately, the Wall Street Journal reported that Nvidia’s board is preparing to oust Huang, and Nvidia’s shares fell in after-hours trading.

The U.S. and China are embroiled in a fierce technological arms race to build the most advanced AI infrastructure, which could shape each country’s economic future, military prowess, and capacity for innovation. Nvidia is valuable in this competition as one of the world’s largest makers of AI chips, which are essential for accelerating AI development. But as an American company, Nvidia faces government restrictions on the sale of its most powerful chips to Chinese entities.

As the controversy unfolds, we are organizing the most relevant events into a reverse-chronological timeline to keep you up to date.

Nvidia confirmed in an SEC filing on Thursday that the board accepted Huang’s resignation on Wednesday. The company’s current CFO, Collete Kress, will take over as interim CEO while still fulfilling her CFO duties. Nvidia is expected to conduct a larger search to determine a permanent successor to Huang.

Harvey C. Jones, a managing partner of Square Wave Ventures and former CEO of Synopsys, was appointed as the company’s first-ever chair of the board of directors. Jones has been on Nvidia’s board since 1993.

After reports of a second extramarital affair, Nvidia’s board reportedly plans to meet to discuss Huang’s future at the company, per the Wall Street Journal. Stock fell about 8% in after hours trading on Wednesday and is down 11% in the past five days.

Huang famously delegates very little and has about 60 direct reports, he has said. The company has not had an obvious successor or a succession plan.

The New York Times reported that Jensen Huang also had an affair with Nvidia vice president of healthcare Kimberly Powell, who reports directly to the CEO.

According to the report, Huang and Powell were captured on film “being affectionate” at a hotel during a conference earlier this year.

Huang has not responded to a request from three Republican congressman to appear for a closed-door briefing for questioning about his affair with an alleged Chinese spy. House China committee Chairman Moolenaar said Wednesday on Fox Business that if Huang doesn’t agree to the briefing, he’ll likely be subpoenaed.

The Nvidia CEO pulled out of upcoming speaking engagements at the ElevenLabs Summit and Anthropic CEO Forum.

Alice Huang and her family reportedly built a network of companies in China and Singapore to acquire Nvidia chips, including founding a Chinese company called Shanghai Century Litong. Founded in 2023, it became an Nvidia-preferred cloud partner, giving it priority access to chips, The Information reported. The Information also reported that Shanghai Century Litong facilitated buying $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia’s chips, including banned chips.

Nvidia’s head lawyer, Tim Teter, intervened in a 2025 U.S. government probe surrounding the suspected smuggling of AI chips by Megaspeed International, Bloomberg reported. Teter contacted Commerce Under Secretary Jeffrey Kessler asking questions about whether the Trump administration played a role in detaining Alice Huang.

In early 2025, Alice Huang and a business partner sought China-based investors for an Asian data center, promising they had no problem in securing GPUs, the Financial Times reported.

On Sunday night, President Trump weighed in on Huang’s controversy and showed support for the Nvidia CEO.

“I have not spoken to him about it. I just know he’s a fine person, but you never know what’s going on,” Trump said.

When asked if Huang should remain chief of Nvidia, Trump said, “I think so. Oh yeah, sure.”

Jensen Huang wrote in a letter to Nvidia employees — which was also published as a press release — that he cheated on his wife.

“I owe my family and all of you an apology,” Huang told his company. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

Huang did not mention Alice Huang by name in the message, but linked his infidelity to “the national security concerns raised in news reports.”

“Nvidia’s Board of Directors has initiated an independent investigation. I support the investigation and will cooperate fully. I love our country and our company. I am committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America’s national security. I am proud and grateful to be an American,” Huang wrote.

Nvidia shares slid over 5% the following day.

Three Republican congressmen — Chairman John Moolenaar of the House Select Committee on China, Chairman Brian Mast of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, and Chairman Rick Crawford of the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence — sent a request to Huang for a closed-door joint briefing with their three committees.

“Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the [People’s Republic of China]’s access to sensitive U.S. technology and information,” the chairmen wrote. “The issue of PRC infiltration and access to cutting-edge U.S. technology is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress. It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk.”

U.S. officials had been investigating Alice Huang and Megaspeed for more than a year when the New York Times broke the story in late October that the two executives also had a close relationship.

An Nvidia spokesperson denied that this relationship was something out of the ordinary.

“Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO’s job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year,” Nvidia spokesperson John Rizzo said. He added that “if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know.”

U.S. officials circulated a secret memo among policymakers that warned that Megaspeed’s Alice Huang, who had a relationship with Jensen Huang, was a spy for China’s Ministry of State Security. This memo was not public knowledge until it was later reported by the New York Times.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio supervised the drafting of the memo.

Around the same time, per the New York Times’ report, U.S. President Donald Trump was briefed about intelligence findings that possible Chinese spy Alice Huang had formed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. Trump did not act on this information.

The Nvidia CEO, an ally of Trump, has repeatedly advocated for the U.S. government to let Nvidia expand its business in China.

Megaspeed was confirmed to be under investigation by both the U.S. and Singaporean governments in October 2025. As a customer of Nvidia, Megaspeed was suspected of helping Chinese companies get around American embargoes on chip exports.

Megaspeed, a neocloud that used Nvidia chips, had been renting computing power to Chinese internet giant Alibaba and other Chinese companies, the Times reported. A Commerce Department official who had visited Megaspeed’s data center in Malaysia in 2024 had found a hoard of Nvidia equipment that was still packed in boxes and the concern was that this equipment was being directly shipped somewhere else, like China, rather than installed and sold as a cloud service.

Jensen Huang had been spotted socializing with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang as early as 2024 in Taiwan, where the Nvidia CEO was born.

These investigations into Megaspeed highlighted concerns about the degree to which Nvidia is able to track its chip exports. At the time, an Nvidia spokesperson told press that the company did not have “any reason to believe products had been diverted.”

“NVIDIA visited multiple Megaspeed sites yet again earlier this week and confirmed what we previously observed — Megaspeed is running a small commercial cloud, like many other companies throughout the world, as allowed by U.S. export control rules,” the Nvidia spokesperson said at the time.

#203. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns over scandal and is replaced by CFO Colette Kress on interim basis – follow live

Nvidia shares plunge 10% in pre-market trading after board ousts longtime boss Huang over two affairs, including with a suspected Chinese spy, in big blow to AI boom

Nvidia’s long-time boss Jensen Huang has stepped down over the scandal, and the company has appointed finance chief Colette Kress as interim chief executive.

The company issued this statement just now, after its board met last night:

double quotation markThe Board of Directors announced today that Jensen Huang has resigned as President, Chief Executive Officer, and a member of the Board, effective immediately.

Colette Kress, current Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of NVIDIA, has agreed to serve as Interim Chief Executive Officer. Kress will continue to serve as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer while serving as Interim Chief Executive Officer until a permanent successor has been named. The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates.

Nvidia’s shares are recovering from that early tumble – they’re down ‘only’ 7.7% – which knocks around $320bn off its valuation.

More than $575bn has been wiped off Nvidia’s value at the start of trading, as investors react to the departure of Jensen Huang.

Nvidia’s shares have tumbled by over 13% at the start of trading, dropping to $149.79 from $173.73 last night.

They ended last week at $186.64 each. And (by my maths), the company’s valuation has now slumped by $888bn so far this week.

Having ended Friday night worth $4.5tn, Nvidia’s value has now dropped to $3.61bn.

In China, 888 is seen as a lucky number... but not for Huang or Nvidia.

double quotation markColette Kress is well respected by investors and should be able to guide Nvidia through this challenging period. The task is daunting, as Nvidia has been founder-led since inception and Mr Huang concentrated much of the decision making.

double quotation markJensen Huang clearly saw the writing was on the wall and has made the decision to resign as chief executive of Nvidia. It remains to be seen if Huang will eventually stick around in some sort of ceremonial or non-executive capacity. It would be difficult to see him not retaining some sort of influence over Nvidia, but we do not yet know the full details of the allegations or the outcome of the investigation, and much of what happens next still depends on that.

For now, CFO Colette Kress has taken the role on an interim position. While there is no natural successor in place, she is the most obvious internal candidate and will now likely use this time to prove she deserves it full time. She certainly knows the company inside out and has helped develop what is a hugely successful strategy.

What this news threatens to do is harm the wider AI ecosystem in the longer-term. Huang is hugely influential in the debates around AI regulation and has developed Nvidia into the leader in AI investment and financing. With his voice now removed from the table, and the potential for Nvidia to pivot on its strategy over time, the company may not carry the sway it once did.

double quotation markThis news ultimately pours petrol onto what is already a volatile picture for tech companies following the midterm elections. That said, this news has the potential to be game changing over the long-term with a key figure out of the picture.

double quotation markThe sudden departure of one of the world’s most influential CEOs illustrates just how quickly an unconventional governance structure can become a source of risk, and often real losses, for investors. While Nvidia’s share price rocketed over the last few years, very few people were asking deep questions about CEO succession planning, board composition and challenge, or oversight of geopolitically sensitive aspects of company strategy. Now, all those issues will be front and centre, perhaps not just for Nvidia, but also for other founder-led companies like Anthropic, Meta Platforms and SpaceX.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#199. Metro Bank up for sale, with billionaire investor in line for £420m windfall

Shares up 7% after announcement as Jaime Gilinski Bacal seeks exit three years after helping to rescue bank

Metro Bank is putting itself up for sale three years after its near-collapse, in a move that could hand a £420m windfall to its Colombian billionaire investor, Jaime Gilinski Bacal.

Metro Bank made the rare decision to publicly announce it was open to bids on Thursday, paving the way for a wider pool of suitors. The announcement pushed the share price up by 7% by mid-morning, valuing the bank at about £1.3bn.

If successful, the sale would allow Gilinski Bacal a chance to sell out of the bank he helped save from near-failure in late 2023.

The 68-year-old banking tycoon, who took a seat on Metro’s board after securing a 53% stake three years ago, has already seen a sixfold increase in the value of his shares, which he bought for just 30p each as part of a wider rescue deal. That means that at Thursday’s 190p share price, his British Virgin Island-based vehicle Spaldy Investments, could cash out with a £427m windfall.

It would mark the latest turnaround success for the billionaire, who made his fortune snapping up assets of struggling lenders on the cheap, before overhauling their operations and selling them on for a big profit. Gilinski Bacal is one of the wealthiest people in Latin America, worth about $16.9bn (£12.7bn), according to Forbes.

Metro Bank has been focused on a turnaround plan – which has involved lending more heavily to businesses and people with more erratic incomes – since it was forced into the £925m rescue deal in 2023.

Metro was the first high street bank to open in the UK in more than 100 years when it was launched by another billionaire, the US businessman Vernon Hill, in 2010. It attracted a wave of customers, and offered dog-friendly branches with seven-day openings.

However, it struggled after a major accounting blunder in 2019 that prompted investors to dump their shares and fearful customers to pull their cash. It led to the resignation of top executives, as well as the then chair, Hill, who was well-known for having his yorkshire terrier Sir Duffy (and later Sir Duffy II) in tow.

Metro’s shares took another dive in 2023. That year, it emerged that the bank would need more cash from investors, having failed to convince regulators that it could be trusted to assess its own risks.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

The lender finally secured a rescue, which was brokered in part by UK regulators at the Bank of England, in 2023. It has since embarked on the turnaround plan, which has increased its profits but came at a cost for staff and customers. Metro’s chief executive, Daniel Frumkin, slashed more than 1,000 jobs in spring 2024 and decided to close branches on Sundays.

The bank said on Thursday that a sale was just one of the options on the table and that it would still be able to go it alone if no suitable bidder came through.

Metro’s board “unanimously believes that evaluating potential interest in the company” through a potential sale “will maximise the value creation opportunities available to shareholders and deliver benefits for all stakeholders”, the bank said.
