#165. Environmentalists criticise choice of Earthshot prize venue owned by fossil fuel firm

Mumbai facility is part of Reliance Industries, owner of world’s biggest oil refinery and funder of controversial mega-zoo

The Prince of Wales’s Earthshot prize will host its forthcoming annual awards ceremony at a venue owned by the billionaire Ambani family conglomerate, drawing criticism from conservationists over their fossil fuel businesses and their foundation’s controversial mega-zoo.

A spokesperson for the Earthshot prize, which awards £1m to five winners every year to invest in conservation work, confirmed to the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay that this year’s ceremony will be held at the Jio World Convention Centre in Mumbai on 17 November.

The venue is part of Mukesh Ambani’s Reliance Industries, which also owns Jamnagar, the world’s biggest oil refinery in Gujarat on India’s western coast. The group’s philanthropic arm funds the 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo Vantara, which was founded by Mukesh’s son Anant Ambani and inaugurated by the Indian prime minister, Narendra Modi, last year. The zoo is embroiled in a long-running controversy over its vast collection of more than 150,000 animals, which reportedly includes some of the world’s most endangered species – from the radiated tortoise, native to Madagascar, to the Spix’s macaw, endemic to north-eastern Brazil but now considered extinct in the wild.

Megan Carr, of the Wildlife Animal Protection Forum South Africa (WAPFSA), warned that using a venue owned by Reliance “could lend an unearned environmental prestige to a corporate group currently under question by the global conservation community”.

Conservation groups, including WAPFSA, have raised concerns about the speed with which Vantara has amassed so many animals, and about how they were sourced. The site, near the family’s oil refinery, houses more than 2,000 species of animals.

This week, the 32-member committee that governs Cites, the UN-administered body that regulates trade in endangered species, requested that India submit another report on the steps it is taking to address concerns relating to its imports of protected species.

The committee also requested the Cites secretariat review 11 cases where there are unresolved questions over how animals were sent to Vantara.

Joanna Swift, the executive director of communications at the Earthshot prize, said it had not sought a philanthropic partnership for its award ceremony venue and that it was paying the standard commercial rate to rent the space.

“The [Jio World Convention Centre] was selected because it allowed us to host the event in Mumbai while meeting the practical requirements of an event of this size. It offers world-class facilities, technical infrastructure and experience of hosting major international events, including environmental ones,” Swift said. “In February this year, JWCC hosted the first ever Mumbai climate week.”

Vantara, Kensington Palace and JWCC have been approached for comment.

Vantara has previously denied allegations of wildlife trafficking and says its purpose is “to rescue wild animals in distress, provide treatment along with lifelong care, and rehabilitate those fit for the wild”.

“With regulatory approval, we also breed select endangered or extinct-in-the-wild species, serving as a modern Noah’s Ark to ensure their survival under human care and restore wild populations through reintroduction,” Vantara says on its website.

Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo of allegations that animals had been acquired illegally or mistreated.

A later Cites fact-finding mission raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Susan Cheyne, the vice-chair of the International Union for Conservation of Nature’s small apes section, said that Vantara’s model has not yet been shown to support conservation and “may be driving demand for wild animals in trade”.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

“For gibbons and other primates, this matters a great deal. Moving animals out of their range countries into a private facility does nothing for wild populations unless there is a transparent, credible plan for those animals: for welfare, for any conservation breeding with clear goals, and for return to range states where that is possible. Real rescue and sanctuary work for primates is done by underfunded centres in range countries,” Cheyne said.

Daniela Freyer, co-founder of the German NGO Pro Wildlife, added: “Our planet is increasingly threatened by the overexploitation of wildlife and by climate change driven by fossil fuels. This week, the Cites Standing Committee, which oversees international wildlife trade, decided to keep India under its compliance procedure, as questions remain about the origin of some of the world’s rarest animals imported to Vantara. A prize whose goals include protecting nature and fixing our climate should be careful not to lend its reputation to these questions.”

Vantara had sought to lobby the UK, the chair of this week’s Cites meetings, attempting to persuade officials to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara, the Guardian and Mongabay reported earlier this month. Ministers were advised not to engage formally with Vantara, internal documents revealed.

The Earthshot prize, founded by Prince William in 2020, is billed as the most prestigious environmental award. William appeared on the BBC’s Graham Norton show last month to promote the prize, saying it was designed to “talk about urgency and optimism together”.

Swift said that it was vital to host an edition of the Earthshot prize in India.

“Mumbai is the sixth largest city on earth. It is home to exceptional environmental solutions and the people behind them, with an impact felt in India and around the world. Spotlighting progress like this, to the largest possible audience, is why the Earthshot prize exists,” Swift said.

This article was amended on 9 November 2026. An earlier version said the Vantara zoo covers 400 hectares; in fact, it is 1,400 hectares.

#162. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#160. Grenades, grief and a deepening political divide: five takeaways from Sonia Gandhi’s new memoir

From the assassinations of her husband and mother-in-law to press stonewalling under Modi, Belonging offers an insider’s account of India’s political evolution

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

Few love stories have been as closely entwined with a nation’s history as that of Sonia and Rajiv Gandhi. The former’s much-awaited political memoir, Belonging, offers a strikingly candid account of her journey from a small town in Italy to the highest echelons of power in India – all for love, she says.

The veteran Congress party leader has been fiercely protective of her personal world. In Belonging, she opens up for the first time about the extraordinary twists and turns of a life that has largely unfolded in full public view. (The Guardian’s Hannah Ellis-Petersen has a rare in-person interview with Gandhi, and you can read an excerpt from the book here.)

Belonging, published today, contains moving glimpses of Gandhi’s private grief. She writes about the loss of her first child, who died shortly after birth, and the chilling discovery of grenades in a room of their house after Operation Blue Star, the 1984 military operation against Sikh separatists at the Golden Temple in Amritsar. She also provides a ringside view of several turbulent chapters in Indian politics, not least the violent assassinations of her mother-in-law, Indira Gandhi, in 1984 and her husband, Rajiv, in 1991.

Her book also revisits some of the darker episodes that have dogged the Gandhi family and the Congress party, including Indira’s imposition of the Emergency in June 1975, a 21-month period during which civil liberties were suspended, political opponents were jailed and the press was gagged, which caused fear and dismay across the country. In Gandhi’s account, it also caused a rift within the family.

Ultimately, Gandhi’s memoir is a vivid record of six decades of India’s political transformation from the Congress-dominated secular republic she moved to in 1968 until today, when Hindu nationalism has become the dominant political force under Narendra Modi.

Here are five key takeaways from her memoir and what they reveal about the state of Indian politics today.

Gandhi has had no real contact with Modi for the last 12 years

Gandhi’s account offers a glimpse of how deep the political polarisation runs in Indian politics today. In one revealing passage, Gandhi writes that she has had “practically no real contact” with Modi in the 12 years he has led the nation. In fact, at times when the two have been in the same rooms, such as the speaker’s small chamber, Modi “tends to look away, avoiding eye contact”, she says. On occasion, she adds, he has asked briefly about her health after a hospital stay.

For her, this breakdown is a departure from the past, when leaders on opposing sides of the aisle “would treat one another with cordiality”. After the 2001 terror attack on parliament, Gandhi immediately called then-BJP prime minister AB Vajpayee to enquire about his safety, a gesture he appreciated.

Gandhi sees an increasingly authoritarian style of politics, in which those in power seek to “belittle and torment” opponents. For years, Gandhi’s son, Rahul, has been the subject of BJP’s ridicule, which calls him Pappu, a derisive term intended to portray him as inexperienced and naive. She adds that similar things are happening elsewhere in the world, an observation that can be read in the context of US President Donald Trump’s use of personal insults and ridicule against opponents.

It’s lonely at the top for India’s female politicians

India has an illustrious history of women occupying top leadership positions, long before many other democracies. But their rise has often come at a cost – that of loneliness in navigating a male-dominated political world.

In the book, Gandhi describes a 2012 conversation with Hillary Clinton on the countrys’ female leaders and their backgrounds. In her recollections of Indira, she shares that, despite wielding authority, Indira could feel besieged and profoundly alone, and this was particularly acute, Gandhi writes, in the months leading up to the declaration of the Emergency by her mother-in-law.

Gandhi also writes of her own isolation, including when she decided to take on the leadership of the Congress out of a sense of duty to her family’s political legacy.

Those in power are increasingly stonewalling the press

Among the many ways in which Gandhi charts the erosion of India’s democratic conventions in Belonging, one particularly telling shift is the changed relationship between the media and those in power. She recalls a time when journalists had opportunities to question prime ministers directly.

In 12 years in power, Modi has not addressed a single press conference on his own. He has sat in on one press briefing at the conclusion of an election campaign, and appeared alongside US presidents at joint press encounters after bilateral meetings, where they took questions from the press. Modi has consistently avoided facing journalists in an open forum, and his media interactions have largely been one-on-one interviews with sympathetic, pro-government news anchors or in one much publicised exchange with a Bollywood actor who asked him how he liked to eat mangoes.

The book’s publication has brought renewed attention to concerns about the space for free expression in contemporary India, and the extent to which apprehensions about repercussions may influence what can be said, written and published.

Penguin Random House India was to publish Gandhi’s memoir but withdrew from the project in August. Gandhi told the Guardian that it was “uneasy in general with the criticism of the government” and said it had dropped the book after she declined certain suggested edits. Penguin has disputed her account, saying that the suggestion it decided not to publish the book because of refused editorial changes “does not accurately reflect the circumstances”.

The book, released in the US by Alfred A Knopf, is being published in India by HarperCollins.

In June, Penguin had also pulled out of plans to distribute graphic journalist Joe Sacco’s The Once and Future Riot, about the 2013 Muzaffarnagar communal violence. The publisher cited an inaccurate map of India and unresolved citation issues. Sacco, however, blamed the decision on the “current political climate in India”.

She’s had quiet fallouts with the ‘thoroughly dishonest’

Western political memoirs, particularly by former heads of government or senior politicians, are often forthcoming in naming political adversaries, recounting private exchanges and offering candid assessments of colleagues. David Cameron’s 2019 memoir, For the Record, took aim at his former friend, Michael Gove, describing him as “mendacious” and a “foam-flecked Faragist”.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Gandhi, in contrast, is restrained. She writes warmly of the closeness between the Gandhi family and that of Bollywood superstar Amitabh Bachchan, whose parents she lived with when she first moved to India, but she makes no mention of the subsequent estrangement between the two families. In another instance, she does not name the political leader she describes as “thoroughly dishonest” and who she says she trusted in 1999, when Congress unsuccessfully attempted to form a coalition government.

Art | Art Mumbai runs from 12-15 November at the Mahalaxmi Racecourse. Among the highlights of this year’s edition is artist Baaraan Ijlal’s collaboration with the celebrated lyricist Javed Akhtar, turning his evocative Urdu couplets into paintings. The two will be in conversation with curator Ranjit Hoskote on Friday.

Government | The official bungalow of Andhra Pradesh’s chief minister, N Chandrababu Naidu, was renovated by a government-owned enterprise for Rs 6.5 crore (£518,000), according to the Indian Express (£). The renovation work on the Delhi property included was said to have had a major makeover.

Film | Bollywood’s ambitious rendering of Hindu epic Ramayana, starring Ranbir Kapoor and Sai Pallavi, was released on 5 November. Touted as the most expensive Indian film ever made, it earned praise from the Guardian’s Peter Bradshaw, who described it as “thoroughly engaging, rousing and spectacular”.

Group celebrations are walled off within exclusive clubs, families light their own fireworks almost competitively, and people send each other sweets through a delivery service. Samyuktha, Chennai

The government has approved the sale of a 60.7% stake in IDBI bank to Canadian Fairfax Holdings for Rs 52,878 crore (£4.22bn).

It’s hard to take one’s eyes off Muhammed Riswan when he’s at work. The 23-year-old football freestyler from Areekode in Kerala’s Malappuram district conjures tricks with the ball in a delicately choreographed dance. It glides between his feet, next it is rolling on to his back right before he leaps into the air to catch it with one hand.

#153. Disgraced former leader Sheikh Hasina plans return to Bangladesh from exile

Former prime minister faces death penalty upon return after being sentenced in absentia over deaths in 2024 uprising

Sheikh Hasina is “confident” she will return to Bangladesh in December, according to exiled members of her party, after the disgraced former prime minister applied for a new passport this week.

Hasina has been living in self-imposed exile since August 2024 when she was toppled from power by a mass uprising against her authoritarian rule. She crossed the border to India, where she has since been allowed to live freely in Delhi.

Last November, a tribunal in Bangladesh found Hasina guilty of crimes against humanity and sentenced her to death over her crackdown on a student uprising that left an estimated 1,400 people dead.

Her return to Bangladesh is likely to be disruptive for the country where Hasina’s party, Awami League, is still banned and thousands of its members are in jail. Should she be allowed to return, she is likely to be arrested immediately.

Hasina first raised the prospect of going back to Bangladesh in August. Speaking to the Guardian this week, several exiled Awami League leaders also living in India confirmed that Hasina was serious and “confident” about returning by next month. They confirmed that several other party figures had already returned in anticipation.

On Monday, a British lawyer acting on behalf of Hasina wrote to the Bangladesh high commission in Delhi requesting a new passport and national identity card to be issued “in a timely fashion”, in a letter seen by the Guardian.

“The reason our leader Sheikh Hasina has applied for a passport is that she genuinely wants to return,” said a party leader based in the Indian city of Kolkata, who spoke on condition of anonymity because he is not authorised to speak to the media on the party’s behalf.

Hasina has rejected the charges against her and claimed the tribunal that convicted her was illegitimate. “She is confident about her return, which is why she has applied for a new passport and a national identity card. If the authorities do not issue her a passport, she will apply for a travel pass to make her return to Bangladesh possible,” said the Awami League leader.

Since Hasina’s conviction, the Bangladesh government has put pressure on the Indian authorities to extradite the former prime minister. However, the Indian government, which was close to Hasina while she was in power, has ignored the demands, straining ties between the two countries.

This week, the Bangladesh government said that, as a “terrorist”, Hasina would only be allowed to return via an “acceptable prisoner exchange” under the India-Bangladesh extradition treaty.

Aminul Islam, the chief prosecutor of the tribunal that sentenced Hasina, said there was “no legal scope” for her to be issued a new passport as she was a “convicted fugitive”.

Islam emphasised that Hasina would be hanged if she returned, as the deadline for her to appeal against her conviction had passed. “Sheikh Hasina has no option but to face the execution of the verdict,” he said.

Despite the abuses and oppression that mired her 15 years in power and subsequent conviction for crimes against humanity, Hasina still commands significant popularity among the thousands of Awami League cadre still in Bangladesh, many of whom have been driven underground.

Analysts said they believed her decision to return was driven by an attempt to revive her support base and destabilise the newly elected government, led by Tarique Rahman. It came to power in February during Bangladesh’s first democratic election in decades.

The government has since struggled with inflation and protests that shut down garment factories, which the Awami League hopes to use to its advantage.

Another Awami League leader said all those in exile intended to follow Hasina back to Bangladesh, despite many of them facing serious criminal charges and having been given lengthy prison sentences in absentia.

“Some of us have been sentenced in absentia by kangaroo courts, and we know we will be jailed. But we are ready to face the consequences and return to our own people. We will fight this politically,” said the senior leader, who is based in India.

Some senior party figures have returned in recent months, including the former MP Mustaque Ahmed Ruhi. He was immediately arrested on his return in September and faces prosecution over an alleged murder during the 2024 uprising.

Exiled Awami League leaders have continued to meet in Kolkata, where many of them are living, and are routinely summoned to Delhi to meet Hasina.

Speaking over email this week, Hasina told the Associated Press: “If death is my destiny, I would rather die on the soil of my own country, among my own people.”

#147. Trump planning new military assault or tighter sanctions, say Iranian officials

Regime adviser says Tehran could be forced into pre-emptive strikes if stalemate continues

Senior Iranian officials believe the US is planning either further military strikes or an ever-tightening economic stranglehold that will push Tehran into retaliatory action.

The assessment comes from Mehdi Mohammadi, an adviser to Iran’s chief negotiator in talks with the US, Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf.

Mohammadi said on his Telegram channel that the Americans had proposed a timeframe of about two weeks after which Donald Trump would make a decision on military action. Mohammadi, who is regarded as a reliable indicator of Ghalibaf’s thinking, cast doubt on the US president’s claim that the military option was off the table. He claimed Washington was preparing for a limited strike and Iran was already preparing a response.

If the US sanctions continued, he said, a comprehensive pre-emptive war would be needed “to increase the cost of sanctions for the enemy”.

Mohammadi described the US response to Iran’s latest proposals on reopening the strait of Hormuz as “not only unsatisfactory but completely deceptive”. “They do not want to release the frozen funds,” he said. “They do not want to lift the sanctions and they do not want to stop the war in Lebanon.”

He also disclosed that Iran had rejected a request from the US vice-president, JD Vance, for a direct meeting with its negotiators before giving a response to Tehran’s conditions for lifting the siege on the strait.

For now, Trump has settled on a strategy of suffocating Iran through economic pressure and tightening the blockade on its oil exports while facilitating those of other Gulf states through Hormuz.

Mohammadi’s assessments suggest some senior Iranians fear Washington’s strategy might work by destabilising the country. The thinking goes that economic turmoil might spark street protests, potentially around the anniversary of nationwide demonstrations that began in late December last year.

On this basis, the Iranian leadership might conclude that the status quo – a “no peace, no war” impasse – was not sustainable and pre-emptive action was required.

Despite Iranian claims that their blockade of the strait is working, there is concern that Brent crude is at a relatively low $93 a barrel, while Saudi oil production is near prewar levels of 9.3m barrels a day. In an attempt to close any loopholes, Iran said it bombed a US radar post on the Omani island of Didamar three days ago.

In another development, Mohsen Rezaei, the secretary of Iran’s national security council, said he had told Russia that Iran was not prepared for even a particle of its highly enriched stockpile of uranium to leave the country, saying it might be needed for planned nuclear-powered submarines.

A third senior official, Mohammad Mokhber, an adviser to Mojtaba Khamenei, Iran’s supreme leader, warned Europe that any complicity in the US blockade would be “an act of war that will be etched in the nation’s historical memory”.

Tehran has been increasingly clear that US bases in Europe will be targeted if fighting resumes. Rezaei said: “If American planes take off from a distant military base and come to this country to bomb Iran, we will also target that base. We are announcing this now so they have no excuse tomorrow.”

Marco Rubio, the US secretary of state, gave the impression this week that the policy of economic strangulation was sufficient and described Iran’s demands as unrealistic. However, Trump is under pressure to resolve the crisis before the Democrats take charge of Congress on 3 January.

A Reuters Ipsos poll in the US shows 71% of Americans want Congress to pass legislation to end the war in Iran. The overwhelming message from the midterm elections was that the conflict was highly unpopular but the Democrats do not have a strategy to end it and open up the strait of Hormuz. This risks leaving the political initiative with the White House, and an increasingly hardline Iranian leadership.

#142. Oil slicks seen from space: the devastating impact of boat strikes on fragile Gulf ecosystems

Since the US war on Iran began in February, there have been hundreds of oil spills, posing a ‘generational’ threat to wildlife

The vast, dark slicks in the strait of Hormuz are visible from space. Throughout October, oil tankers were struck by missiles or sea mines in the waterway and spilled crude into the sea. The pollution has spread across the Gulf, contaminating coastlines and threatening to cause lasting damage to marine ecosystems that are home to dolphins, sea turtles and countless species of fish.

Eight months into the war between the US and Iran, one of the world’s busiest oil shipping routes has become a battlefield and the centre of a growing environmental disaster.

The naval blockade of the strait has made documenting the pollution from on the ground almost impossible. But analysis of satellite images, combined with the testimony of environmental experts and marine biologists, can help build up a picture.

The strait of Hormuz, the only maritime passage between the Gulf and the Arabian Sea, is home to a rich but fragile marine environment including coral reefs, mangrove forests and seagrass meadows that support a wealth of marine life.

It plays a vital role in the circulation of water and nutrients and serves as a migration route for marine mammals. Even in peacetime, its ecosystems have faced mounting pressure from shipping, oil extraction and coastal development.

Oil can coat birds’ feathers, making it difficult for them to float and find food. It can cover mangrove roots, preventing the trees from absorbing oxygen and potentially killing them.

Bernhard Riegl, a marine biologist at Nova Southeastern University in Florida, who has studied the Gulf for more than 30 years, warned that the war could cause lasting damage to its confined waters, already under severe pressure from the climate crisis.

“Since the Gulf is shallow, and summer is the least windy period of the year, atmospheric heat is rapidly translated into ocean heat. That heat kills corals, algae, fishes. There is no cold, deep water that could mix and buffer this heat,” Riegl said.

“Everybody knows that oil in water is not good. The potential for additional damage is significant. There is no such thing as a ‘clean war’. Not for the unlucky humans that are drawn into it, nor for the unlucky environment in which the conflict takes place.”

Since the war began on 28 February, Greenpeace has identified more than 500 instances of oil spills through satellite monitoring across the Gulf, the strait of Hormuz and the Arabian Sea. The findings include major spills linked to attacks on vessels, as well as others detected around the time of strikes but whose origins could not be confirmed.

“Adding together the daily mapped footprints [of the spills] gives a cumulative total of approximately 20,000 square kilometres – almost the size of Wales,” said Nina Noelle, a Greenpeace expert on environmental disaster relief.

More than 133 attacks on oil tankers have taken place since the beginning of the conflict, according to Greenpeace. October was the worst month, with more than 30 confirmed or reported attacks in the strait of Hormuz and surrounding waters, according to the Washington Institute for Near East Policy. The attacks have continued into November.

Among the most polluting of the October attacks was a strike on the supertanker Gem No 2 off the coast of the United Arab Emirates, which left a slick stretching more than 21 miles (35km). A satellite image dated 19 October shows a huge patch of black moving into the clear waters off the coast of Umm Al Quwain.

Greenpeace estimates that an attack on a tanker in early June resulted in a slick off the coast of Oman that covered more than 600 sq km (230 sq miles) at its peak. Two Iranian naval vessels hit in March continued leaking oil for months, said Wim Zwijnenburg, an environmental researcher with the Dutch peace organisation Pax.

An attack on the Liberian-flagged Minoan Pioneer on 3 August has also been linked to a major slick.

Oil pollution has reached several coastlines since the war began. In March, a slick from the Shahid Bagheri was detected near the Hara mangroves. In April, pollution threatened the protected Shidvar Island, home to coral reefs, seabirds and turtles. Oil was also reported washing ashore at Qeshm Island in August, near important fishing grounds and turtle nesting sites.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Satellite imagery also shows slicks near Oman’s Musandam national park and Iran’s Minab River delta, two areas rich in wildlife. Musandam is home to coral reefs and seabird breeding grounds, while the Minab delta has protected mudflats and forests of mangroves.

Environmental organisations are continuing to analyse satellite imagery but warn that the full extent of the damage cannot be established without investigations on the ground, which remain extremely difficult. Some of the researchers and environmentalists contacted for comment in Iran and Gulf states said they had been instructed by domestic authorities not to speak publicly about the environmental damage caused by the war.

Establishing responsibility is not straightforward, but Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) has been blamed for most of the attacks. US forces have also targeted tankers, disabling Iranian vessels attempting to cross a US naval blockade and striking others in response to Iranian missile attacks on US warships.

According to a Wall Street Journal report, the largest oil spill was caused by a strike carried out by the UAE’s air force on oil storage tanks on Lavan Island. The strikes resulted in crude oil flushing into the Gulf and affecting coastal areas, including the nearby nature reserves of Shidvar Island.

In some instances, countries bordering the strait have responded to spills. After the attack on the Gem No 2, for instance, the UAE closed beaches, launched a major cleanup and monitored its desalination plants. Authorities said on 30 October that the leak had been stopped and pollution was declining.

In Oman, divers and equipment were deployed to a damaged vessel and crews cleared oil from 7 miles of coastline near the Hallaniyat Islands.

But the wider environmental damage may prove far harder to contain. “Oil could remain trapped in sediments, shorelines and mangrove roots long after slicks disappear, damaging marine food chains and threatening coastal communities dependent on fishing,” Noelle said.

Contamination can affect organisms ranging from small invertebrates to fish, birds and turtles. “Mangroves and coral reefs can take decades to recover, particularly when oil persists in sediments,” she added. “Repeated spills, such as those in the strait of Hormuz, risk worsening the damage.”

Riegl said the aftermath of the Iran-Iraq war of the 1980s and the first Gulf war, when tankers, loading facilities and oilfields were targeted, provided evidence of the lasting impact of large-scale oil pollution on Gulf ecosystems.

A 2020 study traced widespread deposits of tar – the thick, sticky residue left as crude oil breaks down – on Qatari beaches to the Gulf war. A study in 2022 found sediment on Saudi Arabia’s Abu Ali Island still contaminated by oil from the 1983-85 Nowruz spills during the Iran-Iraq war, which collectively leaked 80m gallons of crude into the sea.

Riegl said: “Any organism that has to dig into beaches [affected by spills], like sea turtles when they lay eggs, will encounter the contaminated strata and adverse effects can be expected. Burrowing organisms like ghost crabs will continue to bring contaminated material to the surface.

“Clearly, the effects of large-scale oil pollution remain detectable for decades – so this is a generational problem. We could really lose something irreplaceable.”

#141. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#136. Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in Gaza kills surgeon and five others

Hospital director says 28 wounded in attack on one of few remaining medical facilities in the strip

An Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The Israeli military carried out the attack on the hospital, in Khan Younis, early on Thursday saying in a statement it had struck a Hamas commander and other fighters there, without providing evidence.

The strike destroyed part of the surgery department, and caused extensive damage to several floors. Services were suspended in four departments: radiology, laboratories, surgical operations and inpatient surgical wards, according to Dr Ahmed al-Fara, hospital director.

The ceiling of one of the wards had caved in, while debris-covered beds lay next to medical instruments twisted from the force of the blast. In other rooms, piles of rubble and shattered concrete cluttered the floor and a hole was left in the floor, where the bomb penetrated the building.

Dr Ayman Abu Ubaid, a surgical specialist at Nasser medical complex, who was wounded in the attack, said: “The bombing caused thick smoke to fill the rooms and fires to break out, amid the groans of wounded and people’s screams. There were many casualties and blood everywhere … Most of us sustained injuries to our limbs, as well as shrapnel wounds in different parts of our bodies.”

Ubaid said the strike came at about 12.30am, without warning. “The patients in the department were all surgical patients waiting to undergo operations. The bombing happened at a time when they should have been asleep,” he said.

Among the dead was Rizk Abu Shaira, 52, a general surgeon who had featured in American Doctor, a documentary about doctors operating in Gaza.

“Dr Rizk was in the American Doctor documentary operating and serving his people. He lost his life doing that, he lost his life because he was doing that,” Dr Thaer Ahmad, who also featured in the documentary, posted on X.

Abu Shaira was killed alongside one of his patients, on whom he had operated.

Nasser hospital is one of the last functioning healthcare facilities in Gaza, after Israeli bombing made most of the strip’s facilities unusable. Last year Israel also struck the hospital, killing five journalists in a double-tap strike on a location often used by reporters for filming. In all, the hospital has been attacked seven times since the war on Gaza began.

The Israeli military has accused Hamas of using hospitals as command centres and to store weapons, an accusation the militant movement rejects. More than 1,700 healthcare workers have been killed in Gaza, while Doctors Without Borders (MSF) says Israel has detained hundreds of medics since 7 October 2023, with at least 95 still in custody.

About 90% of Gaza’s health infrastructure has been destroyed or damage by Israeli bombing, systematic destruction UN experts called “medicide”.

Attacking healthcare facilities and staff is illegal under international humanitarian law, though these facilities can lose their protected status if they are used for military purposes.

There is an increased need for healthcare during the winter months in Gaza, where rains have displaced people from their tents and increased communicable diseases in crowded encampments.

Already, Nasser hospital is lacking about 80% of its essential medicines and medical supplies, said al-Fara, its director.

One of those killed in Thursday’s strike was Seif Ghazi Abu Hatab, 29, who was admitted to Nasser hospital on Monday after a piece of debris fell on his back while he was helping clear rubble from a friend’s home. He was scheduled for surgery at 10am on Thursday, and was meant to receive further treatment abroad.

“Seif was a young man in the prime of his life. He wanted to live, rebuild his home and start a normal life, free from problems and suffering … Seif was killed after already losing two of his brothers and his father. His mother has now lost three of her sons,” said his cousin Tareq Abu Hatab, 37.

Israel launched the war in Gaza after the 7 October 2023 Hamas-led attack that killed about 1,200 people and took 251 hostage. At least 74,645 people have since been killed in Gaza and Israel has carried out a genocide in the strip, according to the UN independent international commission of inquiry on the occupied Palestinian territory.

Israel continues to carry out airstrikes in Gaza, killing more than 1,600 people since the October 2025 “ceasefire”, according to the Gaza ministry of health.

#134. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#133. Two men found guilty over Iranian attempts to ‘eliminate’ UK-based journalists

Mostafa Sepahvand and Farhad Javadi Manesh carried out surveillance in an effort to inflict violence on targets

Two men of Iranian origin who were hired through social media as part of a plan to “eliminate” journalists in the UK have been found guilty of engaging in conduct likely to assist Tehran’s intelligence services.

Mostafa Sepahvand, 41, and Farhad Javadi Manesh, 46, carried out surveillance and reconnaissance between August 2024 and March last year in an attempt to inflict violence on “subjects” in the UK.

These subjects included two journalists for the London-based Persian broadcaster Iran International, Mojtaba Pourmohsen and Kambiz Hosseini, and a founder member of UK Lawyers for Israel, Jonathan Turner.

The defendants visited addresses linked to their targets, took photos and videos and used platforms such as ChatGPT to research them online. They appear to be part of a growing new tactic used by hostile states in which “proxies” are hired to work for foreign security forces, with often little idea who they are targeting or why.

Last month the Norwegian teenager Johannes Natland, 19, was jailed for life after he agreed to carry out a hit in Huddersfield for the Foxtrot Network, a Swedish organised crime group backed by the Iranian regime.

The Iranian men came to the UK illegally between 2016 and 2022 – Sepahvand in the back of a lorry and Manesh in a small boat.

Sepahvand used the AI platform ChatGPT to find the home address of Pourmohsen, who worked for the London-based Persian language broadcaster Iran International. He hoped to be paid £150,000 to carry out a violent attack and help to “finish off” Pourmohsen.

Sepahvand chatted on WhatsApp to his handler, an Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps commander called Amin, as he spied on Pourmohsen in December 2024, Woolwich crown court heard.

Amin wrote: “These are new pictures of the guy/bloke.”

Referring to a picture, Amin said: “And that dot is their office?”

Sepahvand said: “Love you brother, I’ll go there tomorrow.”

The pair also discussed surveillance of the Iranian satirist Kambiz Hosseini, who eventually had to leave home because of the threats made against him.

Bill Emlyn Jones KC, prosecuting, said Iran and its rulers did not and could tolerate dissent. “One organisation critical to the regime, and therefore very much in its sights, is Iran International, a news organisation which has come under particular attack because of its explicit support for opposition groups and its editorial stance, which is anti-Islamic Republic of Iran.

“Eliminating hostile, critical or dissident actors overseas is a staple policy of the Islamic republic. Together, the defendants were engaged in what is very obviously a form of spying,” Jones said.

Hosseini, 51, had to leave home and stop work as a standup comedian as a result of the threats he received. He was sent a message with a picture of his car saying the next time he started the engine it would blow up. Hosseini’s family in Iran also received threats, and his family had to leave the country.

The court heard Sepahvand had been convicted of theft in Iran and was drawn into the plan to “eliminate” dissidents in the hope of reducing his criminal record.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He told his Iranian handlers he was prepared to escalate from surveillance to violence, the court heard.

He hoped to receive a final payment of £150,000 for “finishing the job”, a reference to killing one of the journalists, the court was told. “If the person [the target] is inside, they pay the £150,000, and I’ll finish him off,” said Sepahvand in a voice note.

Sepahvand claimed his family was being threatened and that he never intended to go through with the hit. He also denied plotting to kill Turner, of UK Lawyers for Israel.

Manesh claimed he took a picture of the Iran International site because it was “a beautiful building” and did not know they were working with the Iranian intelligence services.

Earlier this year, Roman Lavrynovych, 22, and Stanislav Carpiuc, 27, were jailed at the Old Bailey for seven and two years respectively for arson attacks on property connected to Keir Starmer. An anonymous Russian-speaking figure named El Money had recruited Lavrynovych and Carpiuc on Telegram.

Last year, six Britons were jailed for their parts in a Russian-ordered arson attack on a London warehouse providing aid to Ukraine. The ringleader, Dylan Earl, was recruited by the Wagner group, a mercenary organisation that acts on behalf of the Russian state.

Sepahvand was convicted by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct. Manesh was convicted of assisting a foreign intelligence service, but cleared of preparatory conduct.

Shapoor Noori, 57, of Ealing, was cleared by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct.

Mrs Justice Cheema-Grubb remanded Sepahvand and Manesh ahead of sentencing at the Old Bailey on 17 December.

#128. NAZA: how and where to watch the Guardian’s acclaimed Gaza documentary

Film features testimony from 24 Israeli officials involved in surveillance and remote killing of Palestinian civilians

NAZA will be released in full on the Guardian’s website for free in early December. Sign up here to be notified of the exact streaming date and to be sent a link to the film when it is live.

NAZA has been shown by cinemas in 47 countries and seen by more than 1.5 million people since its release last month, making it one of the most-watched documentary films with a theatrical release in 2026. The film has been offered to a number of distributors and cinemas in Israel but has not been screened there yet.

NAZA is a documentary featuring testimony from 24 Israeli military intelligence officers and soldiers involved in the surveillance and remote killing of Palestinian civilians during the war in Gaza. The film’s title comes from an Israeli military acronym used euphemistically to refer to civilians expected to be killed in a military attack.

The interviewees detail the methods used by the Israeli military in Gaza, including AI-based systems built to identify large numbers of potential Hamas operatives, who were then bombed in their homes at night, in the knowledge that the attacks would also kill their families.

Interviews were filmed at night on rooftops in Tel Aviv, on the condition of anonymity, because of the risks involved in speaking publicly. The appearance and voices of participants in the film have been digitally altered to disguise their identities.

The film was directed by the Oscar-winning Israeli directors Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor and produced by the Guardian. It builds on revelations first published by the Israeli-Palestinian magazine +972, the Hebrew-language news outlet Local Call and the Guardian newspaper between 2023 and 2025.

Abraham and Szor were two of the four co-directors – with the Palestinians Basel Adra and Hamdan Ballal – of No Other Land, which documents the destruction of Masafer Yatta, a village in the occupied West Bank targeted by Israeli forces. It won the Oscar for best documentary in 2025.

NAZA was co-produced by James Wilson and executive-produced by Jonathan Glazer, the team behind the Oscar-winning film The Zone of Interest.

NAZA has received widespread critical acclaim and is selling out in cinemas across the world. The New York Times says it is “tough to watch and impossible to ignore”, while New York Magazine describes it as “one of the most important films of our time”. In a five-star review, the Financial Times says that “journalism and film-making brilliantly entwine”. British Vogue calls NAZA a “brilliant and harrowing film”.

The film won the special jury prize at the Venice film festival in September, where its premiere had a 25-minute ovation – a festival record. In November, it won the best political documentary from the Critics Choice Association.

The film, its directors and their confidential sources have faced fierce attacks from the Israeli government and military. The prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, has ordered the attorney general and the head of the Shin Bet security service to reveal the identities of sources interviewed and prosecute them for causing “harm to Israel’s diplomatic and security interests”.

Netanyahu also threatened to strip Abraham and Szor of their citizenship, accusing them of treason. The Israeli military’s chief of staff, Eyal Zamir, said the military would “not allow soldiers to be made targets through lies and blood libels” and would use “every tool at our disposal” against the film.

Far-right groups have vowed to hound the film-makers on their return to Israel and picketed Szor’s family home. Some of the messages included explicit calls for violence, with one protester demanding the “death penalty for traitors”.

The Israeli military claimed there were “substantial errors” in the film and that it had relied on sources who had only a partial picture of the target approval process. It said that while AI was used to help identify targets in Gaza, final decisions on strikes, and the risk of civilian casualties, were signed off by officers.

In response, the team behind the film have revealed they spoke to more than 100 sources with knowledge of the military systems used in the war.

The Israeli government is promoting an online film project, called NAZA: The Real Story, which mimics the style of the genuine NAZA film and is hosted on a website called nazadocumentary.com. It is not the real NAZA documentary and has been misleading some viewers.

Initially, the rival film’s website falsely presented itself as a promotional page for NAZA itself, naming Abraham and Szor as the directors, and listing film festivals where the documentary was to be screened. The site now appears as a platform for the rival film, which has been promoted by Israeli embassies.

Ariel Averbuch, an Israeli attorney and army reservist who co-created NAZA: The Real Story, describes it as an “offensive move” to shift the world’s focus from allegations about the Israeli military’s killing of civilians.

Szor and Abraham have addressed the backlash to their documentary. “Our country is doing so much to hide these systems,” Szor said.

NAZA is in cinemas worldwide and will screen for free on the Guardian’s website from early December. Sign up here to be the first to watch it

#125. 'My darling girl, you are everything to me' - auctioned letters reveal WWI love story

Gunner Willie Webber sent his new wife Gladys more than 100 letters from the Western Front over 10 months

"My darling girl, you are everything to me..."

Those words were written by Gunner Willie Webber to his wife Gladys from the battlefields of World War One.

The newly-weds, who were married at St Edward's Church in Knighton, Powys, in December 1917, had very little time together as Willie - a gunner with the Royal Garrison Artillery's 297th Siege Battery - was serving on the Western Front. He was killed just weeks before the Armistice in 1918.

More than a century later, Willie's letters have inspired an extraordinary act of remembrance by the community - thanks to the efforts of two strangers who refused to let the couple's love story be forgotten.

The inscription on the memorial bench at St Edward's Church in Knighton

The bundle of 100 letters, dated from December 1917 to October 1918, chart a love story unfolding against the backdrop of war.

After briefly returning home injured and marrying Gladys in late 1917, Willie went back to the front line in France.

He sent his new wife pressed flowers and affectionate messages, signing off: "Your own true hubby & loving sweetheart. Willie."

Willie wrote regularly and his letters are filled with hopes and dreams for the future.

But the reunion he longed for never came.

Just four weeks before the Armistice, in October 1918, the young soldier was killed in an accident when his gun backfired. He was 25 years old.

The couple had no children, no direct descendants and, as time passed, nobody left to tell their story.

Their story could so easily have ended there. Instead, those letters triggered a remarkable act of remembrance more than a century later.

A drawing of Willie was found among the letters, captioned "Gazing Homewards"

In 2025, the collection of love letters was put up for sale at an online auction.

Among those following the auction was Andy Garner, a mechanic from Norwich, who collects, transcribes and self-publishes wartime letters - ensuring the people in them are not lost to history.

When he came across Willie and Gladys' correspondence, he was determined to save it.

"Those letters just kept drawing me back," he said. "I'm adamant Willie was going, 'Come on Andy, you can do this'!'"

The auction price kept climbing, but - in the final seconds - Andy placed a winning bid of Â£351.51.

Later, reading through the complete collection, he found himself deeply moved by the couple's devotion and Willie's steadfast belief he would make it home.

"No matter what he's going through, their love shines through," said Andy.

"It's giving them both the hope to survive."

Though he could barely afford the money he had spent, Andy was determined to publish the transcripts.

Keith Budgen, the man who had sold the letters, decided he couldn't keep the auction money.

"I woke up the following morning and immediately contacted Andy," said Keith.

"I said, 'You can have the money back. It's the right thing to do.'

"I had been given the letters, and I just knew that Andy had to have them."

The pair bonded over the letters and came up with the idea to commemorate Willie, the fallen soldier, in Knighton - the place where he and Gladys had married.

What followed was a remarkable community effort.

Within just two-and-a-half weeks, supporters raised Â£1,600 for a memorial bench at St Edward's Church.

The church immediately embraced the project and a remembrance service was organised. Bell-ringers created a special tribute, a bugler volunteered to play, and members of the Royal British Legion lent their support.

"The fact that he wasn't commemorated on any cenotaph, either in the town where he was born or in Knighton, made it a fabulous idea," said Ian Ferguson, of the Royal British Legion.

Reverend Rachael Storer, of St Edward's Church, paid tribute to Andy's determination to share the couple's story.

"It all started with those letters that he bought at auction," she said.

âUltimately, this is a story about love."

The community gathered at the church where Willie and Gladys married to commemorate their story

Lynn Scales, who wrote a special standard for the bell-ringers in memory of the couple said: "Sometimes we forget the sacrifice that some people have made for the defence of this country.

"Gunner Willie sounds like he was doomed to be forgotten - but not any more."

Andy's wife, Lex, wrote to the BBC because she wanted others to know about her husband's work.

"Once he spreads the word, other people come on board and they all want to help because they care," she said.

"It seems to have this really beautiful ripple effect.

"Willie and Gladys were just regular everyday people, and for someone to actually take that time to remember them, I think it's beautiful."

More than a century after Willie wrote those letters, he and Gladys have found a new community. Not through blood or family ties, but through an act of remembrance initiated by two strangers, who refused to let their story be forgotten.

#123. ‘The world we know is over’ without urgent climate action, leaders tell Cop31

Marshall Islands president warns of worsening floods as UN chief calls for a windfall tax on oil and gas firms

Failure to provide an overwhelming response to the climate crisis means “the world we know is over”, the president of the embattled Marshall Islands has warned, as dozens of leaders gathered in Turkey for the Cop31 climate summit.

Hilda Heine, one of a handful of Pacific Island leaders to make an impassioned speech at Cop31 on Wednesday, told other heads of state that overshooting the 1.5C Paris temperature target would mean waves eating away their shores and floods that destroy towns, schools and hospitals.

She described seeing bright, lush coral beneath the water of her home island in July that had been bleached white just months later.

“It’s time to wake up,” said Heine. “Our response to the threat of overshoot must be overwhelming, or the world we know is over.”

Mia Mottley, the prime minister of Barbados, also called out governments who had “not tried hard enough” to curb emissions since the Paris agreement.

“The UN tells us that, even if every pledge is delivered, we are heading for 2.6 degrees,” she said. “On current policies, 2.8 degrees is where we will end up.

“Let me be plain: overshoot is not a new target. It is a debt, a debt of heat, borrowed against our children’s future. And it must be repaid.”

The opening of the summit of international leaders, on day three of Cop31, saw impassioned speeches interspersed with vigorous displays of Turkish music and dancing.

António Guterres, the UN secretary general, blamed a deceitful fossil fuel industry and countries that “keep expanding fossil fuel production while claiming to be committed to 1.5C”. His speech – a salvo aimed at boosting the talks – was one of the last Guterres, a vocal climate advocate, will deliver as secretary general before he stands down next month.

“We need an extraordinary acceleration,” Guterres said. “Our planet is heating up. The clock is winding down. It’s make or bake time.

“Fossil fuels must go the way of the dinosaurs,” he added. “And that won’t happen on its own. The dinosaurs did not have a lobby – fossil fuels do.”

Guterres said oil and gas companies had distorted science and delayed action on the climate crisis, and called for a windfall tax on their bumper profits they have made from the Iran war. “It’s time to kick the habit and end the fossil-fuel addiction,” he said, to applause. “It’s time for governments to tax these windfall profits and use them to help vulnerable countries recover. Polluters must pay.”

Unusually, Turkey is co-hosting Cop with Australia, with the leaders of both countries, Recep Tayyip Erdoğan and Anthony Albanese respectively, each praising the partnership.

Erdoğan said he wants Cop31 to be remembered as a turning point in the climate crisis and called on countries to act, rather than just make promises. “Every minute we fail to meet the targets in the Paris agreement, the risks and costs we face continue to grow,” the Turkish president said. However, on Monday, away from the conference hall, ​​Erdoğan celebrated the discovery of two new oilfields with an estimated in-situ potential of 135m barrels.

In her speech before world leaders, Heine said every new oil well drilled and every piece of coal extracted was a threat to her country’s future. Without naming any individual oil or gas producer, she added: “The discovery of new fossil fuel resources is not something to celebrate but something to fear.”

Albanese said the conference was a “historic moment” for Australia. The country is one of the world’s biggest exporters of coal and liquified gas. Albanese said governments had a “solemn responsibility” to act on the climate crisis, but he was criticised for failing to mention fossil fuels in his speech.

The gathering of leaders was relatively threadbare. While 91 national leaders attended, according to the UN, major figures such as Donald Trump of the US and Xi Jinping of China were absent. Perhaps tellingly, some other leaders who declined to attend, including the French president, Emmanuel Macron, and the German chancellor, Friedrich Merz, are instead heading to Ireland for a conference on AI.

Almost every country at Cop31 is lagging in cutting emissions, with insufficient finance for the nations most vulnerable to extreme heat, floods, fires and other climate-fuelled calamities.

Although the world is barrelling past the 1.5C limit and is entering the teeth of one of the strongest El Niño events in history, there is little sign that the talks will feature any further progress on phasing out planet-heating fossil fuels.

“I’m getting a feeling of panic from around here, from the science community,” said Bill Hare, a climate scientist and chief executive of Climate Analytics, who described fraught conversations with rainforest and cryosphere scientists. “The mood is gradually darkening among scientists. In fact, people talk about feeling as though things have gone backwards.”

Small island nations face an existential threat from rising sea levels and are some of the countries most exposed to catastrophe when global heating shoots past 1.5C. The UN Environment Programme has found that such overshoot is now inevitable, and warned that its effects on icy regions will be so calamitous it should be kept as brief and low as possible.

Other world leaders stressed the urgency of this moment of multiple crises and the need for multilateralism to finally deliver. Gordana Siljanovska-Davkova, the president of North Macedonia, said: “We are the first generation to fully understand the consequences of our choices – and perhaps the last with time to change course. Future generations will live with the consequences of our decisions.”

Nataša Pirc Musar, the president of Slovenia, said: “If we cannot agree that human dignity and respect for nature must be at the heart of our decisions then, frankly, what is left to unite us?”
