#121. New Indian mega library rocked by scandal over 'AI-generated books'

The newly opened Periyar Arivulagam library is at the centre of the allegations

An Indian court has ordered an investigation into allegations that a public library in the southern state of Tamil Nadu bought hundreds of books suspected of being generated using AI.

The controversy began after Tamil novelist Perumal Murugan raised concerns about books containing false information and questionable authorship at a newly opened library in Coimbatore city.

The Madras High Court has ordered the state's anti-corruption agency to investigate the purchases and freeze bank accounts linked to a publisher accused of supplying the books.

The state government has blamed the previous Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam (DMK) party-led administration for buying the books. The DMK denies allegations.

Tamil Nadu's School Education Minister A Rajmohan has described the allegations as potentially "India's first AI scam" and said the investigation would cover purchases at other public libraries across the state.

The case has raised wider questions about the growing use of AI in publishing, where tools can generate entire books quickly and cheaply, but also produce false information and invented references.

It has also highlighted the challenges facing libraries and publishers in checking the authenticity of books as AI-generated content becomes more widespread.

The controversy centres on the Periyar Arivulagam library, built at a cost of about 3bn rupees ($34m; Â£26m). Named after social reformer Periyar EV Ramasamy, it was inaugurated on 16 October by Tamil Nadu Chief Minister C Joseph Vijay.

Murugan, whose novels Pyre and One Part Woman have received international recognition, raised concerns after visiting the library last month.

Writing in an online magazine, he alleged that nearly 1,800 books in its Tamil section had been supplied by a little-known publisher called Agal Vilakku Pathippagam.

He said many of the books carried unfamiliar authors' names, unusually high prices and questionable International Standard Book Numbers (ISBNs).

One book cited in the controversy, Naladiyar Vaazhvin Vazhikatti (A Guide to Life through Naladiyar), was reportedly priced at 1,445 rupees for 300 pages.

It describes Naladiyar as a Tamil poet and philosopher, although Naladiyar is a classical Tamil work consisting of 400 four-line verses on morality and ethics.

Another book reportedly attributes the ancient Tamil poem Madurai Kanchi to an incorrect author. It remains unclear whether AI was used to produce any of the disputed books.

The library was inaugurated by Chief Minister C Joseph Vijay

Questions have also been raised about a book found at another public library in Madurai which names renowned economist Jean DrÃ¨ze as its author.

The book, Democracy and People's Rights in the Contemporary World, names renowned economist Jean DrÃ¨ze as its author and was published by Phoenix Publishing House.

DrÃ¨ze did not respond to BBC Tamil's request for comment but told The Hindu newspaper earlier this week that he had not written the book, raising concerns that his name may have been used without his permission.

BBC Tamil visited a Chennai address listed by Agal Vilakku and another publisher named in the controversy, Phoenix Publishing House, but found no signs identifying either business.

A woman at the address confirmed that Agal Vilakku operated there. Its proprietor, Mohan, declined to comment, saying the matter was before the courts.

The controversy has prompted writers, academics and publishers to question how the books were selected for public libraries.

More than 160 writers, researchers, artists and publishers have signed a joint statement calling for an independent inquiry, the release of procurement records and an audit of library collections across Tamil Nadu.

They have also called for the recovery of public money if the investigation finds that funds were misused.

The group questioned how the disputed books could have passed through a selection process involving expert committees, price negotiations and several levels of review.

The state government has ordered an internal audit of public library collections.

Rajmohan said 523 books had already been removed from the historic Connemara Public Library in Chennai city after preliminary checks raised concerns about their authenticity and quality.

Disputed books have also been removed from Periyar Arivulagam, while anti-corruption officials examine procurement records and inspect other libraries, he added.

The controversy also sparked a political row in Tamil Nadu. Rajmohan accused the previous DMK government of ordering the disputed books and said the state would seek to recover public money if the investigation found wrongdoing.

The DMK has rejected the allegations as politically motivated and has called for an investigation into the purchases.

#120. ‘Like priests who don’t believe in God’: Italy’s anti-vax medics eye return to frontline duty

Progress of bill to reinstate struck-off doctors hinged on support from far right figure Roberto Vannacci, whose rise is shaping politics before 2027 elections

Antonio Cremasco contracted Covid at a dinner party on 6 March 2020. Four days later, as Italy became the first European country to go into nationwide lockdown, he was fighting for his life. “I spent 20 days in intensive care, followed by another 20 days in a hospital ward,” said Cremasco, from Bassano del Grappa in the northern Italian Veneto region.

After leaving hospital, he needed oxygen therapy for another six months. Cremasco’s ordeal was such that when Covid vaccines became available in Italy in early 2021, he had no qualms about being vaccinated, and he believes the decision saved his life once again.

“All those who had been in intensive care with me died,” he said. “Without the vaccine, if I had caught Covid again, I had a high risk of dying too.”

But Cremasco, who is now 76 and recently received his annual Covid and winter flu jab, has struggled to convince his vaccine-sceptic son, a father of two, of the drugs’ safety. He said he and his wife were wary of having their potentially unvaccinated grandchildren in their home whenever the children were ill, fearing for their own health. “They always seem to be sick,” Cremasco said.

His family rift mirrors a wider divide over vaccination in Italy, which has been brought back to the political spotlight by a controversial bill approved in October in the lower house of parliament.

The measure allows healthcare workers who were struck off their professional registers during the pandemic for serious violations connected to their anti-vaccination beliefs – for example, promoting treatments that lacked a scientific basis – to apply to be reinstated.

The bill is being championed by the prime minister Giorgia Meloni’s Brothers of Italy party and is strongly backed by its far-right coalition partner, the League. But with allies in Forza Italia split, its approval in the lower house hinged on support from National Future (FN), a rival far-right force led by the former army general Roberto Vannacci, whose emergence is shaping the political landscape before general elections next year. Vannacci has voiced opposition to vaccine mandates, and his rhetoric frequently targets the vaccine-sceptic audience.

The measure still needs to clear the senate, with a vote scheduled in January. But its progress has alarmed medical experts, less because of the number of healthcare workers involved – it is believed to be very small – and more because of the risk of further eroding trust in vaccines.

Nearly one in two Italian adults are hesitant about vaccination, according to a report by the department of public health and paediatric sciences at the University of Turin published in the Lancet Regional Health Europe in February. The report, which sampled 50,000 adults across Italy, found the ambivalence was driven more by misinformation and lack of institutional trust than by fears over vaccine safety.

“Political communication and the lack of trust among Italians in institutions carry a significant weight,” said Roberta Siliquini, the head of the school of hygiene and preventive medicine at the University of Turin and the report’s chief researcher. “There really is a disinformation campaign in this sense – and those who want to disinform are very good at communicating.”

The implications are being profoundly felt: this year there has been a resurgence in measles, with 472 infections reported during the first five months, compared with 322 in the same period last year, according to health ministry figures. More than half of the cases were reported in young adults. Take-up of the flu vaccine among people aged 65 and over so far this season has dipped to 49.81%, the first time it has fallen below 50% since the winter of 2014-15.

In August, a four-year-old child in Palermo, Sicily, died of diphtheria. Her parents reportedly told doctors they had not vaccinated her because her cousin was autistic and they were convinced vaccines were the reason. There is no evidence of any link between autism and vaccines.

Siquilini said the tragic case was a reminder of the responsibility that everyone, “but especially politicians”, had to deliver the correct message on vaccines. She warned that the rehabilitation of those who rejected scientific evidence could have a “devastating” impact on how people perceived vaccines.

The message has been repeatedly undermined. On 28 October, a day after the lower house vote, Claudio Borghi, a League politician, came under fire for hosting an anti-vaccine seminar in the senate.

Later in the day, in what was believed to have been an indirect rebuke, the Italian president, Sergio Mattarella used a speech during a high-profile cancer research gathering to condemn the spread of anti-vax messages while warning against “irrational beliefs” and “destructive fake news”.

Borghi has proposed revising a law introduced in 2017 by the then leftwing government that extended the number of obligatory childhood vaccinations from four to 10, adding, for example, one against pertussis, or whooping cough. He has defended his position, arguing he is not anti-vaccine but that mandatory vaccines “lead to resistance”.

As for the reinstatement of the medics struck off during the pandemic, he told the Guardian that the proposal’s aim was “to foster reconciliation after all these years”. He said: “In our view, nobody should lose their job because of a personal belief.”

Scepticism over vaccinations took root in Italy in the late 1990s, as in many other countries, mainly driven by now debunked claims of a connection between the combined measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccination and autism.

The arrival of social media accelerated the spread of vaccine misinformation, contributing to a drop in immunisation rates against preventable diseases. The 2017 move to make childhood vaccinations obligatory further emboldened anti-vaccine groups while creating fertile ground for rightwing populist parties, including the Five Star Movement (M5S), which continued to spread misinformation about vaccines after coming to power in 2018, and before the realities of the pandemic forced a shift in tone. Now in opposition, M5S is highly critical of the move to re-embrace anti-vax healthcare workers.

Lorenzo Mattia Signori, a nurse in Veneto, said politicians were “opportunists” and Meloni’s coalition was simply trying to draw votes from anti-vax voters as it eyed a second mandate.

Signori, who is also a regional secretary for Cittadinanzattiva, an association focused on patient advocacy, including pushing for campaigns to inform citizens about vaccine benefits, said: “Ensuring that people get vaccinated is the largest prevention system we have. If everyone gets sick instead, the Italian healthcare system would not hold.”

He hopes that Mattarella will use his power as president to stop the anti-vaccine medics measure from being signed into law. “We cannot have them in hospitals,” he said. “It would be like going to church and hearing a priest who says he doesn’t believe in God.”

#119. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#116. 'It can kill us': Kenyans fear impact of El Niño with floods predicted

Agnes Shikoli says she fears either drowning, or being crushed by her house

"It flooded up to here in March," Agnes Shikoli says, pointing to a spot halfway up the 6ft [1.8m] corrugated iron wall of her home in Kibera, one of the largest informal settlements in Kenya.

Her tiny house lies just a couple of metres from the Ngong river, which runs through this densely populated area in the capital, Nairobi.

The scars of floods in the long rainy season earlier this year can still be seen - including a broken TV in the corner which was immersed in water.

Tucked out of sight is a photo of her sister Beatrice Khalayi, 58, who died when intense rains caused widespread destruction in Kibera. She was among more than 60 people across the country who lost their lives.

"Someone called me to tell me that she had died of electrocution after her house flooded," Shikoli recalls. "When I see a picture of her, I feel uneasy, that's why I keep it far from me, so I don't see it."

With Kenya's short rainy season now starting, Shikoli tells the BBC she is afraid - especially following warnings that the El NiÃ±o weather phenomenon could bring a new round of flooding.

"I feel like it can kill us. These houses aren't made of stone," says the 62-year-old. "They can fall on us, or the water can drown us."

Kibera - and Kenya more widely - are on the front line of preparations for the potential impact of El NiÃ±o.

Forecasters have warned that this year could see one of the strongest on record, bringing extreme weather to many parts of the world.

In southern Kenya, that means the threat of rains even more intense than usual.

Outside Shikoli's house, groups of men and women wearing high-visibility vests are shovelling and raking garbage out of the riverbed and disposing it far from the water.

This programme called Climate Worx, under the office of President William Ruto, is meant to rehabilitate Nairobi's rivers. Such exercises have intensified since August when warnings increased about the possible scale of this year's El NiÃ±o.

Rubbish clogging Nairobi's drains and sewers was seen as a major factor in the deadly floods earlier this year

Similar work to unclog drainage systems is ongoing across Nairobi, a sprawling city built on hilly ground crossed by three major rivers.

But the workers have a huge task on their hands.

Blocked drains and inadequate sewage systems were widely blamed for the high number of deaths after the floods earlier in the year, as the excess rainfall had nowhere to escape.

The Nairobi County disaster management chief, Bramwell Simiyu tells the BBC that drainage systems have been "untouched for many years" and that removing the rubbish from them will save lives.

Floods disproportionately affect informal settlements in Nairobi, especially those near rivers, like Kibera and Mathare.

In most of these settlements, houses sit just a few metres from rivers. Some people, including Shikoli, live on the slopes of the river itself due to the constant expansion of the settlements. When flash floods occur, these houses are swept away, endangering the lives of their occupants.

Nairobi's drainage system has not been upgraded to deal with the rapid growth in the population and infrastructure over the decades

"The existing drainage system was developed 30 years ago to cater for a population of about 2.5 million residents but we're dealing with about seven million in the daytime and 5.5 million night-time," Simiyu says.

He adds that investment of up to $192m (Â£143m) is needed to upgrade the sewerage and drainage system entirely.

But there is little sign that either the cash-strapped national or city council will able to raise this kind of money.

The Nairobi River Basin Regeneration programme, a government programme aimed at restoring the city's rivers, found that settlements near rivers, and where there is weak enforcement of regulations, have increased flooding risks. It recommended a "buffer" to protect rivers and "humane relocation" of communities where necessary.

However, rising poverty has driven many people to live close to rivers.

"Our worst case scenario looking at low-income settlements is that up to about 100,000 people are at risk," Simiyu says. "Moderate projections is about 5,000 households or about 20,000 people may be at risk."

Anyone forced to leave their homes will be relocated to 167 designated holding grounds where a stockpile of relief food and supplies will be distributed, Simiyu says.

Nobody knows exactly when the rains will come - or how much rain there will be.

But Kenya's meteorological department warns that November is usually the peak rainfall month of the short rainy season, which normally lasts from October to December.

A regional forecast from the Intergovernmental Authority on Development (Igad) warned in September that there was "an 81% probability of a very strong El NiÃ±o during October-December 2026".

So far, the heavy rains predicted have not occurred but experts remain concerned.

In East Africa, this El NiÃ±o is being widely compared to a similar weather event in 1997-1998 which caused widespread flooding and destruction in Somalia and Kenya.

Weather forecaster Jemima Gacheru tells the BBC that is because temperature conditions in the Indian Ocean in the 1997 El NiÃ±o and those recorded now are similar. This interaction, she says, has been seen to result in very strong rainfall.

"For this season we have a very strong El NiÃ±o already in play in the Pacific and then we have a positive Indian Ocean dipole," Gacheru said. This is often called the "Indian NiÃ±o" because of its similarity to its Pacific equivalent and refers to the difference in sea-surface temperatures in opposite parts of the Indian Ocean.

"We expect to have some enhanced rainfall over several parts of the country."

The UN's migration body the International Organization for Migration (IOM) has warned that almost 900,000 people could be forced to leave their homes in Kenya this year, mostly because of the predicted floods.

Dozens of people died in floods in Kenya earlier this year

Kenya is not the only African country worried about the potential impact of El NiÃ±o.

In neighbouring Somalia, the country's disaster management agency has warned that 1.2 million people are at risk of displacement caused by extreme weather - including the risk of both floods and drought.

The 1998 floods killed 2,000 people in Somalia and forced a further 250,000 to flee their homes, according to the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO). Up to 80% of crops were destroyed in some parts of the country.

The United Nation's famine warning system IPC has warned of the increased risk of food insecurity in Somalia due to supply chain issues exacerbated by the impact of El NiÃ±o.

Elsewhere on the continent forecasters fear El NiÃ±o could bring extreme heat and prolonged periods without rain.

It could cause a crisis for farmers across parts of West Africa, including the semi-arid Sahel region south of the Sahara, as well as in southern parts of the continent, where the risk of crop failure is high.

There are fears El NiÃ±o's impact will worsen existing issues.

Communities like Kibera are already grappling with poor housing and rampant poverty, while parts of Somalia are in the middle of a devastating drought.

National governments in many countries are doing what they can to prepare but the extent of El NiÃ±o's impact is impossible to predict.

Back in Kibera, Shikoli and her family are preparing for the worst.

Council officials have told them they should leave their home on the riverbank until the rainy season has passed but Shikoli makes just $2 a day selling groundnuts and fruit from a small stall next to the river.

"I want to move," she says. "But I don't have the means.

"We just have to persevere and leave it to God."

#115. European survey reveals 900m galaxies – and possibly secrets of ‘dark universe’

Unprecedented view of cosmos provided by Euclid mission throws up many more cases of gravitational lensing

A mission to create the largest ever 3D map of the universe has revealed more than 900m galaxies in its first major data release together with about 15,000 potential cases of a phenomenon that could help unlock the secrets of the “dark universe”.

Launched in July 2023, the European Space Agency’s Euclid mission promised to create the largest cosmic map ever made, encompassing a third of the sky and more than 10bn years of cosmic history.

The goal is to shed light on two of the biggest mysteries in the universe: the nature of dark energy and dark matter. These are thought to make up about 70% and 25% of the universe respectively.

Prof Andy Taylor, of the University of Edinburgh, who leads the UK science ground segment for the mission, said while there were bigger surveys, none had the detail and the information content of Euclid. By contrast the Hubble telescope has produced images of incredible resolution, but does not cover as large an area of the sky.

“The technological leap that’s Euclid is that we can keep that high resolution, but now we can basically map huge volumes of the sky, huge volumes of the universe,” Taylor said.

Now, the first major Euclid data release has landed, offering researchers an unprecedented view of the cosmos. “It’s the biggest high-resolution image of the universe we’ve ever had,” Taylor said.

Covering about 14% of the total area of sky that will be mapped by Euclid, the newly released data captures over 900m galaxies as well more than 60m potential quasars: blazing objects at the centre of distant galaxies, powered by supermassive black holes.

It also includes a tranche of candidates for what was previously considered a rare phenomenon: strong gravitational lensing. This is a magnification effect that occurs when one galaxy, often embedded in a galaxy cluster, sits in front of another, distant galaxy.

As Taylor notes, Einstein predicted the gravitational influence of the foreground galaxy would distort space-time, bending the light from the background galaxy and creating the appearance of bright arcs or rings. Crucially, the effect is significantly boosted by the presence of dark matter, which increases the mass and hence gravitational influence of a galaxy.

Taylor said that by studying strong gravitational lensing, scientists may be able to rule out different theories on the nature of dark matter. That is because the lensing is influenced by how this mysterious substance is distributed in a galaxy – that itself depends on its properties.

Stephen Serjeant, a professor of astronomy at the Open University, agreed. “With strong gravitational lensing, you find out how the dark matter is clumping and that’s very diagnostic of the type of matter that it is, because different types of matter can clump in very different ways,” he said.

“So if you’re looking for where the next breakthrough will be in the properties of dark matter and what dark matter actually is, there’s a good chance – I can’t say it’s certain – but there’s a good chance it might come from strong gravitational lensing.”

Taylor noted that while experts previously knew of about 1,000 cases of strong gravitational lensing, the Euclid data contains about 15,000 probable cases, with an estimated 170,000 expected to be discovered by the end of the mission.

A further release of data was expected to land next summer, Taylor added, which will include results on both the distribution of galaxies as well as weak gravitational lensing – subtle distortions in distant objects by dark matter in the foreground.

“We can use that pattern of distortions to actually make a map of where all the dark matter is in the universe,” said Taylor, adding that scientists could then work out its statistical properties and compare the results with different theories. “Depending on the type of dark matter, the type of dark energy, we’re expecting to see slightly different patterns on the sky.”

And with Euclid generating ever-more data, Taylor noted there was plenty to explore.

“It’s opening up that high resolution of the whole sky, the whole universe. So it just means there are plenty of objects that we’ll never have seen before, plenty of research phenomena,” he said. “No human eyeball is able to look at all of the Euclid data any more. It’s now too big.”

#113. Can Nigeria's president regain voters' trust after painful first term?

President Tinubu faces re-election with the cost of living and economic hardship the major issues for many voters

Nigeria's President Bola Ahmed Tinubu has launched his re-election manifesto, promising to grow the economy, tackle insecurity and address housing shortages.

The plan targets 6% annual economic growth by 2031, 78,000 additional security personnel and 200,000 affordable homes.

Titled From Reform to Prosperity, the manifesto builds on Tinubu's first-term economic reforms, including the removal of the fuel subsidy, which has pushed up living costs.

Nigeria is due to hold a general election in January 2027, with Peter Obi and former Vice-President Atiku Abubakar among Tinubu's main challengers.

The manifesto, launched on 10 November by his party, the All Progressives Congress (APC), reflects the government's bid to win back the trust of Nigerians.

Elected in 2023, Tinubu undertook a series of economic reforms, including the removal of fuel subsidies, the harmonisation of official and parallel foreign exchange rates, and tax reforms.

"That manifesto was trying to get Nigeria back on its feet, given the condition of the country at that time, slipping into austerity," Joshua Osiyemi, who heads Tracka, a platform launched by the BudgIT Foundation, which is a non-profit organisation that aims to make government financial data easier to understand.

The reforms, although considered necessary by some economists, have been painful for many Nigerians.

"Fuel prices went up, and everything that Nigerians consume skyrocketed," Osiyemi told the BBC.

"Most Nigerian families are saying they cannot survive on 10,000 naira [$7.40; Â£5.50] a day," he said, referring to a survey conducted by his organisation.

Analysis by Nigeria's International Centre for Investigative Reporting (ICIR) found that the price of imported rice rose by 184% between May 2023, when Tinubu took office, and October 2025. Other staples, including beans, garri (cassava flour) and bread, also recorded substantial increases.

In a sign that the reforms might be starting to pay off, SBM Intelligence, a security risk consulting firm, found that the cost of preparing a pot of jollof rice fell by 11.5% between June and September 2026, the sharpest decline it had recorded in the 10 years it has been running its Jollof Index.

However, this has not translated into significant relief for most households.

"Nigerians spend more and more of their income on basics," said Ikenna Okonkwo, head of research at SBM Intelligence.

The reforms were praised by the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund (IMF) for their effectiveness.

Nigeria's headline inflation stood at 14% in September 2026, according to the National Bureau of Statistics, down from a high of 34% in 2024. The gap between the official and parallel exchange rates has also narrowed.

Tinubu has said the removal of fuel subsidies freed up more than one trillion ($740m; Â£550m) naira in government resources in just the first two months.

Nigeria is due to hold elections in January, with Peter Obi and former Vice-President Atiku Abubakar (pictured above) among Tinubu's main opposition challengers

Following widespread complaints about the rising cost of living, he recently said he would work to reduce the cost of fuel but denied that this was a return to the subsidy. For decades, many Nigerians have seen cheap fuel as one of the few perks of living in Africa's largest oil producer.

His administration subsequently announced a 125bn naira intervention package for small businesses, including grants for smaller enterprises and loans for manufacturers.

Atiku has criticised Tinubu's decision to remove the fuel subsidy.

"My position stands! I will restore subsidy to make life easier and affordable for Nigerians," Atiku wrote on X.

However, restoring the fuel subsidy could prove challenging given Nigeria's economic realities.

"The current budget for Nigeria in 2026 is about 68.32tn naira, with 15.8tn going towards debt servicing. So, we are not fiscally strong enough to say that we want to return to the subsidy," Osiyemi said.

While Tinubu's reforms are lauded for restoring some stability at the macroeconomic level, they have also created instability in households.

Two months away from elections, the cost of living and economic hardship are the major issues for many voters.

"These kinds of things can get punished in polls," said Okonkwo of SBM Intelligence.

To address this, Tinubu promised to lower the cost of living, grow the economy by 6% and halve youth unemployment if he wins re-election. Achieving these targets will require increased oil and agricultural production.

But much of Nigeria's agricultural production is concentrated in the north, where insecurity has made farming increasingly difficult.

Osiyemi says many farmers are reluctant to return to their fields.

"They're not sure whether when they go to their farms, they have the chances of also coming back home, to meet with their family."

To address insecurity, Tinubu proposes recruiting an additional 28,000 military personnel and 50,000 police officers.

"The security situation, especially in the north, is more complex than a matter of just recruiting more soldiers," Okonkwo said.

The kidnap gangs, jihadists and separatists wreaking havoc in Nigeria

'We fear for our souls' - Nigerian farmers need armed guards to protect them from jihadists

Why Nigeria's one-time political giant may face extinction

The threats include jihadists, kidnap gangs and conflict between herders and farmers in central Nigeria.

Insecurity is also a major priority for Obi, the Nigeria Democratic Congress (NDC) presidential candidate, who came third in the 2023 presidential election and attracted significant support from young Nigerians.

He unveiled his 2027 Governance Agenda on 10 November, under the campaign slogan "A New Nigeria is Possible", placing national unity at the top of his priorities, followed by security.

"Nobody will kidnap school children and disappear into the bush. If you kidnap children and go into the bush, you will not be alone there. We will come after you," he said.

Further, delivering on those ambitions will also depend on improving electricity supply, particularly for businesses and industries. Tinubu promises to increase available electricity generation towards 10,000MW.

"There is nothing in the manifesto about where that investment is going to come from because it will cost a lot of money," said Okonkwo.

Unreliable electricity supplies force many households and businesses to rely on generators, pushing up the cost of living

Another huge challenge facing Nigeria is the power sector, which has suffered from years of underinvestment.

The Nigerian Electricity Regulatory Commission says the country had installed generation capacity of 13,625MW at the end of 2024, but generated an average of just 4,223MW during the year.

Nigeria's power sector has failed to produce at capacity due to ageing infrastructure, inadequate maintenance, gas supply constraints and funding challenges.

The government has also privatised electricity distribution, but distribution companies have struggled to invest in infrastructure, including local transformers and metering systems, partly because of low profit margins.

"The government cannot increase tariffs to an extent where people will revolt, and that also keeps investors away because they don't look at the Nigerian power sector as somewhere that they can make money," said Okonkwo.

Unreliable electricity supplies force many households and businesses to rely on generators, pushing up the cost of living and doing business.

The manifesto also promises to build 200,000 affordable homes. This is a fraction of Nigeria's estimated housing deficit, which a technical committee set up by the housing ministry put at 14.9 million units in 2025.

For Nigerians to feel a meaningful improvement in their lives, the reforms will need to go beyond the promises outlined in the manifesto, analysts say.

"Some of the items in the manifesto seem bloated, but if they can be committed to it, I want to believe that quite a number of these items that they have here are very much achievable," Osiyemi said.

"The big thing, if the APC wins, which they have to do and is a tall order, is to regain the trust of Nigerians," Okonkwo said.

Nigeria's 2023 presidential election saw Tinubu declared winner with 8.79 million votes, ahead of Atiku and Obi. Tinubu's victory came amid growing economic hardship after eight years of Muhammadu Buhari's APC government.

"APC were able to win because opposition was divided between two very strong candidates with two regional bases. In the end, we still go down to basic questions like the region you are from and tribal sentiments," Okonkwo said.

As Nigeria heads towards the 2027 elections, Tinubu, Atiku and Obi are once again central to the contest, but the opposition faces a familiar challenge - building a united front and support beyond regional strongholds.

"It will play out like it did in 2023," Okonkwo said.

"The APC are in a stronger position being the incumbent party and the momentum that some of the opposition candidates had in 2023 has faded."

#110. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#109. Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Arm's boss Rene Haas (L), Nvidia's Colette Kress (C) and Chris Malachowsky (R) have been named as possible successors

Nvidia is now facing a scenario few would have considered until very recently - a future without Jensen Huang.

The chief executive and co-founder of the AI chip-making giant is at the centre of a major scandal over reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

On Thursday, Nvidia's board announced he had resigned effective immediately.

He will be replaced, for now, by Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress.

The tech giant is facing one of its biggest challenges, finding the right person to permanently lead the business that Huang helped create more than three decades ago and turned into one of the world's most valuable companies.

We take a look at which candidates have emerged as potential successors.

Colette Kress is chief financial officer at Nvidia

Nvidia's finance chief, Colette Kress, is taking on the chief executive role for an interim period while the company searches for a permanent replacement.

An industry veteran for more than 30 years, Kress previously held high positions at tech firms like Cisco and Microsoft.

Kress, who also serves as Nvidia's executive vice president, has seen the firm's transformation from making chips for video games in the early 1990s to becoming an AI semiconductor powerhouse.

She "fronts every earnings call" alongside Huang and is a "familiar and trusted voice for the market at a time when it badly needs one," said Josh Gilbert, an analyst at investment firm eToro.

But doing the job permanently could be a different story, said Gilbert, as the firm, known for its edge in technology, could want an engineer in the chair.

Chris Malachowsky is one of Nvidia's three co-founders

Another option is Chris Malachowsky, who founded Nvidia alongside Huang and Curtis Priem, and is the only executive that shares the same level of seniority.

Malachowsky has played a key part in developing the company's computing technology.

Ian Buck, who oversees Nvidia's high-performance computing business, has also emerged as a potential candidate if the company is keen to put an engineer in the top job.

Buck was among the early pioneers who helped popularise the use of graphic processors beyond gaming applications.

"He could protect Nvidia's technological edge, but his ability to manage the wider commercial relationships and geopolitical challenges would face scrutiny," said Charu Chanana, investment strategist at investment bank Saxo.

Today, Nvidia's leading processors are widely regarded as the gold standard for AI development.

Huang's children also work at Nvidia in executive positions but are relatively young and inexperienced.

Nvidia is likely to favour an internal appointment initially but could look externally for a more permanent replacement, said Jun Bei Liu, founder and lead portfolio manager at Ten Cap.

Several names of tech entrepreneurs outside of Nvidia have been floated by experts.

Liu said Apple's former chief executive, Tim Cook, could be a possible choice as someone familiar with managing global supply chains, though she questioned whether he would be willing to leave the iPhone maker, where he is the chair.

Apple's Tim Cook (R) with Jensen Huang (L) and his wife Lori Huang at the White House

Liu and Chanana also suggested Rene Haas, chief executive of Arm Holdings, the UK chipmaking giant.

Nvidia's processors are made with the help of chipmakers around the world, including Taiwan's TSMC and South Korea's SK Hynix and Samsung.

Haas is familiar with leading a publicly-listed company and would bring semiconductor experience, though Nvidia's business model is considerably different from Arm's, said Chanana.

"The challenge is finding someone who can combine Jensen's technical understanding, strategic vision and relationships across the global technology industry," Liu said.

Ten Cap's Liu had believed it was just a matter of time before Huang would step down as Nvidia's chief executive.

"The issue is no longer simply about personal conduct," she said. "It is about corporate governance, national security concerns and maintaining confidence in the leadership of one of the world's most strategically important companies."

Several major investment firms have downgraded their forecasts for the chip giant - a rare shift given investors' longstanding optimism about the company.

Stacy Rasgon, senior analyst in US semiconductors at Bernstein Research, wrote in a note to investors that "our gut tells us we probably haven't fully probed the depths of what might be found here".

Rasgon halved Bernstein's share price target for Nvidia and said: "We've had enough and prefer to watch from the sidelines."

Ray Wang, chief executive of Constellation Research, said the crisis isn't so much an issue for the wider AI industry but rather one for Nvidia itself which has to consider if the person it appoints as its chief executive has the same appeal as Huang.

"That's really what the issue is, because a lot of these partnerships and investments have been made because of the trust with with Jensen," he said.

#107. Poundland owner secures two extra weeks to ensure retail chain’s survival

US investment firm Gordon Brothers is ‘taking due care’ to consider if one of three bidders has enough funds

Poundland’s owner has said it is “taking due care” to consider if one of three bidders had enough funds to ensure its survival after the troubled budget retail chain secured two more weeks to find a deal.

The management of Poundland, which employs approximately 12,000 people and operates 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, won approval from the high court for an extension of legal protection from creditors on Thursday morning.

Poundland’s board said it had sought the extra time “with a view to identifying the best available prospects for shareholders, creditors and our people”.

It added: “Our stores remain open as usual during this period and we thank our colleagues, suppliers and customers for their incredible and continued support.”

The retail chain’s owner, the US investment firm Gordon Brothers, has been under pressure to accept a bid for the company that would save retail jobs and protect thousands of suppliers since Poundland filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators two weeks ago.

A spokesperson for Gordon Brothers said on Thursday: “We support the board’s decision to extend the legal moratorium over the company by a further 10 working days. This brings additional stability amid an ongoing sale process in the backdrop of increasing cost pressures and a difficult inflationary economic environment.

“With this decision, we are today able to confirm to Poundland employees that funds will be released to allow payroll to be made in full this month, while additional funds continue to be released to support Poundland’s ongoing trading.

“We are in discussions with three parties for a sale of the whole business, and we have also received several offers from established high street retailers for significant parts of the business.

“While our preference remains a sale of the whole company, we are taking due care to ensure that any successful offer can provide the funds required to acquire the business and see it through to sustainable cash generation. We, the board, and all involved are working to secure a credible plan that delivers certainty, preserves value and protects as many jobs as possible.”

Gordon Brothers confirmed it had provided £85m of financing, understood to be via an asset-backed loan that Poundland has only partially used over the past year.

That financing is in addition to a £30m shareholder loan largely taken on by Gordon Brothers when it bought the business for a nominal £1 last year.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Gordon Brothers fought back after MPs on Wednesday accused the Poundland owner of extracting millions of pounds from the retailer when it was on a “knife-edge”.

Documents provided to MPs showed the finance firm had taken close to £20m in interest payments and various fees, including those relating to store closures since acquiring Poundland.

Gordon Brothers said it had provided finance for Poundland “when no one else was willing to finance the company – and has contributed substantial efforts to support a turnaround.

“Had we not, the company would have collapsed into insolvency in August 2025. Our efforts have continuously focused on the company’s value and its people, not prioritising one over the other.”
